INSTRUCTION: ADD
FORMAT: add rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: In the RV32I ISA, the ADD instruction MUST add its two source registers as 32-bit integers and write the 32-bit sum to the destination register.
RULE 2: In the RV64I ISA, the ADD instruction MUST add its two source registers as 64-bit integers and write the 64-bit sum to the destination register.
RULE 3: In the RV64I ISA, 32-bit integer addition is performed by the ADDW instruction, and the ADD instruction MUST NOT perform a 32-bit addition.
RULE 4: ADD computes the XLEN-bit integer sum of the values held in source registers rs1 and rs2 and produces an XLEN-bit result.
RULE 5: ADD writes the computed result into the destination register rd.
RULE 6: ADD never raises any arithmetic exception, including overflow, underflow, or other arithmetic traps.
RULE 7: Because ADD does not raise an exception on overflow, software MUST explicitly detect overflow, for example by using a branch instruction that compares rd with rs1 for unsigned addition or with rs1 for signed addition.
RULE 8: In RV64I, a 32-bit signed addition can be verified by comparing the result of ADD with the result of ADDW on the same operand values.
RULE 9: ADD reads the integer values from source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the resulting value to destination register rd.
RULE 10: ADD computes the integer sum of the values read from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 11: ADD discards any overflow beyond the XLEN width and writes only the low XLEN bits of the sum into rd.
RULE 12: ADD is an integer register-register addition instruction defined in the base ISA.
RULE 13: ADD adds the integer values from its two source registers and writes the sum to the destination register.
RULE 14: ADDW's behavior mirrors ADD's behavior but operates on 32-bit operands, sign-extending the low 32 bits of the result to 64 bits before writing to the destination register.
RULE 15: The AMOADD.B/H instruction defined by the Zabha extension atomically adds a value to a byte- or halfword-sized memory location.
RULE 16: The result written to the destination register rd by AMOADD.B/H must be sign-extended to the XLEN of the processor.
RULE 17: AMOADD.B/H must ignore the bits supplied in the source register rs2, using only the address in rs1 and the value in rd for the operation.
RULE 18: The address contained in rs1 for AMOADD.B/H must be naturally aligned to the operand size (1-byte for AMOADD.B, 2-byte for AMOADD.H); if the address is not naturally aligned, the same exception behavior specified for misaligned accesses in the base A extension applies.
RULE 19: AMOADD.B/H may set the acquire (aq) and release (rl) bits to request optional release-consistency ordering guarantees.
RULE 20: C.ADD is defined only when the rd and rs2 fields are not both zero, because the encoding with both fields zero denotes C.EBREAK.
RULE 21: The compressed instruction c.add, which performs an ADD operation, MUST be supported by all RV32 implementations.
RULE 22: The compressed instruction c.add, which performs an ADD operation, MUST be supported by all RV64 implementations.

INSTRUCTION: ADD.UW
FORMAT: add.uw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: ADD.UW reads the 32-bit low word of the register specified by rd'/rs1' and produces a result in which bits 31-0 equal those original bits and all bits more significant than bit 31 are set to zero.
RULE 2: ADD.UW writes the zero-extended result to the same register specified by rd'/rs1', replacing the register's previous value.
RULE 3: ADD.UW does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd'/rs1'.
RULE 4: ADD.UW has an architectural effect that is identical to the effect of the c.zext.w instruction applied to the same register.
RULE 5: ADD.UW must be defined only on RV64 implementations; on RV32 the encoding is reserved and its behavior is not defined.
RULE 6: In an ADD.UW instruction the source register specified with the .uw suffix provides an unsigned 32-bit operand that is taken from the low 32 bits of that register.
RULE 7: In an ADD.UW instruction the source register that is not designated with the .uw suffix contributes its full XLEN-bit value to the operation.
RULE 8: ADD.UW adds the unsigned 32-bit operand from the .uw source register to the full-XLEN operand from the other source register using full-XLEN arithmetic and produces an XLEN-wide result.
RULE 9: ADD.UW writes the XLEN-wide result of the addition into the destination register rd.
RULE 10: ADD.UW is only available in the RV64 configuration and is not defined for RV32.
RULE 11: ADD.UW adds the unsigned word values from the two source registers and writes the resulting sum to the destination register.
RULE 12: ADD.UW computes X(rd) as the XLEN-bit sum of the value in register rs2 and the zero-extended 32-bit value taken from bits 31..0 of register rs1.
RULE 13: For ADD.UW the operand derived from rs1 is formed by filling the upper XLEN-32 bits with zeros after extracting bits 31..0 of X(rs1).
RULE 14: The addition performed by ADD.UW is modulo 2^XLEN, so any overflow is discarded and only the low XLEN bits of the sum are written to rd.
RULE 15: When rs2 is the zero register, ADD.UW writes to rd the zero-extended lower word of rs1, producing the same architectural result as a zero-extension operation.

INSTRUCTION: ADDI
FORMAT: addi rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: ADDI writes its XLEN-bit result to the destination register rd.
RULE 2: ADDI adds the signed value in source register rs1 to the sign-extended immediate and stores the XLEN-bit sum in rd.
RULE 3: ADDI does not raise any arithmetic exception, including on overflow.
RULE 4: ADDI MUST add the sign-extended 12-bit immediate to the value in register rs1 and write the sum to register rd.
RULE 5: If the addition produces a value wider than XLEN bits, ADDI MUST ignore arithmetic overflow and write only the low XLEN bits of the sum to rd.
RULE 6: When the 12-bit immediate field of ADDI is zero, ADDI MUST copy the value of rs1 into rd, implementing the MV pseudoinstruction.
RULE 7: In a PUSH sequence, the adjustment of the stack-pointer register is performed by an ADDI instruction that adds an immediate value to the stack-pointer.
RULE 8: The architectural effect of the ADDI that adjusts the stack-pointer must be committed only when it is certain that the entire PUSH instruction will commit.
RULE 9: ADDI may specify the same architectural register as both its source register and its destination register.
RULE 10: ADDI uses a signed immediate operand, allowing negative values such as –64.
RULE 11: ADDI adds the sign-extended immediate to the value read from the source register and writes the 32-bit sum to the destination register.
RULE 12: When the destination register specified by ADDI is identical to its source register, the register is overwritten with the sum of its original content and the sign-extended immediate.
RULE 13: ADDI adds the sign-extended immediate to the value in the source register to produce an architectural result.
RULE 14: The architectural result of an ADDI instruction is written to the destination register specified by the instruction.
RULE 15: When the immediate field of an ADDI instruction is -1, the architectural result equals the source register value minus one.

INSTRUCTION: ADDIW
FORMAT: addiw rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: ADDIW is defined only for RV64I implementations.
RULE 2: ADDIW adds the sign-extended 12-bit immediate to the 64-bit value in rs1, discards any overflow by taking the low 32 bits of the sum, and writes to rd the sign extension of those 32 bits to 64 bits.
RULE 3: When the immediate field of ADDIW is zero, ADDIW writes to rd the sign extension of the lower 32 bits of rs1, acting as the assembler pseudoinstruction SEXT.W.
RULE 4: In RV64C, the compressed instruction encoding that uses the same opcode as the RV32C compressed jump-and-link must execute the ADDIW operation.
RULE 5: In RV64C, the compressed ADDIW instruction must produce the same architectural effect as the corresponding uncompressed ADDIW instruction.
RULE 6: An implementation that includes the RV64 base ISA MUST provide the c.ADDIW instruction.
RULE 7: An implementation that includes only the RV32 base ISA MUST NOT provide the c.ADDIW instruction (its encoding is reserved).

INSTRUCTION: ADDW
FORMAT: addw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: ADDW is defined only for RV64I implementations and is not defined for RV32I.
RULE 2: ADDW adds the low 32 bits of the two source registers as signed 32-bit integers and discards any overflow, keeping only the low 32 bits of the sum.
RULE 3: ADDW sign-extends the low 32-bit sum to 64 bits and writes the resulting 64-bit value to the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: AND
FORMAT: and rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The AND instruction reads the integer register identified by rs1 as a source operand.
RULE 2: The AND instruction reads the integer register identified by rs2 as a source operand.
RULE 3: The AND instruction writes the result of its operation into the integer register identified by rd.
RULE 4: The AND instruction computes the bitwise logical AND of the values read from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 5: The AND instruction MUST compute the bitwise logical AND of the values in registers rs1 and rs2 and write the resulting XLEN-bit value to register rd.
RULE 6: The AND instruction MUST NOT modify any architectural registers other than rd.
RULE 7: The AND instruction MUST not raise an exception or trap for any defined encoding of the instruction.
RULE 8: The AMOAND instruction atomically loads a data value from the address in rs1, places the loaded value into rd, computes the bitwise AND of the loaded value and the original value in rs2, and stores the result back to the address in rs1.
RULE 9: When an AMOAND.W is executed on RV64, the 32-bit result of the AND operation placed in rd is sign-extended to 64 bits and the upper 32 bits of rs2 are ignored.
RULE 10: The address in rs1 used by an AMOAND instruction must be naturally aligned to the operand size, AND if it is not, an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception is generated.
RULE 11: If a misaligned-atomicity-granule PMA is present and all bytes accessed by an AMOAND lie within the same granule, the instruction does not raise an exception for address alignment AND executes atomically as a single memory operation.
RULE 12: If the aq bit of an AMOAND instruction is set, then no later memory operations in the same hart may be observed to occur before the AMOAND, AND this ordering is enforced.
RULE 13: If the rl bit of an AMOAND instruction is set, then other harts may not observe the AMOAND before any memory accesses that precede it in the same hart, AND this release ordering is guaranteed.
RULE 14: If both aq and rl bits of an AMOAND instruction are set, the operation is sequentially consistent, AND it cannot be reordered with earlier or later memory operations from the same hart.
RULE 15: The AMOAND.B/H instruction must atomically read the byte or halfword at the address in rs1, compute the bitwise AND of that original memory value with the least-significant byte or halfword of rs2 (ignoring any higher-order bits of rs2), and write the resulting byte or halfword back to the same address.
RULE 16: The AMOAND.B/H instruction places the original memory byte or halfword value into rd and sign-extends that value to the XLEN of the integer register file.
RULE 17: The AMOAND.B/H instruction must require that the address in rs1 be naturally aligned to the operand size and must raise the same misaligned-address exception defined for the base A-extension if the address is not naturally aligned.
RULE 18: The aq and rl bits of the AMOAND.B/H encoding may be set to request acquire and/or release ordering, thereby providing optional release-consistency semantics for the AND operation.
RULE 19: For AMOAND.B/H the bits of rs2 beyond the selected byte or halfword are ignored in the AND computation, so they have no effect on the stored result.
RULE 20: The vand.vv instruction computes the bitwise logical AND of each element of source vector register vs1 with the corresponding element of source vector register vs2 and writes the resulting elements to destination vector register vd while applying the optional mask vm.
RULE 21: The vand.vx instruction computes the bitwise logical AND of each element of source vector register vs2 with the low XLEN bits of integer register rs1 broadcast to every element and writes the resulting elements to destination vector register vd while applying the optional mask vm.
RULE 22: The vand.vi instruction computes the bitwise logical AND of each element of source vector register vs2 with the immediate value imm broadcast to every element and writes the resulting elements to destination vector register vd while applying the optional mask vm.
RULE 23: The vmand.mm instruction must perform an element-wise logical AND of the corresponding bits in the source mask registers vs2 and vs1.
RULE 24: The vmand.mm instruction must write the result of the element-wise logical AND to the destination mask register vd, overwriting its previous contents.
RULE 25: For each element, the destination mask bit resulting from the vmand.mm instruction is set to 1 if both source mask bits are 1, AND it is cleared to 0 otherwise.
RULE 26: The vector reduction AND instruction computes vd[0] as the bitwise AND of vs1[0] and every element of vs2, i.e., vd[0] = AND(vs1[0], vs2[*]).
RULE 27: The vmand.mm instruction computes for each mask element i the logical AND of the corresponding bits in the source mask registers, storing the result in the destination mask register as vd.mask[i] = vs2.mask[i] AND vs1.mask[i].
RULE 28: Each element of a mask register is a single bit and the vmand.mm instruction applies the AND operation to whole mask registers regardless of the vlmul setting in vtype.
RULE 29: The destination vector register may be the same as either source vector register AND the vmand.mm instruction does not modify the architectural register vlmul.
RULE 30: Elements whose indices are less than the current vstart value are left unchanged by the vmand.mm instruction AND are not affected by the AND operation.
RULE 31: After the vmand.mm instruction completes, the architectural register vstart is reset to zero AND no other state is altered by the AND operation.
RULE 32: All vector mask logical instructions, including vmand.mm, are always executed unmasked, therefore there are no inactive elements during the AND operation.
RULE 33: Mask elements whose indices are beyond the current vector length vl are updated according to the tail-agnostic policy when the vmand.mm instruction performs the AND operation.
RULE 34: When the AMOLogical support level is implemented, the processor MUST support the logical atomic memory operation amoand together with swap instructions.
RULE 35: When the AMOLogical support level is provided, a naturally aligned amoand of a given width is supported only if the accessed memory region permits reads and writes of that width.

INSTRUCTION: ANDI
FORMAT: andi rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: ANDI must compute the bitwise logical AND of the value in source register rs1 and the sign-extended 12-bit immediate operand.
RULE 2: ANDI must write the result of that bitwise AND operation into destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: ANDN
FORMAT: andn rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The ANDN instruction computes rd = rs1 & ~rs2 (bitwise AND of rs1 with the bitwise complement of rs2) and writes the XLEN-wide result to rd.
RULE 2: Because the ANDN mnemonic has no .b, .h, .w, or .uw suffix, the ANDN instruction operates on the full XLEN width of its source registers and produces an XLEN-wide result.
RULE 3: ANDN must be defined for implementations that support the RV32 base ISA and for implementations that support the RV64 base ISA.
RULE 4: ANDN computes the destination register rd as the bitwise AND of the first source register rs1 and the bitwise complement of the second source register rs2.
RULE 5: ANDN treats the operation as if the rs2 operand is inverted before the logical AND is performed.
RULE 6: ANDN produces a result whose width equals the XLEN of the executing hart (32 bits on RV32 and 64 bits on RV64).
RULE 7: The ANDN instruction computes rd = rs1 & ~rs2, performing a bitwise AND of the first source operand with the bitwise complement of the second source operand.
RULE 8: The ANDN instruction operates on XLEN-bit values, where XLEN is 32 for RV32 and 64 for RV64.
RULE 9: The ANDN instruction MUST compute for every bit position *i* the logical AND of the *i*-th bit of register *rs1* and the logical NOT of the *i*-th bit of register *rs2*, and MUST write the resulting word to register *rd*.
RULE 10: The ANDN instruction MUST NOT modify the contents of registers *rs1* or *rs2*.
RULE 11: The ANDN instruction MUST NOT affect any architectural status flags or cause side effects other than updating *rd*.
RULE 12: The ANDN instruction computes rd as the bitwise logical AND of the value in rs1 and the bitwise inversion of the value in rs2.
RULE 13: ANDN must be provided by any implementation that includes the RVB (Bitmanip) extension for both RV32 and RV64 base ISAs.
RULE 14: ANDN computes the bitwise AND of the value in source register rs1 and the bitwise NOT of the value in source register rs2, and writes the XLEN-wide result to destination register rd.
RULE 15: ANDN operates on the full XLEN bits of the registers, where XLEN equals 32 for RV32 and 64 for RV64.

INSTRUCTION: AUIPC
FORMAT: auipc rd, uimm20
RULE 1: AUIPC uses the U-type format and its U-immediate field supplies the upper 20 bits of a 32-bit offset with the lower 12 bits forced to zero.
RULE 2: AUIPC computes the sum of the address of the AUIPC instruction and the 32-bit offset formed by concatenating the U-immediate with twelve zero bits, and writes this sum into the destination register rd.
RULE 3: AUIPC can be paired with a subsequent JALR instruction whose 12-bit immediate is added to the value produced by AUIPC to transfer control to any 32-bit PC-relative address.
RULE 4: AUIPC can be paired with a subsequent load or store instruction whose 12-bit immediate is added to the value produced by AUIPC to access any 32-bit PC-relative data address.
RULE 5: When the U-immediate field of AUIPC is zero, the instruction writes the current PC value (the address of the AUIPC instruction) into rd.
RULE 6: AUIPC followed by a JALR instruction may transfer control to any target address that lies within the full 32-bit PC-relative address range.
RULE 7: AUIPC forms a 32-bit offset by placing the 20-bit U-type immediate in bits 31:12 and setting bits 11:0 to zero.
RULE 8: AUIPC sign-extends the 32-bit offset to the processor's XLEN before using it.
RULE 9: AUIPC adds the sign-extended offset to the address of the AUIPC instruction itself.
RULE 10: AUIPC writes the result of that addition into the architectural register specified by rd.
RULE 11: AUIPC paired with JALR can form address offsets whose signed values lie in the interval [-2^31-2^11 , 2^31-2^11-1].

INSTRUCTION: BCLR
FORMAT: bclr rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: BCLR clears a single bit of the value read from register rs1.
RULE 2: BCLR selects the bit to clear using the bit index supplied by register rs2.
RULE 3: BCLR writes the resulting value, with the selected bit cleared, to destination register rd.
RULE 4: BCLR writes to destination register rd the value of source register rs1 with the bit at the position indicated by the low log2(XLEN) bits of source register rs2 cleared to zero, leaving all other bits of rs1 unchanged.
RULE 5: BCLR determines the bit index to clear by extracting the low log2(XLEN) bits from rs2 and interpreting that value as an unsigned position between 0 and XLEN-1.

INSTRUCTION: BCLRI
FORMAT: bclri rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: BCLRI is defined for both RV32 and RV64 implementations and therefore must be a legal instruction for processors with XLEN equal to 32 or 64.
RULE 2: Execution of BCLRI writes to rd the value of rs1 with the bit at position imm cleared to zero while preserving all other bits of rs1.
RULE 3: BCLRI writes to rd the value of rs1 with the single bit at the index specified by shamt cleared to zero while all other bits of rs1 remain unchanged.
RULE 4: BCLRI obtains the bit index to clear by extracting the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt immediate field.

INSTRUCTION: BEXT
FORMAT: bext rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: BEXT extracts the bit at the index given by the low-order bits of rs2 from the value in rs1 and writes a zero-extended result of either 0 or 1 to rd.
RULE 2: An RV32 implementation MUST implement the BEXT instruction.
RULE 3: An RV64 implementation MUST implement the BEXT instruction.
RULE 4: When BEXT is executed, it MUST compute an index value equal to the contents of rs2 masked with the value (XLEN − 1), i.e., index = X(rs2) & (XLEN-1).
RULE 5: BEXT MUST shift the contents of rs1 right by the computed index and then mask the result with 1, i.e., X(rd) = (X(rs1) >> index) & 1.
RULE 6: The architectural result written by BEXT to rd is guaranteed to be either 0 or 1.
RULE 7: BEXT does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: BEXTI
FORMAT: bexti rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: BEXTI extracts the bit of register rs1 at the position given by the immediate imm and writes the extracted bit (zero-extended) to destination register rd.
RULE 2: BEXTI extracts the single bit from the source register rs1 at the bit position specified by the immediate imm and writes that extracted bit to the destination register rd.
RULE 3: BEXTI MUST compute the bit index as the bitwise AND of the shamt field and the value XLEN-1, thereby using the lower log2(XLEN) bits of shamt.
RULE 4: BEXTI MUST place in destination register rd the least-significant bit of the value obtained by logically right-shifting source register rs1 by the computed index.

INSTRUCTION: BINV
FORMAT: binv rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The BINV instruction is defined as a single-bit invert operation that takes three register operands encoded as rd, rs1, and rs2.
RULE 2: The BINV instruction must be implemented for RV32 implementations.
RULE 3: The BINV instruction must be implemented for RV64 implementations.
RULE 4: BINV MUST produce a result in rd that equals the value in rs1 with the single bit whose index is given by the value in rs2 inverted, leaving all other bits unchanged.
RULE 5: BINV writes to rd the result of X(rs1) XOR (1 shifted left by index), i.e., X(rd) = X(rs1) ^ (1 << index).
RULE 6: BINV does not cause any architectural side effects other than writing the destination register; it does not modify memory or any other registers.
RULE 7: BINV never raises an exception for any value of rs2; the operation is defined for all possible contents of rs2.

INSTRUCTION: BINVI
FORMAT: binvi rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: Implementations that support the RV32 integer ISA MUST implement the BINVI instruction.
RULE 2: Implementations that support the RV64 integer ISA MUST implement the BINVI instruction.
RULE 3: When a BINVI instruction executes, the destination register rd receives the value of source register rs1 with the single bit at the position specified by the immediate operand inverted.
RULE 4: Execution of a BINVI instruction MUST NOT modify any integer register other than the destination register rd.
RULE 5: Execution of a BINVI instruction MUST NOT modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 6: BINVI rd, rs1, shamt MUST compute index as shamt & (XLEN-1) (i.e., the lower log2(XLEN) bits of shamt) and write to integer register rd the value X(rs1) XOR (1 << index).
RULE 7: BINVI rd, rs1, shamt MUST leave all integer registers other than rd unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: BLT
FORMAT: blt rs1, rs2, label
RULE 1: BLT performs a signed two's-complement comparison of the values in its first source register and its second source register.
RULE 2: If the signed value in the first source register of a BLT instruction is less than the signed value in the second source register, BLT transfers control to the target label specified by the instruction.
RULE 3: If the signed value in the first source register of a BLT instruction is not less than the signed value in the second source register, BLT does not transfer control to the target label and execution continues with the following sequential instruction.
RULE 4: The signed comparison used by BLT treats the register contents as two's-complement integers, so the sign bit determines ordering for negative and positive values.
RULE 5: BLT may be employed to detect signed-addition overflow when the sign of one operand is known, by comparing the register holding the addition result with the register holding the original operand; a branch taken indicates that the result is less than the original operand, which signals overflow for that pattern.

INSTRUCTION: BLTU
FORMAT: bltu rs1, rs2, label
RULE 1: BLTU MUST be a branch instruction that performs an unsigned-less-than comparison of the unsigned integer values in its first source register and its second source register.
RULE 2: BLTU MUST take the branch to the target address encoded in its immediate when the unsigned value in the first source register is less than the unsigned value in the second source register.
RULE 3: BLTU MUST not take the branch (execution continues with the following instruction) when the unsigned value in the first source register is greater than or equal to the unsigned value in the second source register.
RULE 4: When BLTU takes the branch, the program counter is updated to the address of the label specified by the branch immediate.
RULE 5: When BLTU does not take the branch, the program counter is incremented to the address of the instruction following BLTU.
RULE 6: Using BLTU after an unsigned addition with the addition result as the first operand and one of the addends as the second operand MUST cause the branch to be taken if unsigned overflow occurred, thereby providing a single-instruction method for overflow checking of unsigned addition.

INSTRUCTION: BNE
FORMAT: bne rs1, rs2, label
RULE 1: BNE MUST compare the value in its first source register with the value in its second source register and take the branch when the two values are not equal.
RULE 2: BNE MUST NOT take the branch when the value in its first source register is equal to the value in its second source register, and execution proceeds to the sequential next instruction.
RULE 3: When BNE takes the branch, it MUST sign-extend the immediate offset field, add the sign-extended offset to the address of the BNE instruction (the current pc), and use the resulting sum as the branch-target address.

INSTRUCTION: BREV8
FORMAT: brev8 rd, rs1
RULE 1: The BREV8 instruction takes a source register rs and a destination register rd, and the architectural result is written to rd.
RULE 2: Executing BREV8 reverses the order of bits within each byte of the value held in rs and writes the concatenation of the reversed bytes to rd.
RULE 3: In an RV32 implementation, BREV8 processes the low 32 bits of rs, reverses the bits in each of the four bytes, and writes the resulting 32-bit value to rd.
RULE 4: In an RV64 implementation, BREV8 processes the full 64 bits of rs, reverses the bits in each of the eight bytes, and writes the resulting 64-bit value to rd.
RULE 5: The BREV8 instruction reverses the bits in each byte of the source operand and writes the resulting value to the destination register.
RULE 6: BREV8 reverses the order of the bits within each 8-bit byte of the value read from the source integer register rs1.
RULE 7: BREV8 writes each bit-reversed source byte into the same byte position of the destination integer register rd, leaving the relative ordering of bytes unchanged.
RULE 8: BREV8 updates the architectural integer register rd with the xlen-bit result that consists of the concatenation of all bit-reversed bytes of rs1, so that every bit of rd is defined after the instruction executes.
RULE 9: BREV8 rd, rs MUST set X(rd) to an XLEN-bit value whose each byte i (for i = 0 … XLEN/8-1) equals the bits of byte i of X(rs) reversed in order.
RULE 10: BREV8 rd, rs reads the architectural register specified as rs and writes the architectural register specified as rd, and MUST NOT modify any other architectural state.
RULE 11: BREV8 rd, rs reverses the order of the eight bits in each byte of the source register so that bit 0 becomes bit 7, bit 1 becomes bit 6, …, and bit 7 becomes bit 0.
RULE 12: The result produced by BREV8 rd, rs has a width equal to the implementation's XLEN, consisting of the reversed-bit bytes concatenated to form an XLEN-bit word.
RULE 13: An implementation that includes the Zbkb extension MUST implement the BREV8 instruction.
RULE 14: An implementation that includes the Zbkc extension MUST implement the BREV8 instruction.
RULE 15: An implementation that includes the Zbkx extension MUST implement the BREV8 instruction.
RULE 16: The BREV8 instruction MUST reverse the order of bits within each byte of the source operand.
RULE 17: The BREV8 instruction MUST write the byte-wise bit-reversed result to the destination register, preserving the register width of the executing ISA (32 bits for RV32, 64 bits for RV64).
RULE 18: The BREV8 instruction is defined for RV32 implementations.
RULE 19: The BREV8 instruction is defined for RV64 implementations.
RULE 20: BREV8 MUST read each active element of the source vector register vs2 from index vstart up to the current vector length vl1.
RULE 21: BREV8 MUST treat each active element as SEW bits partitioned into bytes and replace each byte with the reversal of its bit order.
RULE 22: BREV8 MUST write the resulting SEW-wide element to the corresponding index of the destination vector register vd.
RULE 23: BREV8 MUST not modify any vector register other than vd.
RULE 24: BREV8 MUST not alter any architectural state other than the contents of vd and the normal program-counter advancement.
RULE 25: BREV8 MUST be defined for every supported SEW, allowing the instruction to operate on elements of any width without changing SEW.
RULE 26: BREV8 MUST retire successfully (RETIRE_SUCCESS) after processing all active elements.
RULE 27: BREV8 MUST respect the active vector mask vm, writing results only to elements whose mask bit is set and leaving other elements of vd unchanged.
RULE 28: BREV8 MUST use the current vector configuration (including vtype and vlen) to determine the element width SEW and the effective vector length vl.
RULE 29: BREV8 must produce an output value in which, for every byte of the input, bit 0 is placed in bit 7, bit 1 in bit 6, …, and bit 7 in bit 0 of the corresponding output byte.
RULE 30: In the vghsh.vv instruction, the hash subkey H is defined as the result of applying BREV8 to the vector element obtained from source register vs2.
RULE 31: In the vghsh.vv instruction, the intermediate value S is defined as the result of applying BREV8 to the bitwise XOR of the partial hash Y and the cipher block X.
RULE 32: In the vghsh.vv instruction, the final multiplication result is defined as the result of applying BREV8 to the intermediate product Z.
RULE 33: BREV8 must unconditionally swap each bit with its opposite position within each byte, independent of the data values.

INSTRUCTION: BSET
FORMAT: bset rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: BSET reads the source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes a result to the destination register rd.
RULE 2: BSET rd, rs1, rs2 sets the bit in the value read from rs1 at the index given by the integer value in rs2 to 1 and writes the resulting word to rd.
RULE 3: BSET rd, rs1, rs2 leaves all bits of the rs1 value unchanged except the bit at the index specified by rs2, which is forced to 1.
RULE 4: BSET rd, rs1, rs2 does not alter any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 5: The index used by BSET to select the bit is supplied by the source register rs2.
RULE 6: BSET must compute the bit index by masking the XLEN-bit value of rs2 with the constant XLEN-1, thereby using only the lower log2(XLEN) bits of rs2.
RULE 7: BSET must create a mask value equal to the integer 1 shifted left by the computed index, using XLEN-bit logical left-shift semantics.
RULE 8: BSET must set the destination register rd to the bitwise OR of the XLEN-bit value of rs1 and the mask value produced from the shift.
RULE 9: BSET must produce a result in rd that is identical to the value of rs1 with exactly one bit set at the position indicated by the lower log2(XLEN) bits of rs2.

INSTRUCTION: BSETI
FORMAT: bseti rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: Execution of the BSETI instruction performs a single-bit set where the bit position is specified by the immediate operand.
RULE 2: BSETI sets the bit in the source register rs1 whose index is specified by the immediate operand imm.
RULE 3: BSETI writes the value produced after setting the specified bit to the destination register rd.
RULE 4: BSETI preserves all bits of rs1 other than the bit indexed by imm unchanged in the result written to rd.
RULE 5: BSETI reads the bit index from the lower log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt operand.
RULE 6: BSETI produces a result equal to the value in rs1 with the bit at the index read from shamt set to one, and writes this result to rd.
RULE 7: For RV32 implementations, any encoding of BSETI in which bit 5 of the shamt field (shamt[5]) is one is reserved.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADD
FORMAT: c.add rd, rs2
RULE 1: C.ADD-encoded NTL hints must not change any architectural state.
RULE 2: C.ADD-encoded NTL hints must not alter the architecturally visible effects of their target instruction.
RULE 3: C.ADD-encoded NTL hints affect all memory-access instructions except cache-management instructions defined in the Zicbom extension.
RULE 4: C.ADD-encoded NTL hints affect memory-access instructions defined in the base ISA and the A, F, D, Q, C, and V standard extensions as well as those defined in the hypervisor extension in Volume II.
RULE 5: When a trap is taken on the target instruction, an implementation should ignore the C.ADD-encoded NTL hint for the first instruction in the trap handler.
RULE 6: If an interrupt occurs between the execution of a C.ADD-encoded NTL hint and its target instruction, execution resumes at the target instruction without re-executing the hint and without changing program semantics.
RULE 7: C.ADD adds the integer values in registers rd and rs2 and writes the resulting sum to register rd.
RULE 8: C.ADD has the same architectural effect as executing the 32-bit ADD instruction with operands rd, rd, rs2.
RULE 9: C.ADD MUST NOT be considered valid when the encoded rs2 field equals x0; such encodings correspond to C.JALR or C.EBREAK instead.
RULE 10: C.ADD encodings with rs2 ≠ x0 and rd = x0 are defined as HINTs and do not perform the addition operation.
RULE 11: C.ADD x0, rs2 is defined as an RVC hint and therefore does not modify any architectural state other than advancing the program counter and updating any applicable performance counters.
RULE 12: An instruction whose bits satisfy inst[15:13]=100, the unspecified bit=1, rs1/rd ≠ 0, rs2 ≠ 0, and inst[1:0]=10 must be decoded as C.ADD.
RULE 13: C.ADD must have a non-zero source/rd field; encodings with rs1/rd = 0 are not C.ADD.
RULE 14: C.ADD must have a non-zero second source register; encodings with rs2 = 0 are not C.ADD.
RULE 15: If the destination register field rd of a decoded C.ADD instruction is zero, the implementation must treat the instruction as a hint and must not produce any architectural effect.
RULE 16: C.ADD MUST be implemented as a compressed instruction in all RV32 implementations.
RULE 17: C.ADD MUST be implemented as a compressed instruction in all RV64 implementations.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDI
FORMAT: c.addi rd, c_nzimm6
RULE 1: C.ADDI adds the sign-extended 6-bit immediate to the current value of register rd and writes the result back to rd for encodings where rd ≠ x0 and the immediate is non-zero.
RULE 2: C.ADDI has the same architectural effect as an addi rd, rd, imm instruction.
RULE 3: When a C.ADDI encoding has rd ≠ x0 and the immediate field equal to zero, the instruction is defined as a HINT and must not modify any architectural state.
RULE 4: When a C.ADDI encoding has rd = x0 and the immediate field equal to zero, the instruction is interpreted as C.NOP and performs no architectural effect.
RULE 5: When a C.ADDI encoding has rd = x0 and the immediate field not equal to zero, the instruction is defined as a HINT and must not modify any architectural state.
RULE 6: Executing C.ADDI with immediate zero acts as a hint that does not modify any architectural state except that the program counter is advanced and any applicable performance counters are updated.
RULE 7: Executing C.ADDI with immediate zero overwrites the destination register with a copy of its original value, resulting in no change to architectural state.
RULE 8: Implementations may ignore the hint semantics of C.ADDI and treat the instruction as a no-op.
RULE 9: C.ADDI with an assembled immediate value of zero is classified as a microarchitectural hint (HINT) and does not perform an addition operation.
RULE 10: Execution of C.ADDI with an immediate of 0 overwrites its destination register with the register's current value.
RULE 11: When C.ADDI is used as a hint and executed with XLEN < MXLEN, if the bits of the destination register above XLEN are not all equal to bit XLEN-1, the implementation-defined outcome is either to leave those upper bits unchanged or to overwrite them with copies of bit XLEN-1.
RULE 12: The implementation's choice to elide or perform the register write-back for C.ADDI hint instructions is observable only to privilege modes whose XLEN setting exceeds the current XLEN and is invisible to the current privilege mode.
RULE 13: When UXLEN ≠ SXLEN, C.ADDI must ignore source register operand bits that are above the configured XLEN.
RULE 14: When UXLEN ≠ SXLEN, C.ADDI must sign-extend its result to fill the widest supported XLEN in the destination register.
RULE 15: C.ADDI with an immediate of 0 must write the destination register with its current value, leaving the register unchanged.
RULE 16: When C.ADDI used as the zero-immediate hint is executed with XLEN < SXLEN and the upper bits of the destination register are not all equal to bit XLEN-1, it is implementation-defined whether those upper bits remain unchanged or are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN-1.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDI16SP
FORMAT: c.addi16sp sp, c_nzimm10
RULE 1: When the register field of a C.MOP.n encoding contains x2, the instruction must be decoded as C.ADDI16SP.
RULE 2: C.ADDI16SP writes its result to register x2 (the stack pointer).
RULE 3: C.ADDI16SP adds a sign-extended 6-bit immediate that must be non-zero to the current value of register x2.
RULE 4: C.ADDI16SP scales its immediate by 16, so the addition uses multiples of 16.
RULE 5: C.ADDI16SP produces a result that must lie in the range –512 to 496 inclusive.
RULE 6: C.ADDI16SP has the same architectural effect as executing the 32-bit instruction addi x2, x2, nzimm[9:4].

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDI4SPN
FORMAT: c.addi4spn rd, sp, c_nzuimm10
RULE 1: C.ADDI4SPN adds the zero-extended immediate, multiplied by 4, to the stack pointer register x2 and writes the resulting value to the destination register rd′.
RULE 2: An encoding of C.ADDI4SPN with uimm equal to 0 is reserved and must be treated as an illegal instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDIW
FORMAT: c.addiw rd, c_imm6
RULE 1: C.ADDIW is defined only for RV64C implementations.
RULE 2: The immediate field of C.ADDIW is sign-extended from 6 bits to the operand width before being used in the addition.
RULE 3: When the sign-extended immediate of C.ADDIW is zero, the instruction produces the same architectural effect as a sign-extend-word (sext.w) of the destination register.
RULE 4: C.ADDIW adds the sign-extended immediate to the current value of the destination register, computes the result modulo 2^32, and then sign-extends that 32-bit result to 64 bits before writing it back to the destination register.
RULE 5: The architectural result of C.ADDIW is equivalent to executing the 32-bit-operand instruction addiw rd, rd, imm.
RULE 6: C.ADDIW must not be used with destination register x0; encodings where rd = x0 are reserved.
RULE 7: C.ADDIW MUST NOT have a zero register specifier; the rs1/rd field must be a non-zero register.
RULE 8: C.ADDIW with the destination register rd equal to x0 is reserved, meaning the architecture does not define any effect for that encoding.
RULE 9: C.ADDIW implements the same architectural effect as the 32-bit ADDIW instruction from the application-class profile.

INSTRUCTION: C.ADDW
FORMAT: c.addw rd, rs2
RULE 1: C.ADDW adds the values in registers rd′ and rs2′, takes the lower 32 bits of the sum, sign-extends that 32-bit result to 64 bits, and writes the sign-extended value to register rd′.
RULE 2: C.ADDW expands architecturally to the 32-bit-add instruction addw rd′, rd′, rs2′.
RULE 3: C.ADDW is not defined for RV32C implementations, and its encoding is reserved when executed on an RV32C core.
RULE 4: The encoding that identifies C.ADDW is defined only for the RV64 base ISA and is reserved in RV32.
RULE 5: C.ADDW uses the register specified by rd′/rs1′ as both the destination register and the first source register.
RULE 6: C.ADDW uses the register specified by rs2′ as its second source register.

INSTRUCTION: C.AND
FORMAT: c.and rd, rs2
RULE 1: C.AND MUST compute the bitwise AND of the architectural values in the registers specified by rd′ and rs2′.
RULE 2: C.AND MUST write the result of that bitwise AND into the register specified by rd′, replacing its previous architectural value.
RULE 3: C.AND MUST have the same architectural effect as an AND instruction that uses rd′ as both the destination and first source register and rs2′ as the second source register.
RULE 4: RV32 implementations must support C.AND.
RULE 5: RV64 implementations must support C.AND.

INSTRUCTION: C.ANDI
FORMAT: c.andi rd, c_imm6
RULE 1: C.ANDI MUST compute the bitwise AND of the value in register rd′ and a sign-extended 6-bit immediate.
RULE 2: C.ANDI MUST write the result of the bitwise AND operation back to register rd′.
RULE 3: C.ANDI MUST treat its 6-bit immediate field as a signed value that is sign-extended to the XLEN before the AND operation.
RULE 4: C.ANDI MUST be architecturally equivalent to executing the base ISA instruction andi rd′, rd′, imm where imm is the sign-extended immediate.
RULE 5: C.ANDI MUST use the same register rd′ as both its source operand and its destination operand.
RULE 6: When the 2-bit opcode field inst[1:0] is 01 and the 3-bit field inst[15:13] is 100, the instruction MUST be decoded as C.ANDI.
RULE 7: The funct3 field of a decoded C.ANDI instruction MUST be binary 10.
RULE 8: The most-significant immediate bit (imm[5]) of a C.ANDI instruction MUST be taken from the encoding bit labeled imm[5].
RULE 9: The lower five immediate bits (imm[4:0]) of a C.ANDI instruction MUST be taken from the encoding bits labeled imm[4:0] that appear in both the high-order and low-order immediate positions.
RULE 10: The register specifier of a C.ANDI instruction MUST select the compressed register rs1′, which also serves as the destination register rd′.
RULE 11: C.ANDI MUST be defined for RV32 implementations.
RULE 12: C.ANDI MUST be defined for RV64 implementations.
RULE 13: C.ANDI MUST follow the same criteria as the uncompressed ANDI instruction defined in the RVI ISA.

INSTRUCTION: C.BEQZ
FORMAT: c.beqz rs1, label
RULE 1: C.BEQZ must transfer control to the branch target address if the value in register rs1′ equals zero.
RULE 2: C.BEQZ must continue execution at the sequential next instruction if the value in register rs1′ is not zero.
RULE 3: C.BEQZ computes its branch target address by sign-extending the immediate offset and adding the result to the address of the C.BEQZ instruction.
RULE 4: C.BEQZ may branch to any address within ±256 bytes of the address of the C.BEQZ instruction.
RULE 5: C.BEQZ has the same architectural effect as the 32-bit instruction beq rs1′, x0, offset, where offset is the sign-extended immediate used by C.BEQZ.
RULE 6: C.BEQZ is encoded using the CB instruction format.

INSTRUCTION: C.BNEZ
FORMAT: c.bnez rs1, label
RULE 1: C.BNEZ's offset field is sign-extended and added to the current PC to produce the branch target address.
RULE 2: C.BNEZ can target a branch target address that lies within ±256 bytes of the PC.
RULE 3: C.BNEZ takes the branch when the value in rs1′ is not zero.
RULE 4: C.BNEZ uses the rs1′ field to specify its source register.

INSTRUCTION: C.EBREAK
FORMAT: c.ebreak
RULE 1: The encoding of C.EBREAK requires that the rs2 field of the compressed instruction be set to x0.
RULE 2: C.EBREAK causes a breakpoint exception that transfers control to the debugging environment.
RULE 3: The RVC Quadrant 2 encoding `100 | 1 | 0 | 0 | 10` MUST be decoded as the C.EBREAK instruction.
RULE 4: When a breakpoint exception is raised by a C.EBREAK instruction, the mtval CSR MUST be written with either zero or the virtual address of the faulting instruction.
RULE 5: When a breakpoint exception is raised by a C.EBREAK instruction, the mepc CSR MUST be written with the virtual address of the faulting instruction.
RULE 6: C.EBREAK performs the same architectural operation as the EBREAK instruction.
RULE 7: C.EBREAK raises a breakpoint exception and performs no other operation unless overridden by an external debug environment.
RULE 8: C.EBREAK sets the epc register of the receiving privilege mode to the address of the C.EBREAK instruction itself.
RULE 9: C.EBREAK causes a synchronous exception.
RULE 10: C.EBREAK is not considered retired.
RULE 11: C.EBREAK should not increment the minstret CSR.
RULE 12: C.EBREAK generates a breakpoint exception whose cause value is Breakpoint (3).
RULE 13: The breakpoint exception generated by C.EBREAK is synchronous.
RULE 14: C.EBREAK does not cause entry into Debug Mode.
RULE 15: If the breakpoint exception generated by C.EBREAK traps to M-mode or S-mode and sctrctl.BPFRZ=1, hardware sets sctrstatus.FROZEN.
RULE 16: If the breakpoint exception generated by C.EBREAK traps to M-mode or S-mode and sctrctl.BPFRZ=1, the breakpoint exception is not recorded.
RULE 17: C.EBREAK MUST raise a breakpoint exception.
RULE 18: When a breakpoint exception is raised by a C.EBREAK instruction, the architectural register stval is written with either zero or the virtual address of that C.EBREAK instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLD
FORMAT: c.fld fd, c_uimm8(rs1)
RULE 1: C.FLD loads a double-precision floating-point value from the memory address computed by its effective address into the floating-point register rd′.
RULE 2: C.FLD computes its effective address by zero-extending the immediate offset, multiplying that offset by 8, and adding the result to the contents of the base register rs1′.
RULE 3: C.FLD has the same architectural effect as executing the uncompressed instruction fld rd′, offset(rs1′) with the same rd′, rs1′, and offset fields.
RULE 4: If an implementation supports the Zcd extension, then the C.FLD instruction is included as a member of that extension.
RULE 5: The C.FLD instruction is only legal when the implementation also supports the Zca extension and the D extension.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLDSP
FORMAT: c.fldsp fd, c_uimm9(sp)
RULE 1: C.FLDSP loads a double-precision floating-point value from memory into the destination floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: C.FLDSP computes its effective address by adding the zero-extended offset, scaled by eight, to the stack pointer register x2.
RULE 3: C.FLDSP expands to the 32-bit instruction fld rd, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.FLDSP writes the floating-point register specified by the rd field of its encoding.
RULE 5: C.FLDSP forms its unsigned immediate by using the uimm[5] field as the most-significant bit and concatenating instruction bits 4-3 and 8-6 to provide the remaining bits.
RULE 6: C.FLDSP is subject to pointer masking.
RULE 7: The address used by C.FLDSP is transformed by the pointer-masking mechanism in the same way as for any other explicit load instruction.
RULE 8: When MXR is in effect at the effective privilege mode for the explicit memory access, pointer masking does not apply to the address used by C.FLDSP.
RULE 9: When MPRV or SPVP are in effect, pointer masking for C.FLDSP is performed according to the pointer-masking settings of the effective privilege mode.
RULE 10: C.FLDSP permits misaligned memory accesses, subject to the same limitations that apply when pointer masking is not in effect.
RULE 11: When address triggers in debug are evaluated, pointer masking is applied to the address used by C.FLDSP.
RULE 12: Pointer masking does not apply to memory accesses generated by page-table walks, IOMMUs, devices, or DMA controllers, so only the address used by C.FLDSP generated by the CPU is subject to pointer masking.
RULE 13: C.FLDSP inherits the permission checks, exception conditions, and architectural effects of the base FLD instruction for explicit memory accesses.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLW
FORMAT: c.flw fd, c_uimm7(rs1)
RULE 1: C.FLW loads a 32-bit single-precision floating-point value from memory into the floating-point register rd′.
RULE 2: C.FLW computes its effective address by zero-extending the encoded offset, multiplying the zero-extended offset by 4, and adding the result to the contents of base register rs1′.
RULE 3: C.FLW performs the same architectural effect as executing the FLW instruction with the same destination register rd′, base register rs1′, and offset.
RULE 4: C.FLW must be decoded when the fetched instruction has bits [15:13] equal to 011 and bits [1:0] equal to 00.
RULE 5: In the encoding of C.FLW, the immediate field uimm[5:3] is taken from bits 12-10 of the instruction.
RULE 6: In the encoding of C.FLW, the source register specifier rs1' is taken from bits 9-7 of the instruction.
RULE 7: In the encoding of C.FLW, the immediate bits uimm[2] and uimm[6] are taken from bits 6 and 5 of the instruction, respectively.
RULE 8: In the encoding of C.FLW, the destination register specifier rd' is taken from bits 4-2 of the instruction.
RULE 9: The bit pattern "011 uimm[5:3] rs1' uimm[2|6] rd' 00" identifies the C.FLW instruction.
RULE 10: A processor that implements the Zca extension must not define C.FLW as a valid instruction.
RULE 11: In a Zca-compliant implementation the encoding for C.FLW is reserved, and its architectural behavior is not defined.

INSTRUCTION: C.FLWSP
FORMAT: c.flwsp fd, c_uimm8(sp)
RULE 1: C.FLWSP loads a 32-bit single-precision floating-point value from memory into floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: C.FLWSP computes its effective address by adding the stack pointer x2 to the zero-extended offset field of the instruction after scaling the offset by 4 (i.e., left-shifting the offset by two bits).
RULE 3: C.FLWSP has the same architectural effect as executing the base ISA instruction flw rd, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.FLWSP writes the 32-bit word loaded from memory into the floating-point register specified by the rd field.
RULE 5: C.FLWSP is defined only for RV32 implementations, and its encoding is reserved in RV64 or RV128 implementations.
RULE 6: C.FLWSP imposes no explicit privilege-level restrictions, generates no defined exceptions, and has no side-effects beyond performing the floating-point load.
RULE 7: If the Zcf extension is implemented, the implementation must provide the C.FLWSP compressed single-precision floating-point load instruction.
RULE 8: C.FLWSP is defined only for RV32 implementations and is not defined for RV64.
RULE 9: C.FLWSP requires that both the Zca and F extensions be present in the implementation.
RULE 10: C.FLWSP must load a 32-bit floating-point value from the memory address computed by its address operand into the destination floating-point register, exactly as the FLW instruction does.
RULE 11: C.FLWSP is an explicit memory access and therefore pointer masking applies to the effective address used by C.FLWSP.
RULE 12: When pointer masking is in effect, the effective address used by C.FLWSP must be transformed according to the pointer-masking rules defined for FLW.
RULE 13: C.FLWSP must support misaligned memory accesses, and the result of a misaligned access must be equivalent to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each aligned sub-access that composes the original misaligned request.
RULE 14: C.FLWSP must enforce the same permission checks and raise the same exceptions as the FLW instruction for any memory access.
RULE 15: C.FLWSP must not permit a misaligned access that would be illegal for an unmasked FLW; such an access must cause the same exception as defined for FLW.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSD
FORMAT: c.fsd fs2, c_uimm8(rs1)
RULE 1: When a C.FSD[SP] instruction defined by the Zdinx extension is executed, any reference to an f register in the original D-extension C.FSD[SP] semantics is replaced by the integer x register with the same number, and all other aspects of the instruction's behavior remain identical to the original D-extension C.FSD[SP] semantics.
RULE 2: C.FSD is defined only for implementations that support the RV32DC or RV64DC extensions; on a core lacking these extensions execution of C.FSD is an illegal instruction.
RULE 3: C.FSD stores the double-precision floating-point value contained in floating-point register rs2′ to the memory location whose effective address is calculated by the instruction.
RULE 4: C.FSD computes its effective address by zero-extending the offset, multiplying the zero-extended offset by 8, and adding the result to the base address in register rs1′.
RULE 5: C.FSD is semantically equivalent to the standard instruction fsd rs2′, offset(rs1′).
RULE 6: C.FSD is a 16-bit instruction that stores a double-precision floating-point value from an F register to memory.
RULE 7: C.FSD is not defined as part of the Zca extension, and when Zca is implemented the C.FSD encoding is excluded.
RULE 8: C.FSD is only defined when an implementation provides the C extension together with both the D and F extensions.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSDSP
FORMAT: c.fsdsp fs2, c_uimm9(sp)
RULE 1: C.FSDSP is defined only for implementations that support the RV32DC or RV64DC extension.
RULE 2: C.FSDSP stores the 64-bit double-precision floating-point value contained in floating-point source register rs2 to memory.
RULE 3: C.FSDSP computes the effective memory address by adding the stack pointer register x2 to the zero-extended immediate offset multiplied by 8 (i.e., shifted left three bits).
RULE 4: C.FSDSP has the same architectural effect as executing the 64-bit store instruction fsd rs2, offset(x2).
RULE 5: Implementations MUST NOT provide the C.FSDSP instruction as part of the Zca extension.
RULE 6: The C.FSDSP instruction is defined in the C extension only when both the D extension and the F extension are implemented.
RULE 7: When the Zcmp extension is implemented, some binary encodings that identify C.FSDSP are reused for other Zcmp instructions.
RULE 8: If the Zcd extension (included when both C and D extensions are present) is implemented, the C.FSDSP instruction is not available.
RULE 9: C.FSDSP MUST be subject to pointer masking because pointer masking applies to all explicit memory accesses.
RULE 10: C.FSDSP MUST support misaligned accesses, and the architectural effect is identical to applying the pointer masking transformation to each constituent aligned memory access.
RULE 11: When MPRV or SPVP is in effect, C.FSDSP MUST apply the pointer masking settings of the effective privilege mode to its effective address.
RULE 12: When MXR is in effect at the effective privilege mode where C.FSDSP performs an explicit memory access, C.FSDSP MUST NOT apply pointer masking to that access.
RULE 13: C.FSDSP MUST apply pointer masking only to accesses generated by CPU instructions, including extensions such as an FPU.
RULE 14: C.FSDSP MUST NOT apply pointer masking to accesses generated by page-table walks, the IOMMU, or devices.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSW
FORMAT: c.fsw fs2, c_uimm7(rs1)
RULE 1: C.FSW stores the single-precision floating-point value contained in floating-point register rs2′ to the memory location whose effective address is computed by the instruction.
RULE 2: C.FSW computes its effective address by adding a zero-extended immediate offset, multiplied by 4, to the base address held in integer register rs1′.
RULE 3: C.FSW has the same architectural effect as executing the 32-bit instruction fsw rs2′, offset(rs1′).
RULE 4: C.FSW MUST store a single-precision (32-bit) floating-point value to memory using the compressed instruction encoding.
RULE 5: The Zcf extension MUST provide C.FSW as the compressed form of a single-precision floating-point store operation.

INSTRUCTION: C.FSWSP
FORMAT: c.fswsp fs2, c_uimm8(sp)
RULE 1: C.FSWSP MUST store the single-precision floating-point value in floating-point register rs2 to the memory location whose effective address is the stack pointer (x2) plus the zero-extended offset field multiplied by 4.
RULE 2: C.FSWSP computes its effective address by zero-extending the offset field, scaling the result by 4, and adding that value to the current contents of the stack pointer register x2.
RULE 3: C.FSWSP behaves as if it were expanded to the 32-bit instruction fsw rs2, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.FSWSP is decoded when the instruction bits inst[15:13] are binary 111 and the bits inst[1:0] are binary 10.
RULE 5: C.FSWSP uses an immediate value formed by concatenating the bits labelled uimm[5:2] and uimm[7:6] from its encoding.
RULE 6: C.FSWSP obtains its source register operand from the rs2 field of the instruction encoding.
RULE 7: C.FSWSP has no specified additional permissions, exception behavior, or side effects beyond its decoding and operand extraction.
RULE 8: C.FSWSP stores a single-precision floating-point value from a floating-point register to memory.
RULE 9: C.FSWSP is a compressed (16-bit) instruction that performs a single-precision floating-point store operation.
RULE 10: C.FSWSP is defined only in implementations that support the Zcf extension.
RULE 11: C.FSWSP is defined only for the RV32 architecture.
RULE 12: C.FSWSP stores a single-precision floating-point value from a floating-point register to memory.
RULE 13: When the Zcf extension is not implemented, the encoding for C.FSWSP is reserved.
RULE 14: C.FSWSP maps to an explicit memory access that stores a 32-bit floating-point value to the effective address, equivalent in architectural effect to the base-ISA FSW instruction.
RULE 15: The effective address used by C.FSWSP is transformed by the pointer-masking operation that is in effect for the current privilege mode.
RULE 16: C.FSWSP supports misaligned memory accesses.
RULE 17: When C.FSWSP performs a misaligned store, the architectural result is identical to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each constituent aligned memory access that would result from splitting the misaligned store.

INSTRUCTION: C.J
FORMAT: c.j label
RULE 1: C.J performs an unconditional control transfer.
RULE 2: C.J sign-extends its immediate offset and adds the result to the current pc to compute the jump target address.
RULE 3: C.J offset must be a multiple of 2 bytes.
RULE 4: C.J may jump to any address within plus or minus 2 KiB of the current pc.
RULE 5: C.J expands to the base instruction jal x0 with the same offset.
RULE 6: When the CTR is in its default configuration (all transfer-type filter bits unimplemented or zero), execution of C.J records a control transfer in enabled privileged modes.
RULE 7: If the transfer-type filter bit for encoding 11 is implemented and set by software, execution of C.J is excluded from CTR recording.

INSTRUCTION: C.JAL
FORMAT: c.jal label
RULE 1: C.JAL computes its jump target address by sign-extending the encoded immediate and adding the result to the current pc.
RULE 2: C.JAL's sign-extended offset permits jumps to any address within ±2 KiB of the current pc.
RULE 3: C.JAL's immediate offset is required to be a multiple of 2 bytes.
RULE 4: C.JAL writes the address of the instruction following the jump (pc + 2) into the link register x1.
RULE 5: C.JAL has the same architectural effect as executing the base instruction jal x1, offset.
RULE 6: C.JAL is a 16-bit compressed instruction that performs a jump-and-link operation.

INSTRUCTION: C.JALR
FORMAT: c.jalr rs1
RULE 1: C.JALR must perform an indirect jump to the address contained in the source register rs1 and must write the address of the following instruction (pc + 2) into the link register x1.
RULE 2: C.JALR is only architecturally valid when the source register rs1 is not x0; encodings with rs1 = x0 are defined as the C.EBREAK instruction.
RULE 3: C.JALR must compute the link address by adding an offset of 2 bytes to the current pc, not the 4-byte offset used by the base-ISA jalr instruction.
RULE 4: C.JALR is defined only when the three most-significant bits (inst[15:13]) of the 16-bit instruction encoding are binary 100.
RULE 5: C.JALR is defined only when the bit immediately following those three most-significant bits in the 16-bit encoding is 1.
RULE 6: C.JALR is defined only when the register source field rs1 encoded in the instruction is not zero.
RULE 7: C.JALR is defined only when the bit that follows the rs1 field in the 16-bit encoding is 0.
RULE 8: C.JALR is defined only when the two least-significant bits (inst[1:0]) of the 16-bit encoding are binary 10.
RULE 9: Any 16-bit encoding that does not satisfy all of the above conditions is not a valid C.JALR encoding and is reserved.
RULE 10: C.JALR MUST perform an unconditional control transfer to the address contained in register rs1.
RULE 11: C.JALR MUST write the address of the instruction following the jump (pc+2) into the link register x1.
RULE 12: If the Zicfilp extension is enabled and a C.JALR instruction executes with rs1 not equal to x1, x5, or x7, then the hart MUST update the ELP to LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 13: A software guarded branch using C.JALR does not need to land on a LPAD instruction and therefore does not set the ELP to LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 14: For any C.JALR instruction the jump target is taken from the value in the specified rs1 register and is not encoded in the instruction opcode.
RULE 15: The CTRCTL[47:32] transfer-type filter bits may be used to record or omit recording of C.JALR control transfers of type 8 (Indirect call) and type 12 (Co-routine swap).
RULE 16: When all transfer-type filter bits are unimplemented or set to 0, a C.JALR control transfer is recorded in enabled privileged modes while non-taken branches are not recorded.
RULE 17: After completion of C.JALR, a trap may be delivered to the same or a higher privilege mode before the target instruction is decoded due to an asynchronous interrupt.
RULE 18: After completion of C.JALR, a trap may be delivered to the same or a higher privilege mode before the target instruction is decoded due to a synchronous exception whose priority exceeds that of a software-check exception with xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 19: When a software-check exception caused by Zicfilp occurs in conjunction with C.JALR, its priority is higher than that of an illegal-instruction exception and lower than that of an instruction access-fault.
RULE 20: When a software-check exception due to the executing instruction not being an LPAD instruction while ELP is LP_EXPECTED occurs during C.JALR, the resulting trap is delivered to the same or a higher privilege mode.
RULE 21: When a software-check exception caused by the LPAD instruction itself occurs during C.JALR, the resulting trap is delivered to the same or a higher privilege mode.
RULE 22: When a trap is taken into privilege mode x as a consequence of C.JALR, the x PELP bit is set to the previous value of ELP and ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.

INSTRUCTION: C.JR
FORMAT: c.jr rs1
RULE 1: C.JR performs an unconditional control transfer to the address contained in register rs1.
RULE 2: C.JR expands to the base instruction jalr x0, 0(rs1).
RULE 3: C.JR is only defined when rs1 is not x0; encodings with rs1 equal to x0 are reserved.
RULE 4: The rs1 field of C.JR must be zero.
RULE 5: C.JR must set the program counter to the address contained in register rs1, performing an unconditional control transfer.
RULE 6: C.JR x5 must set the program counter to the value contained in register x5, which is the return address.
RULE 7: C.JR is defined as an indirect jump without linkage when its rs1 operand is neither x1 nor x5.
RULE 8: C.JR is defined as a function return when its rs1 operand is either x1 or x5.
RULE 9: When a trap that may be taken after a C.JR is entered into privilege mode x, the x PELP bit must be set to ELP and the ELP field must be set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.

INSTRUCTION: C.LBU
FORMAT: c.lbu rd, c_uimm2(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LBU computes the effective memory address by adding the unsigned immediate (uimm) to the value in source register rs1′.
RULE 2: C.LBU loads a single byte from memory at the effective address.
RULE 3: C.LBU zero-extends the loaded byte to the XLEN width of the executing hart.
RULE 4: C.LBU writes the zero-extended result to destination register rd′.
RULE 5: In a C.LBU encoding, bits other than 15–10, 9, 6, 4, and 1 are reserved and their values do not affect architectural behavior.
RULE 6: C.LBU forms its immediate uimm by setting bits 31:2 to zero, assigning bit 1 from instruction bit 5, assigning bit 0 from instruction bit 6, and then zero-extending uimm to XLEN bits for address calculation.
RULE 7: C.LBU adds the zero-extended immediate uimm to the value in source register rs1' (which is one of x8-x15) to compute the effective memory address.
RULE 8: C.LBU reads an 8-bit byte from memory at the effective address.
RULE 9: C.LBU zero-extends the loaded byte to XLEN bits before any further processing.
RULE 10: C.LBU writes the zero-extended byte to destination register rd' (which is one of x8-x15).
RULE 11: In the abstract operation of C.LBU, the value written to rd' equals EXTZ(mem[X(rs1c) + EXTZ(uimm)][7..0]).
RULE 12: C.LBU uses rd' and rs1' from the standard compressed register set x8-x15.

INSTRUCTION: C.LD
FORMAT: c.ld rd, c_uimm8(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LD loads a 64-bit value from memory into the destination register rd′.
RULE 2: C.LD computes its effective address by adding to the base address in register rs1′ the zero-extended offset multiplied by 8.
RULE 3: C.LD has the same architectural effect as the 64-bit load instruction ld rd′, offset(rs1′).
RULE 4: C.LD is decoded only when a 16-bit instruction in quadrant 0 has inst[15:13]=011, inst[1:0]=00, and the remaining bits interpreted as rd′, rs1′, uimm[5:3], and uimm[7:6].
RULE 5: C.LD uses rs1′ as the base register containing the address to which the unsigned immediate offset is added.
RULE 6: C.LD forms its unsigned immediate offset by concatenating uimm[7:6] as the high bits with uimm[5:3] as the low bits, producing a zero-extended offset.
RULE 7: C.LD computes the effective memory address by adding the zero-extended offset to the value held in rs1′.
RULE 8: C.LD loads a doubleword from memory at the effective address and writes the loaded value into the destination register rd′.
RULE 9: C.LD is defined only for RV64 implementations and has no defined behavior on RV32 implementations.
RULE 10: C.LD reads a doubleword from the memory location whose address equals the current value in register sp.
RULE 11: C.LD writes the doubleword read from memory into register x5.
RULE 12: C.LD must load eight contiguous bytes from the memory address formed by adding the sign-extended offset to the value in source register rs1' and must place bits [31:0] of the loaded doubleword into destination register rd' and bits [63:32] into register rd'+1.
RULE 13: C.LD must be treated as an illegal instruction on RV64 implementations.
RULE 14: C.LD must be available only when the Zclsd extension is implemented, and the Zclsd extension requires that both the Zilsd and Zca extensions also be implemented.
RULE 15: C.LD must specify both its destination register and its base register using registers x8 through x15.
RULE 16: C.LD loads a 64-bit value from memory at the computed effective address into the destination register pair rd' (containing the low-order word) and rd'+1 (containing the high-order word).
RULE 17: C.LD computes its effective address by adding the contents of base register rs1' to the zero-extended offset field multiplied by 8.
RULE 18: C.LD treats the offset field as an unsigned value that is zero-extended before being scaled by 8 for address calculation.
RULE 19: A processor that implements the Zclsd extension must provide the C.LD instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.LDSP
FORMAT: c.ldsp rd, c_uimm9(sp)
RULE 1: C.LDSP is an RV64C-only instruction that loads a 64-bit value from memory into the destination register rd.
RULE 2: C.LDSP computes its effective address by adding the stack pointer x2 to a zero-extended immediate offset multiplied by eight.
RULE 3: C.LDSP has the same architectural effect as executing an ld rd, offset(x2) instruction with the same offset.
RULE 4: C.LDSP is valid only when the destination register rd is not x0.
RULE 5: Encodings of C.LDSP where rd = x0 are reserved and have no defined architectural effect.
RULE 6: C.LDSP MUST have the funct3 field encoded as binary 011.
RULE 7: C.LDSP encodes the high-order immediate bit in the uimm[5] position of the instruction.
RULE 8: C.LDSP encodes the low-order immediate bits in the uimm[4:3|8:6] positions of the instruction.
RULE 9: C.LDSP requires the destination register rd to be non-zero; an encoding with rd = 0 is reserved.
RULE 10: C.LDSP belongs to the RVC opcode map quadrant where inst[15:13] equals binary 10.
RULE 11: C.LDSP computes its effective memory address by sign-extending the instruction's offset field and adding the result to the current value of the stack pointer.
RULE 12: C.LDSP loads an 8-byte doubleword from the effective memory address into a register pair, placing the low-order 32 bits into the destination register rd and the high-order 32 bits into the next register rd+1.
RULE 13: For C.LDSP, using register x0 as the destination register is reserved.
RULE 14: C.LDSP loads a 64-bit value from memory into the register pair consisting of rd′ and rd′+1, where rd′ is the destination register encoded in the instruction.
RULE 15: C.LDSP computes its effective address by zero-extending the immediate offset, multiplying the result by eight, and adding that value to the stack pointer register x2.
RULE 16: C.LDSP produces the same architectural effect as executing the standard instruction ld rd, offset(x2) with the same offset, loading the identical 64-bit value from the same address.
RULE 17: C.LDSP is only valid when the encoded destination register rd is not x0; any encoding that sets rd = x0 is reserved and its behavior is undefined.
RULE 18: C.LDSP, when executed as a CPU instruction, MUST apply the pointer-masking transformation to its effective memory address as defined for explicit memory accesses.
RULE 19: When the MXR flag is set in the effective privilege mode at the time C.LDSP accesses memory, the pointer-masking transformation MUST NOT be applied to the effective address.
RULE 20: When MPRV or SPVP are set, the pointer-masking settings of the effective privilege mode MUST be applied to the effective address used by C.LDSP.
RULE 21: C.LDSP must support misaligned memory accesses, and the architectural effect of a misaligned access MUST be identical to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each constituent aligned access that would result from splitting the misaligned operation.
RULE 22: Pointer-masking MUST only be applied to memory accesses generated by CPU instructions such as C.LDSP, and MUST NOT be applied when the access is performed by hardware CSR writes or DMA devices.

INSTRUCTION: C.LH
FORMAT: c.lh rd, c_uimm1(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LH loads a signed halfword from the memory address formed by adding the unsigned immediate offset to the value in source register rs1', sign-extends the 16-bit data, and writes the result to destination register rd'.
RULE 2: C.LH does not introduce any additional architectural permission checks or exception behavior beyond those defined for standard load instructions.
RULE 3: C.LH forms the immediate uimm by setting bits 31-2 to zero, bit 1 to the value of encoding bit 5, and bit 0 to zero, and then zero-extends this value to XLEN bits.
RULE 4: C.LH computes the effective memory address by adding the zero-extended immediate uimm to the current value of register rs1'.
RULE 5: C.LH reads a 16-bit halfword from memory at the effective address.
RULE 6: C.LH sign-extends the 16-bit halfword loaded from memory to the processor's XLEN width.
RULE 7: C.LH writes the sign-extended XLEN-wide result into destination register rd'.
RULE 8: C.LH uses rd' and rs1' registers that are selected from the compressed register set x8-x15.

INSTRUCTION: C.LHU
FORMAT: c.lhu rd, c_uimm1(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LHU loads an unsigned halfword from the memory address formed by adding the unsigned immediate to the value in rs1' and writes the zero-extended result to rd'.
RULE 2: C.LHU forms its unsigned immediate uimm such that uimm[31:2] are zero, uimm[1] equals the instruction's bit 5, and uimm[0] is zero.
RULE 3: C.LHU computes the effective address by adding the zero-extended uimm to the value in rs1', where rs1' must be one of registers x8-x15.
RULE 4: C.LHU loads a 16-bit halfword from memory at the effective address computed from rs1' plus uimm.
RULE 5: C.LHU zero-extends the loaded 16-bit halfword to XLEN bits and writes the result to rd', where rd' must be one of registers x8-x15.
RULE 6: C.LHU has no architectural prerequisites and may be executed in any processor state.

INSTRUCTION: C.LI
FORMAT: c.li rd, c_imm6
RULE 1: Execution of C.LI writes the sign-extended 6-bit immediate imm into the architectural register identified by rd.
RULE 2: The architectural effect of C.LI is defined to be identical to the effect of the 32-bit instruction addi rd, x0, imm.
RULE 3: When the destination register field rd of C.LI is x0, the instruction is defined as a HINT and MUST NOT produce any architectural state change.
RULE 4: C.LI MUST have its destination register field set to x0.
RULE 5: Execution of C.LI MUST NOT modify any architectural state other than advancing the program counter and updating any applicable performance counters.
RULE 6: An implementation may ignore C.LI as a hint and treat it as a no-op, performing only the required program-counter advancement and performance-counter update.
RULE 7: C.LI has no defined operational effect such as loading a value into a register, and the specification provides no architectural state change for the instruction.

INSTRUCTION: C.LUI
FORMAT: c.lui rd, c_nzimm18
RULE 1: C.LUI loads the non-zero 6-bit immediate field into bits 17-12 of the destination register, clears the lower 12 bits, and sign-extends bit 17 into all higher bits of the destination register.
RULE 2: C.LUI encodings whose immediate field equals zero are reserved and have no defined architectural effect.
RULE 3: C.LUI encodings with destination register x2 and a non-zero immediate are interpreted as the C.ADDI16SP instruction rather than as C.LUI.
RULE 4: C.LUI encodings with destination register x0 and a non-zero immediate are defined as hint instructions that produce no architectural state change.
RULE 5: C.LUI execution must not modify any architectural registers, memory, or other architectural state.
RULE 6: C.LUI execution must advance the program counter to the address of the following instruction.
RULE 7: C.LUI encodings must have the destination register field rd equal to x0.
RULE 8: C.LUI encodings must have an immediate field that is not zero.
RULE 9: C.LUI MUST have its destination register rd distinct from registers x0 and x2.
RULE 10: C.LUI is a reserved instruction when its immediate field imm is zero, and such an encoding is reserved for future standard extensions.
RULE 11: C.LUI is treated as a hint when its destination register rd is register x0.
RULE 12: C.LUI MUST be implemented as a compressed instruction.
RULE 13: C.LUI MUST be supported in both the RV32 and RV64 base ISA variants.

INSTRUCTION: C.LW
FORMAT: c.lw rd, c_uimm7(rs1)
RULE 1: C.LW must load a 32-bit value from memory into register rd′.
RULE 2: C.LW must compute its effective address by adding the value in register rs1′ to four times the zero-extended offset field encoded in the instruction.
RULE 3: C.LW must have the same architectural effect as the 32-bit lw instruction with the computed offset and base register rs1′ (i.e., lw rd′, offset(rs1′)).
RULE 4: C.LW MUST be a 16-bit compressed instruction that resides in quadrant 0, meaning its two least-significant opcode bits are 00.
RULE 5: C.LW MUST have its primary opcode field bits [15:13] equal to the binary pattern 010.
RULE 6: C.LW MUST encode the low-order offset bits by concatenating uimm[5:3] from bits [12:10] with uimm[2|6] taken from bits [6] and [5].
RULE 7: C.LW MUST use the rs1′ field encoded in bits [9:7] as the base register for address calculation.
RULE 8: C.LW MUST use the rd′ field encoded in bits [4:2] as the destination register that receives the loaded word.
RULE 9: C.LW MUST be defined and non-reserved for the base ISA in both RV32 and RV64 implementations.
RULE 10: C.LW MUST have no additional operand restrictions beyond those implicit in its encoding fields.

INSTRUCTION: C.LWSP
FORMAT: c.lwsp rd, c_uimm8(sp)
RULE 1: C.LWSP loads a 32-bit value from memory into the destination register rd.
RULE 2: C.LWSP computes its effective address by adding to the stack pointer x2 a zero-extended offset that is multiplied by 4.
RULE 3: C.LWSP has the same architectural effect as executing the 32-bit load instruction lw rd, offset(x2).
RULE 4: C.LWSP is decoded when the instruction bits 15-13 equal 010 and bits 1-0 equal 10, identifying the instruction as belonging to quadrant 2 of the RVC opcode map.
RULE 5: C.LWSP encodes a destination register field rd that must satisfy rd ≠ 0.
RULE 6: When the rd field of a C.LWSP encoding equals zero, the encoding is reserved for future extensions and does not define a C.LWSP operation.
RULE 7: In a C.LWSP encoding, the bit named uimm[5] provides the most-significant bit of the unsigned immediate used by the instruction.
RULE 8: In a C.LWSP encoding, the bits named uimm[4:2] and uimm[7:6] together supply the remaining lower bits of the unsigned immediate according to RVC conventions.
RULE 9: The unsigned immediate for C.LWSP is formed by concatenating uimm[5] as the high bit with the lower bits supplied by uimm[4:2] and uimm[7:6] in the order defined by the RVC encoding.
RULE 10: C.LWSP is defined as a valid instruction for both RV32 and RV64 implementations.
RULE 11: The effective address used by C.LWSP MUST be masked according to the pointer-masking rules that apply to all explicit memory accesses.
RULE 12: C.LWSP MUST perform the same pointer-masking transformation and permission checks as the base LW instruction.
RULE 13: C.LWSP MUST NOT introduce any additional permission checks, exceptions, or behavioral rules beyond those required for explicit memory accesses subject to pointer masking.

INSTRUCTION: C.MOP.1
FORMAT: c.mop.1
RULE 1: C.MOP.1 must not write any architectural register.
RULE 2: C.MOP.1 must not read any architectural register in the current definition.
RULE 3: C.MOP.1 may be defined by future extensions to read register x1, but such a read is not present in the current definition.
RULE 4: The store performed by C.MOP.1 is identical to existing store instructions except that the base address is implicitly the shadow-stack pointer (ssp).
