RULE 2: FCVTMOD.W.D takes the lower 32 bits of the rounded, unbounded two's-complement result, sign-extends them to XLEN bits, and writes the value to integer register rd.
RULE 3: FCVTMOD.W.D converts a source operand of positive or negative infinity to an integer result of zero.
RULE 4: FCVTMOD.W.D converts a source operand that is NaN to an integer result of zero.
RULE 5: FCVTMOD.W.D raises floating-point exception flags exactly as FCVT.W.D would raise for the same input operand.
RULE 6: FCVTMOD.W.D encoding requires the rs2 field to be 8 and the rm field to be 1 (RTZ), and any other rm value makes the encoding reserved.

INSTRUCTION: FDIV.Q
FORMAT: fdiv.q fd, fs1, fs2, rm
RULE 1: FDIV.Q MUST perform a floating-point division of the quad-precision value in source register rs1 by the quad-precision value in source register rs2, using the rounding mode specified by its RM field, and write the quad-precision result to destination register rd.
RULE 2: FDIV.Q operates on 128-bit quad-precision operands and produces a 128-bit quad-precision result.

INSTRUCTION: FDIV.S
FORMAT: fdiv.s fd, fs1, fs2, rm
RULE 1: FDIV.S MUST be encoded using the R-type instruction format with the OPFP major opcode.
RULE 2: FDIV.S reads the floating-point register identified by the rs1 field as its first operand.
RULE 3: FDIV.S reads the floating-point register identified by the rs2 field as its second operand.
RULE 4: FDIV.S writes the result of the operation to the floating-point register identified by the rd field.
RULE 5: FDIV.S performs a single-precision floating-point division of the value in rs1 by the value in rs2.
RULE 6: FDIV.S sets the 2-bit fmt field to the value S (binary 00) to indicate a 32-bit single-precision operand, as required for all instructions in the F extension.
RULE 7: FDIV.S may use the rm field to select the rounding mode applied to the division result.
RULE 8: When FDIV.S uses the rm field, the selected rounding mode governs how the division result is rounded in accordance with the rounding-mode semantics of the F extension.

INSTRUCTION: FENCE
FORMAT: fence pred, succ
RULE 1: An implementation may implement the FENCE instruction as a no-operation that has no architectural effect.
RULE 2: FENCE instructions order device input, device output, memory read, and memory write operations as observed by other RISC-V harts and external devices or coprocessors.
RULE 3: For a FENCE, no other RISC-V hart or external device may observe any operation belonging to the successor set before any operation belonging to the predecessor set that precedes the FENCE.
RULE 4: FENCE instructions also order memory reads and writes performed by the hart as observed by memory reads and writes performed by an external device.
RULE 5: FENCE instructions do not order observations of external-device events made by any signaling mechanism other than memory accesses, and therefore they provide no guarantee about the visibility timing of interrupt signal changes to an interrupt controller.
RULE 6: A FENCE with fm = 0000 (normal fence) orders all memory operations in its predecessor set before all memory operations in its successor set.
RULE 7: A FENCE.TSO orders all load operations in its predecessor set before all memory operations in its successor set.
RULE 8: A FENCE.TSO orders all store operations in its predecessor set before all store operations in its successor set.
RULE 9: A FENCE.TSO does not order non-AMO store operations in its predecessor set with non-AMO load operations in its successor set.
RULE 10: Because a FENCE RW,RW imposes a superset of the orderings required by a FENCE.TSO, a base implementation may treat a FENCE.TSO as a FENCE RW,RW and ignore the fm field.
RULE 11: The rs1 and rd fields of a FENCE are reserved for finer-grain fences in future extensions, and base implementations shall ignore these fields while standard software shall set them to zero.
RULE 12: Configurations where the fm field or the predecessor or successor sets are reserved shall be treated by base implementations as a normal FENCE with fm = 0000, and standard software shall use only non-reserved configurations.
RULE 13: A simple implementation may ignore the predecessor and successor fields of a FENCE and always execute a conservative ordering that applies to all operations.
RULE 14: Because the predecessor and successor sets of a HINT-encoded FENCE intersect only in the null set, the instruction imposes no memory ordering and therefore has no architecturally visible effect.
RULE 15: A hart MUST not observe stores to instruction memory via instruction fetches until it has executed a FENCE.I instruction.
RULE 16: A FENCE.I instruction orders all explicit memory accesses that precede it in program order before any instruction fetches that follow it in program order.
RULE 17: After a FENCE.I instruction, any subsequent instruction fetch on the same hart must see any data stores that were already visible to that hart prior to the fence.
RULE 18: A FENCE.I instruction does NOT guarantee that instruction fetches on other harts will observe stores performed by the local hart.
RULE 19: To make a store to instruction memory visible to all harts, the writing hart MUST execute a data FENCE before causing remote harts to execute a FENCE.I.
RULE 20: The ordering enforced by a FENCE.I ensures that an instruction fetch is always ordered before any explicit memory accesses generated by the fetched instruction.
RULE 21: The unused fields (funct12, rs1, rd) in a FENCE.I instruction are reserved for finer-grain fences, base implementations MUST ignore them, and standard software MUST zero them.
RULE 22: In the base RISC-V ISA, the FENCE instruction imposes additional ordering constraints on accesses because the memory model is relaxed.
RULE 23: The FENCE instruction provides options to order accesses to the memory domain, the I/O domain, or both domains as defined by the execution environment.
RULE 24: The FENCE instruction does not imply any ordering constraint on accesses to a domain that is not selected in its ordering options.
RULE 25: A FENCE instruction orders a before b.
RULE 26: When a FENCE instruction orders a before b, the architecture must ensure that a precedes b in the preserved program order, and therefore also precedes b in the global memory order.
RULE 27: A memory write to the jump vector table MUST be followed by a FENCE.i instruction before any subsequent instruction fetch is guaranteed to observe the updated table entry.
RULE 28: If no memory writes to the jump vector table have occurred since the last executed FENCE.i, then switching the jump vector table does NOT require an additional FENCE.i to guarantee visibility to the instruction fetch.
RULE 29: When the FIOM bit in the menvcfg CSR is set to one, a FENCE executed in any privilege mode less privileged than Machine MUST treat a requirement to order device I/O accesses as also requiring ordering of main memory accesses.
RULE 30: When FIOM=1, if a FENCE executed in a privilege mode less privileged than Machine has the PI bit set, it MUST order predecessor device input operations and also order predecessor memory reads.
RULE 31: When FIOM=1, if a FENCE executed in a privilege mode less privileged than Machine has the PO bit set, it MUST order predecessor device output operations and also order predecessor memory writes.
RULE 32: When FIOM=1, if a FENCE executed in a privilege mode less privileged than Machine has the SI bit set, it MUST order successor device input operations and also order successor memory reads.
RULE 33: When FIOM=1, if a FENCE executed in a privilege mode less privileged than Machine has the SO bit set, it MUST order successor device output operations and also order successor memory writes.
RULE 34: If the FIOM bit in senvcfg is set to one and a FENCE instruction is executed in U-mode, the ordering requirements of that FENCE are modified so that ordering device I/O accesses also requires ordering main memory accesses.
RULE 35: When FIOM=1 in senvcfg and a FENCE executed in U-mode has the PI bit set, the instruction must ensure that all predecessor device input accesses and all predecessor main memory reads are completed before any later memory or I/O operations.
RULE 36: When FIOM=1 in senvcfg and a FENCE executed in U-mode has the PO bit set, the instruction must ensure that all predecessor device output accesses and all predecessor main memory writes are completed before any later memory or I/O operations.
RULE 37: When FIOM=1 in senvcfg and a FENCE executed in U-mode has the SI bit set, the instruction must ensure that all successor device input accesses and all successor main memory reads occur after all earlier memory or I/O operations.
RULE 38: When FIOM=1 in senvcfg and a FENCE executed in U-mode has the SO bit set, the instruction must ensure that all successor device output accesses and all successor main memory writes occur after all earlier memory or I/O operations.
RULE 39: A FENCE instruction MUST order the page-table-entry (PTE) updates associated with the loads and stores it orders, and this ordering is observed by remote harts.
RULE 40: PTE updates that arise from memory accesses ordered after a FENCE are NOT ordered by that FENCE.
RULE 41: For a page whose underlying physical memory attribute is I/O and whose PBMT field is NC, all accesses to that page must be treated as both I/O accesses and main-memory accesses for the purposes of FENCE ordering.
RULE 42: For a page whose underlying physical memory attribute is main memory and whose PBMT field is IO, all accesses to that page must be treated as both I/O accesses and main-memory accesses for the purposes of FENCE ordering.
RULE 43: If a fence iorw,iow instruction is executed between such accesses, then loss of memory ordering must not occur.
RULE 44: If the sequence fence iorw,iow, followed by a cbo.flush to the address of the location, followed by another fence iorw,iow is executed between such accesses, then both loss of coherence and loss of memory ordering must be prevented.
RULE 45: If the same location might later be referenced again using its original attributes, then the fence iorw,iow -> cbo.flush -> fence iorw,iow sequence must be repeated beforehand.
RULE 46: If the FIOM bit in the henvcfg CSR is 1 and the current virtualization mode V is 1, then a FENCE instruction executed must treat the requirement to order device I/O accesses as also requiring ordering of main-memory accesses.
RULE 47: When FIOM=1 and V=1, setting the PI bit in a FENCE instruction must cause the predecessor set to include both device input operations and memory reads (PR).
RULE 48: When FIOM=1 and V=1, setting the PO bit in a FENCE instruction must cause the predecessor set to include both device output operations and memory writes (PW).
RULE 49: When FIOM=1 and V=1, setting the SI bit in a FENCE instruction must cause the successor set to include both device input operations and memory reads (SR).
RULE 50: When FIOM=1 and V=1, setting the SO bit in a FENCE instruction must cause the successor set to include both device output operations and memory writes (SW).

INSTRUCTION: FENCE.I
FORMAT: fence.i
RULE 1: Stores to instruction memory are not guaranteed to become visible to instruction fetches on a hart until that hart executes a FENCE.I.
RULE 2: After a hart executes a FENCE.I, any instruction fetch that occurs later in program order on that hart will see all data stores to instruction memory that were already visible to the hart before the FENCE.I.
RULE 3: A FENCE.I orders all explicit memory accesses that precede it in program order to complete before any instruction fetches that follow it in program order.
RULE 4: A FENCE.I does not guarantee that instruction fetches on other harts will observe the local hart's stores to instruction memory.
RULE 5: To make a store to instruction memory observable by all harts, the storing hart must execute a data FENCE before requesting remote harts to execute a FENCE.I.
RULE 6: The funct12, rs1, and rd fields of a FENCE.I encoding are reserved; base implementations shall ignore these fields.
RULE 7: A FENCE.I only synchronizes the local hart, so application code must rely on its effect only while the executing thread remains on the same hart.
RULE 8: FENCE.I orders stores, guaranteeing that the completion of all store instructions preceding the FENCE.I is observed before any memory operation that follows the FENCE.I.
RULE 9: After any memory write to the jump vector table, a FENCE.I instruction must be executed before any instruction fetch can be guaranteed to observe the updated table entries.
RULE 10: If no memory writes to the jump vector table have occurred since the most recent FENCE.I, then switching the jump vector table to another table may be performed without executing an additional FENCE.I.

INSTRUCTION: FEQ.S
FORMAT: feq.s rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FEQ.S must compare the values in the two specified single-precision floating-point source registers for equality.
RULE 2: FEQ.S must write the integer value 1 to the integer destination register rd when the two compared operands are equal, and must write the integer value 0 to rd when they are not equal.
RULE 3: FEQ.S must set the floating-point invalid-operation exception flag only if at least one of its source operands is a signaling NaN.
RULE 4: FEQ.S must write the integer value 0 to rd when either source operand is NaN, regardless of whether the NaN is signaling or quiet.

INSTRUCTION: FLD
FORMAT: fld fd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: The memory operations generated by an FLD are ordered normally with respect to memory operations generated by preceding and subsequent instructions in program order.
RULE 2: FLD MUST load a double-precision floating-point value from the memory location specified by its effective address into the floating-point destination register rd.
RULE 3: FLD MAY load a double-precision value that is a NaN-boxed representation of a single-precision floating-point value.
RULE 4: FLD is guaranteed to execute atomically only when the effective address is naturally aligned and the implementation's XLEN is at least 64 bits; otherwise atomic execution is not guaranteed.
RULE 5: FLD transfers the bits from memory to the destination register without modification, thereby preserving the payload of non-canonical NaNs.
RULE 6: When the D-extension FLD would access a floating-point register f rd, the Zdinx FLD instead accesses the integer register x rd with the same register number.
RULE 7: The FLD instruction loads a double-precision floating-point value from the effective memory address into the integer register x rd whose number equals the floating-point destination register number defined for the D-extension FLD.
RULE 8: FLD loads an 8-byte double-precision floating-point value from the memory address computed as its effective address into the floating-point register rd'.
RULE 9: FLD computes its effective address by zero-extending the offset, multiplying the result by 8, and adding it to the base address contained in register rs1'.
RULE 10: The compressed instruction C.FLD expands to FLD rd', offset(rs1').
RULE 11: The Zca extension excludes the FLD instruction c.fld, so the instruction is not available when Zca is implemented.
RULE 12: The FLD instruction c.fld is only present when both the D and F extensions are implemented, because the C extension includes F/D instructions only when D and F are specified.
RULE 13: FLD must have the width field bits [2:0] equal to 0 1 1.
RULE 14: FLD must read a memory element of exactly 64 bits (Membits = 64).
RULE 15: FLD must write the loaded value into a floating-point register element whose size is FLEN.
RULE 16: When a synchronous exception is caused by executing FLD, the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) may be written with a standard transformation of FLD.
RULE 17: In the transformed instruction for FLD, the funct3, rd, and opcode fields are the same as in the original FLD instruction.
RULE 18: The Addr. Offset field in the transformed instruction for FLD replaces the rs1 field bits 19:15 and contains the positive difference between the faulting virtual address written to mtval or stval and the original virtual address, and this difference can be non-zero only for a misaligned memory access.
RULE 19: In the transformed instruction for FLD, the immediate offset fields are replaced with zero.
RULE 20: Because FLD is not a compressed instruction, bits 1:0 of its transformed standard instruction encoding are binary 11.
RULE 21: FLD must have pointer masking applied to its effective address when pointer masking is enabled for the privilege mode that performs the FLD access.
RULE 22: When MPRV or SPVP is in effect, FLD must use the pointer-masking settings of the effective privilege mode to transform its address.
RULE 23: When MXR is in effect at the effective privilege mode where FLD performs its explicit memory access, pointer masking must not be applied to FLD's address.
RULE 24: FLD must support misaligned memory accesses, subject to the same limitations that apply when pointer masking is not present.
RULE 25: For a misaligned FLD address, the architectural effect must be identical to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each constituent aligned memory access that composes the misaligned operation.
RULE 26: FLD is not exempt from pointer masking by the CSR read/write exemption; pointer masking applies to FLD even though CSR accesses are exempt.
RULE 27: If software stores a tagged address into a CSR and later uses that CSR value as the address for an FLD operation, the FLD address must be subject to pointer masking only if pointer masking is enabled for the privilege mode performing the FLD.
RULE 28: FLD must observe the pointer-masking transformation of its address according to the current privilege-mode settings, including the effects of MPRV, SPVP, and MXR.

INSTRUCTION: FLE.S
FORMAT: fle.s rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FLE.S MUST write the integer value 1 to rd if the single-precision floating-point value in rs1 is less than or equal to the value in rs2, and MUST write the integer value 0 to rd otherwise.
RULE 2: FLE.S MUST set the invalid-operation exception flag if either rs1 or rs2 contains a NaN.
RULE 3: FLE.S MUST write the integer value 0 to rd if either rs1 or rs2 contains a NaN, irrespective of the comparison result.
RULE 4: When the FLE.S instruction is executed with a quiet NaN operand, it must set the invalid operation exception flag.

INSTRUCTION: FLEQ.D
FORMAT: fleq.d rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: If the D extension is implemented, the FLEQ.D instruction MUST operate on double-precision floating-point operands.
RULE 2: The FLEQ.D instruction MUST produce the same comparison result as the FLE.D instruction for all operand values.
RULE 3: If either source operand of FLEQ.D is a quiet NaN, the invalid-operation exception flag MUST NOT be set.

INSTRUCTION: FLEQ.H
FORMAT: fleq.h rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: The FLEQ.H instruction performs a half-precision floating-point less-than-or-equal comparison whose result is defined identically to the result of the FLE.H instruction.
RULE 2: When a quiet NaN is supplied as either source operand to the FLEQ.H instruction, the invalid-operation exception flag must not be set.

INSTRUCTION: FLEQ.Q
FORMAT: fleq.q rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FLEQ.Q must produce the same Boolean comparison result as the FLE.S instruction for the same pair of operands.
RULE 2: FLEQ.Q must not set the invalid operation exception flag when either source operand is a quiet NaN.
RULE 3: In an implementation that provides the Q extension, FLEQ.Q operates on quad-precision floating-point operands.

INSTRUCTION: FLEQ.S
FORMAT: fleq.s rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: For any pair of floating-point operands that are not quiet NaNs, the FLEQ.S instruction MUST produce the same comparison result and raise the same exception flags as the FLE.S instruction.
RULE 2: When either source operand of the FLEQ.S instruction is a quiet NaN, the instruction MUST NOT set the invalid operation exception flag.

INSTRUCTION: FLH
FORMAT: flh fd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: FLH must transfer the loaded 16-bit value without altering any of its bits, and must preserve the payload bits of non-canonical NaNs present in the memory source.
RULE 2: FLH must NaN-box the value written to the destination floating-point register, placing the loaded halfword into the low bits and setting the remaining upper bits according to the NaN-boxing rule.
RULE 3: FLH must read a 16-bit half-precision floating-point element from the effective memory address.
RULE 4: FLH must place the value read from memory into the destination floating-point register, converting the 16-bit half-precision representation to the register's FLEN format.
RULE 5: If a synchronous exception is caused by an FLH instruction due to load address-misaligned, load access-fault, load page-fault, or load guest-page-fault, then hardware may write a transformed instruction to the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst).
RULE 6: In the transformed instruction generated for an FLH exception, the funct3, rd, and opcode fields must be identical to those of the trapping FLH instruction.
RULE 7: The Addr. Offset field (bits 19:15) in the transformed FLH instruction must contain the positive difference between the faulting virtual address recorded in mtval or stval and the original virtual address, and this field is non-zero only when the exception is due to a misaligned memory access.
RULE 8: When an FLH instruction is transformed for a trap, the immediate offset fields at bits 31:25, 24:20, and 11:7 of the transformed instruction must be set to zero.
RULE 9: The bits 1:0 of a transformed FLH instruction must be binary 11.
RULE 10: A trap-instruction register may contain a transformed FLH instruction for any of the following exception causes: load address misaligned, load access fault, load page fault, or load guest-page fault.

INSTRUCTION: FLI.D
FORMAT: fli.d fd, rs1
RULE 1: If the D extension is implemented, executing the FLI.D instruction loads a double-precision floating-point value into the destination floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: The double-precision value loaded by FLI.D for entry 1 (the minimum positive normal value) is numerically different from the value loaded by the corresponding single-precision entry of FLI.S.
RULE 3: The FLI.D instruction never sets any floating-point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FLI.H
FORMAT: fli.h fd, rs1
RULE 1: The FLI.H instruction is defined only if the Zfh or Zvfh extension is implemented.
RULE 2: Execution of FLI.H uses the rs1 field to index a table of 32 half-precision constants and writes the selected constant into floating-point register rd.
RULE 3: In the FLI.H constant table, entry 1 provides the minimum positive normal half-precision value, which is numerically different from the value provided by entry 1 of the FLI.S table.
RULE 4: In the FLI.H constant table, entry 29 loads positive infinity.
RULE 5: In the FLI.H constant table, entry 30 also loads positive infinity, making entry 29 redundant.
RULE 6: For FLI.H, the constant produced by entry 1 is numerically greater than the constants produced by entries 2 and 3 because 2^-16 and 2^-15 are subnormal in half-precision.
RULE 7: Execution of FLI.H never sets any floating-point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FLI.Q
FORMAT: fli.q fd, rs1
RULE 1: Execution of FLI.Q loads a quad-precision floating-point value into the destination floating-point register rd, performing the analogous operation to FLI.S but with quad precision.
RULE 2: For FLI.Q, immediate-value entry 1 represents the minimum positive normal quad-precision value, which is numerically different from the entry 1 value of other formats.
RULE 3: An instruction whose encoding matches the FLI.S format with the fmt field set to Q is decoded as the FLI.Q instruction.
RULE 4: Execution of FLI.Q never sets any floating-point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FLI.S
FORMAT: fli.s fd, rs1
RULE 1: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 0, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value –1.0 (sign = 1, exponent = 01111111, significand = all zeros).
RULE 2: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 1, the floating-point register rd is written with the smallest positive normal single-precision value (sign = 0, exponent = 00000001, significand = all zeros).
RULE 3: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 2, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 1.0 × 2⁻¹⁶ (sign = 0, exponent = 01101111, significand = all zeros).
RULE 4: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 3, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 1.0 × 2⁻¹⁵ (sign = 0, exponent = 01110000, significand = all zeros).
RULE 5: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 4, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 1.0 × 2⁻⁸ (sign = 0, exponent = 01110111, significand = all zeros).
RULE 6: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 5, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 1.0 × 2⁻⁷ (sign = 0, exponent = 01111000, significand = all zeros).
RULE 7: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 6, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.0625 (2⁻⁴) (sign = 0, exponent = 01111011, significand = all zeros).
RULE 8: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 7, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.125 (2⁻³) (sign = 0, exponent = 01111100, significand = all zeros).
RULE 9: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 8, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.25 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111101, significand = all zeros).
RULE 10: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 9, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.3125 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111101, significand = 010…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 11: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 10, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.375 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111101, significand = 100…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 12: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 11, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.4375 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111101, significand = 110…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 13: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 12, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.5 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111110, significand = all zeros).
RULE 14: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 13, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.625 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111110, significand = 010…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 15: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 14, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.75 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111110, significand = 100…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 16: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 15, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 0.875 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111110, significand = 110…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 17: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 16, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 1.0 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111111, significand = all zeros).
RULE 18: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 17, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 1.25 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111111, significand = 010…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 19: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 18, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 1.5 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111111, significand = 100…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 20: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 19, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 1.75 (sign = 0, exponent = 01111111, significand = 110…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 21: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 20, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 2.0 (sign = 0, exponent = 10000000, significand = all zeros).
RULE 22: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 21, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 2.5 (sign = 0, exponent = 10000000, significand = 010…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 23: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 22, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 3.0 (sign = 0, exponent = 10000000, significand = 100…000, where only the most-significant two bits of the fraction are non-zero).
RULE 24: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 23, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 4.0 (sign = 0, exponent = 10000001, significand = all zeros).
RULE 25: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 24, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 8.0 (sign = 0, exponent = 10000010, significand = all zeros).
RULE 26: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 25, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 16.0 (sign = 0, exponent = 10000011, significand = all zeros).
RULE 27: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 26, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 128.0 (2⁷) (sign = 0, exponent = 10000110, significand = all zeros).
RULE 28: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 27, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 256.0 (2⁸) (sign = 0, exponent = 10000111, significand = all zeros).
RULE 29: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 28, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 2¹⁵ (sign = 0, exponent = 10001110, significand = all zeros).
RULE 30: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 29, the floating-point register rd is written with the single-precision value 2¹⁶ (sign = 0, exponent = 10001111, significand = all zeros).
RULE 31: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 30, the floating-point register rd is written with the positive infinity single-precision value (sign = 0, exponent = 11111111, significand = all zeros).
RULE 32: When the FLI.S instruction is executed with rs1 = 31, the floating-point register rd is written with the canonical NaN single-precision value (sign = 0, exponent = 11111111, significand = 100…000, where only the most-significant bit of the fraction is non-zero).
RULE 33: For rs1 values 8 through 22, the floating-point value loaded by FLI.S has only the two most-significant bits of the significand possibly non-zero and all other significand bits are zero.
RULE 34: Execution of the FLI.S instruction never sets any floating-point exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FLQ
FORMAT: flq fd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: FLQ must execute atomically only when its effective address is naturally aligned and the processor XLEN is 128; if either condition is not satisfied, atomic execution is not guaranteed.
RULE 2: FLQ must transfer the 128-bit floating-point value without modifying any of its bits, and must preserve the payloads of non-canonical NaNs present in the source.
RULE 3: FLQ accesses a memory element of exactly 128 bits.
RULE 4: FLQ places the loaded 128-bit value into a floating-point register whose width is FLEN bits.
RULE 5: FLQ must be encoded with the mew bit cleared to 0, and any encoding that sets the mew bit to 1 for a 128-bit load is reserved.
RULE 6: In a transformed FLQ instruction the funct3, rd, and opcode fields must be identical to those of the trapping FLQ instruction.
RULE 7: The Addr. Offset field occupying bits 19:15 of a transformed FLQ instruction must hold the positive difference between the faulting virtual address recorded in mtval or stval and the original virtual address.
RULE 8: The Addr. Offset field of a transformed FLQ instruction may be nonzero only when the original FLQ caused a misaligned memory access.
RULE 9: All immediate offset fields of a transformed FLQ instruction are set to zero by the transformation.
RULE 10: Bits 1:0 of a transformed FLQ instruction are binary 01 if the trapping FLQ was a compressed instruction and binary 11 if it was not compressed.
RULE 11: Pointer masking MUST be applied to the effective address used by FLQ because FLQ is an explicit memory-access instruction generated by the CPU.
RULE 12: The pointer-masking settings applied to FLQ are taken from the effective privilege mode in which FLQ executes.
RULE 13: When MXR is in effect in the effective privilege mode for a FLQ execution, pointer masking MUST NOT be applied to the address used by FLQ.
RULE 14: A misaligned FLQ access is performed as a series of aligned memory accesses, each transformed by pointer masking, and the overall operation is subject to the same limitations that apply to aligned accesses without pointer masking.

INSTRUCTION: FLT.S
FORMAT: flt.s rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FLT.S compares the single-precision floating-point values in registers rs1 and rs2 using the less-than relation.
RULE 2: FLT.S writes the integer value 1 to rd when the comparison rs1 < rs2 is true, and writes the integer value 0 to rd when the comparison is false.
RULE 3: FLT.S sets the floating-point invalid operation exception flag if either operand is a NaN.
RULE 4: FLT.S produces a result of 0 (writes 0 to rd) when either operand is a NaN.
RULE 5: FLT.S interprets the contents of rs1 and rs2 as IEEE-754 binary32 (single-precision) floating-point numbers.

INSTRUCTION: FLTQ.D
FORMAT: fltq.d rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FLTQ.D produces the same comparison result as the FLT.D instruction for all operand values.
RULE 2: When a quiet NaN is an input to FLTQ.D, the invalid-operation exception flag must not be set.
RULE 3: For inputs that are not quiet NaNs, FLTQ.D must set the invalid-operation exception flag in the same manner as FLT.D.

INSTRUCTION: FLTQ.H
FORMAT: fltq.h rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FLTQ.H treats its source registers as half-precision floating-point numbers.
RULE 2: FLTQ.H produces the same integer comparison result as the FLT.H instruction for the given half-precision operands.
RULE 3: FLTQ.H does not set the invalid-operation exception flag when either source operand is a quiet NaN.
RULE 4: For all operand cases other than quiet NaNs, FLTQ.H sets exception flags in the same way as FLT.H.

INSTRUCTION: FLTQ.Q
FORMAT: fltq.q fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FLTQ.Q performs a floating-point less-than comparison on two quad-precision operands, behaving analogously to the FLT instruction, when the Q extension is implemented.
RULE 2: FLTQ.Q must not set the invalid-operation exception flag when any source operand is a quiet NaN.

INSTRUCTION: FLTQ.S
FORMAT: fltq.s rd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FLTQ.S performs the same floating-point less-than comparison as FLT.S and must not set the invalid-operation exception flag when either source operand is a quiet NaN.

INSTRUCTION: FLW
FORMAT: flw fd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: FLW MUST load a single-precision floating-point value from the memory location whose effective address is computed and place the value into floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: FLW MUST compute its effective address by adding a sign-extended 12-bit byte offset to the base address contained in register rs1.
RULE 3: FLW MUST be executed atomically only when its effective address is naturally aligned; atomicity is not guaranteed for unaligned addresses.
RULE 4: FLW MUST preserve all bits of the transferred single-precision value, including the payloads of non-canonical NaNs.
RULE 5: The FLW instruction loads a single-precision floating-point value from memory into the destination floating-point register rd′.
RULE 6: The FLW instruction computes its effective address by adding to the base address in register rs1′ the zero-extended offset multiplied by 4.
RULE 7: The FLW instruction is semantically equivalent to the uncompressed instruction flw rd′, offset(rs1′).
RULE 8: FLW MUST ignore the mew bit (inst[28]) during execution.
RULE 9: FLW MUST access memory with an element size of 32 bits.
RULE 10: FLW MUST write the loaded value into a floating-point register whose size is FLEN bits.
RULE 11: FLW MUST not use an index register as part of its address calculation.
RULE 12: In the transformed instruction for FLW, the funct3, rd, and opcode fields are identical to those of the original trapping FLW instruction.
RULE 13: Because the original FLW is not compressed, bits 1 and 0 of a transformed FLW instruction are set to binary 11.
RULE 14: The Addr.Offset field of a transformed FLW instruction occupies bits 19:15 and contains the positive difference between the faulting virtual address (written to mtval or stval) and the original virtual address.
RULE 15: All immediate offset fields (bits 31:25, 24:20, and 11:7) of a transformed FLW instruction are set to zero.
RULE 16: For a load address misaligned, load access fault, or load page fault exception arising from an FLW, the trap-instruction register may contain a transformed FLW encoding.
RULE 17: Only synchronous exceptions that arise from explicit memory accesses, such as the load address misaligned, load access fault, and load page fault exceptions, may cause the trap-instruction register to contain a transformed FLW encoding.
RULE 18: Because FLW is a standard load instruction, a transformed instruction encoding is defined for FLW and may be written to mtinst or htinst when any of the specified load-related synchronous exceptions occur.
RULE 19: FLW is an explicit floating-point memory-access instruction and its effective address MUST be processed by the architectural pointer-masking transformation.
RULE 20: Because pointer masking applies to all explicit memory accesses generated by CPU instructions, including those from the floating-point unit, FLW accesses MUST be subject to pointer masking.
RULE 21: Misaligned FLW accesses MUST be supported and are subject to the same limitations that apply to misaligned accesses when pointer masking is not present.
RULE 22: The architectural effect of a misaligned FLW access MUST be identical to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each aligned sub-access that composes the misaligned operation.

INSTRUCTION: FMAX.S
FORMAT: fmax.s fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FMAX.S must write to destination register rd the operand that is larger in value between the single-precision floating-point registers rs1 and rs2.
RULE 2: For FMAX.S, when the two operands are signed zeros, the positive zero (+0.0) is considered larger than negative zero (-0.0) and therefore must be written to rd.
RULE 3: If both rs1 and rs2 are NaNs, FMAX.S must produce the canonical NaN as the result written to rd.
RULE 4: If exactly one of rs1 or rs2 is a NaN, FMAX.S must write the non-NaN operand to rd.
RULE 5: When any operand supplied to FMAX.S is a signaling NaN, the invalid operation exception flag must be set even if the result written to rd is not a NaN.
RULE 6: FMAX.S must compute the maximum of its two single-precision floating-point source operands according to the IEEE 754-2019 maximum operation.
RULE 7: FMAX.S must handle NaN operands using the IEEE 754-2019 maximum operation semantics, i.e., it does not apply the special canonical-NaN result used by FMAXM.S.

INSTRUCTION: FMAXM.D
FORMAT: fmaxm.d fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FMAXM.D computes the maximum of its two double-precision operands in the same manner as FMAX.D, but if either operand is a NaN the result is the canonical NaN.

INSTRUCTION: FMAXM.H
FORMAT: fmaxm.h fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: When the Zfh extension is implemented, the FMAXM.H instruction operates on half-precision floating-point operands.
RULE 2: If either operand of the FMAXM.H instruction is a NaN, the result produced by FMAXM.H is the canonical NaN.
RULE 3: If neither operand of the FMAXM.H instruction is a NaN, the result of FMAXM.H is the greater of the two half-precision values as defined by the IEEE 754-2019 maximum operation.

INSTRUCTION: FMAXM.Q
FORMAT: fmaxm.q fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: If the Q extension is implemented, the FMAXM.Q instruction must operate on quad-precision floating-point operands.
RULE 2: When both operands of FMAXM.Q are not NaN, the result must be identical to the result of the FMAX.Q instruction applied to the same operands.
RULE 3: If either operand of FMAXM.Q is a NaN, the result must be the canonical NaN.
RULE 4: The FMAXM.Q instruction must implement the IEEE 754-2019 maximum operation for quad-precision operands.

INSTRUCTION: FMAXM.S
FORMAT: fmaxm.s fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FMAXM.S MUST compute the IEEE 754-2019 maximum of its two single-precision floating-point operands when neither operand is a NaN.
RULE 2: If either operand of FMAXM.S is a NaN, FMAXM.S MUST produce the canonical NaN as the result.
RULE 3: For non-NaN operands, FMAXM.S returns the operand with the greater numeric value according to the IEEE 754-2019 ordering, including the defined handling of signed zeros and infinities.

INSTRUCTION: FMIN.Q
FORMAT: fmin.q fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FMIN.Q is a quad-precision floating-point computational instruction that takes two quad-precision source operands and produces a quad-precision result.

INSTRUCTION: FMIN.S
FORMAT: fmin.s fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FMIN.S writes the smaller of the values in source registers rs1 and rs2 to destination register rd.
RULE 2: For FMIN.S, the value –0.0 is considered less than the value +0.0.
RULE 3: If both rs1 and rs2 are NaNs, FMIN.S produces the canonical NaN as the result.
RULE 4: If exactly one of rs1 or rs2 is a NaN, FMIN.S returns the non-NaN operand as the result.
RULE 5: Signaling NaN inputs to FMIN.S set the invalid-operation exception flag even when the result is not NaN.
RULE 6: FMIN.S MUST compute the IEEE 754-2019 minimum of its two single-precision floating-point source operands.
RULE 7: When the two source operands of FMIN.S are both numeric values and are unequal, FMIN.S MUST return the operand that is numerically smaller according to IEEE 754 ordering.
RULE 8: When the two source operands of FMIN.S are numerically equal, FMIN.S MUST return the operand that preserves the sign of zero (returning -0.0 if either operand is -0.0, otherwise +0.0).
RULE 9: If one source operand of FMIN.S is a NaN and the other operand is a numeric value, FMIN.S MUST return the NaN operand as specified by the IEEE 754-2019 minimum operation.
RULE 10: If both source operands of FMIN.S are NaNs, FMIN.S MUST return a NaN according to the IEEE 754-2019 minimum operation and MUST NOT replace it with the canonical NaN.

INSTRUCTION: FMINM.D
FORMAT: fminm.d fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FMINM.D implements the IEEE 754-2019 minimum operation on its two double-precision floating-point source operands.
RULE 2: If either source operand of FMINM.D is a NaN, the result produced by FMINM.D is the canonical NaN.
RULE 3: When the D extension is implemented, FMINM.D operates on double-precision (64-bit) floating-point numbers.

INSTRUCTION: FMINM.Q
FORMAT: fminm.q fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: If the Q extension is implemented, the FMINM.Q instruction is defined to operate on quad-precision floating-point numbers.
RULE 2: The binary encoding of the FMINM.Q instruction is the same as the encoding of the corresponding FMIN instruction except that instruction bit 13 is set to 1.
RULE 3: The FMINM.Q instruction shall compute the IEEE 754-2019 minimum of its two quad-precision source operands.
RULE 4: When either source operand of the FMINM.Q instruction is a NaN, the result shall be the canonical NaN.
RULE 5: When neither source operand of the FMINM.Q instruction is a NaN, the result shall be the smaller of the two operands according to the IEEE 754-2019 minimum ordering, including the signed-zero handling identical to that of FMIN.S.

INSTRUCTION: FMINM.S
FORMAT: fminm.s fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FMINM.S implements the IEEE 754-2019 minimum operation for single-precision floating-point numbers.
RULE 2: If either operand supplied to FMINM.S is a NaN, the result produced by FMINM.S is the canonical NaN.
RULE 3: When an instruction word matches the encoding of FMIN.S and has bit 13 set to 1, the processor must decode and execute it as the FMINM.S instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FMSUB.S
FORMAT: fmsub.s fd, fs1, fs2, fs3, rm
RULE 1: FMSUB.S uses the R4-type instruction format, reading floating-point values from source registers rs1, rs2, and rs3 and writing the result to destination register rd.
RULE 2: FMSUB.S computes the fused operation (rs1 × rs2) – rs3 as a single arithmetic step and writes the rounded result to rd.
RULE 3: FMSUB.S may select the rounding mode applied to its result by encoding the desired mode in its rm field.
RULE 4: FMSUB.S must set the invalid-operation exception flag when one multiplicand is Infinity and the other multiplicand is zero, regardless of the value in rs3, including when rs3 holds a quiet NaN.

INSTRUCTION: FMUL.Q
FORMAT: fmul.q fd, fs1, fs2, rm
RULE 1: FMUL.Q MUST write the quad-precision floating-point product of its operands to the destination register dest.
RULE 2: FMUL.Q MUST apply the rounding mode indicated by the rm field of the instruction encoding when producing its result.
RULE 3: FMUL.Q MUST compute the product of the two quad-precision operands using the same architectural semantics, rounding behavior, and exception handling as the double-precision FMUL instruction, but operating on quad-precision values.

INSTRUCTION: FMUL.S
FORMAT: fmul.s fd, fs1, fs2, rm
RULE 1: FMUL.S performs single-precision floating-point multiplication of the values in registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the exact mathematical product, after rounding, to register rd.
RULE 2: FMUL.S can select the rounding mode for the multiplication operation by using the rm field of the instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.D.X
FORMAT: fmv.d.x fd, rs1
RULE 1: FMV.D.X is only defined for implementations where XLEN ≥ 64; implementations with XLEN < 64 MUST not provide FMV.D.X.
RULE 2: FMV.D.X copies the 64-bit pattern from integer register rs1 into floating-point register rd, treating the bits as an IEEE 754-2008 double-precision encoding.
RULE 3: FMV.D.X MUST preserve all transferred bits unchanged, including the payloads of noncanonical NaNs.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.H.X
FORMAT: fmv.h.x fd, rs1
RULE 1: FMV.H.X must read the lower 16 bits of integer register rs1 and interpret them as a half-precision floating-point value encoded according to the IEEE 754-2008 standard.
RULE 2: FMV.H.X must write to floating-point register rd a NaN-boxed representation of the half-precision value taken from rs1.
RULE 3: FMV.H.X must preserve all bits of the half-precision value during the move, so that the bit pattern placed in the lower 16 bits of rd is identical to the lower 16 bits read from rs1.
RULE 4: FMV.H.X must preserve the payloads of noncanonical NaNs, meaning that if the source half-precision bits encode a noncanonical NaN, the same payload appears in the NaN-boxed result in rd.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.W.X
FORMAT: fmv.w.x fd, rs1
RULE 1: FMV.W.X must copy the lower 32 bits of integer register rs1 into floating-point register rd, interpreting those bits as an IEEE 754-2008 single-precision value.
RULE 2: FMV.W.X must not modify any bits during the transfer, so the bit pattern placed in rd is identical to the source bits from rs1.
RULE 3: FMV.W.X must preserve the payloads of non-canonical NaNs when moving the value from rs1 to rd.
RULE 4: FMV.W.X MUST have an rs2 field value that is not equal to 1.
RULE 5: The FMV.W.X instruction is not provided by the Zfinx extension.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.X.D
FORMAT: fmv.x.d rd, fs1
RULE 1: FMV.X.D is architecturally defined only for implementations where XLEN ≥ 64; for implementations with XLEN < 64 the encoding is reserved.
RULE 2: FMV.X.D copies the 64-bit IEEE 754-2008 double-precision bit pattern from floating-point source register rs1 into integer destination register rd without alteration.
RULE 3: FMV.X.D does not modify any of the bits being transferred from the floating-point register to the integer register.
RULE 4: FMV.X.D preserves the payloads of noncanonical NaNs when moving the bit pattern from the floating-point register to the integer register.
RULE 5: FMV.X.D is an instruction that is defined only when the Zdinx extension is implemented.
RULE 6: FMV.X.D has the same architectural effect as the D-extension FMV.X.D except that any reference to an f-register operand is replaced by a reference to the integer x-register with the same register number.
RULE 7: FMV.X.D reads its source operand from the integer register x[rs2] whose number is given by the rs2 field of the instruction encoding.
RULE 8: FMV.X.D writes the 64-bit pattern obtained from the source integer register into the integer register x[rd] specified by the rd field, without altering the bits.
RULE 9: FMV.X.D does not generate any floating-point exception because it performs only a register-to-register move of raw bits.
RULE 10: FMV.X.D leaves all floating-point control and status registers unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.X.H
FORMAT: fmv.x.h rd, fs1
RULE 1: FMV.X.H moves the half-precision value in floating-point register rs1 to integer register rd by placing the 16-bit value in the lower 16 bits of rd and filling the upper XLEN-16 bits of rd with copies of the floating-point number's sign bit.
RULE 2: FMV.X.H must not modify any bits of the transferred half-precision value, therefore the payloads of noncanonical NaNs are preserved unchanged.
RULE 3: FMV.X.H is defined as a transfer instruction in the Zfh extension.
RULE 4: The Zhinx extension excludes FMV.X.H, so FMV.X.H is not provided by Zhinx.
RULE 5: Because FMV.X.H is excluded from Zhinx, no Zhinx variant of FMV.X.H exists that would replace accesses to f registers with accesses to x registers.

INSTRUCTION: FMV.X.W
FORMAT: fmv.x.w rd, fs1
RULE 1: FMV.X.W must copy the 32-bit IEEE-754-2008 encoding of the single-precision value in floating-point register rs1 into the lower 32 bits of integer register rd without alteration.
RULE 2: FMV.X.W must preserve all bits of the transferred value, including the payload bits of non-canonical NaNs.
RULE 3: On RV64 implementations, FMV.X.W must fill the upper 32 bits of integer register rd with copies of the sign bit of the source floating-point value.

INSTRUCTION: FMVH.X.Q
FORMAT: fmvh.x.q rd, fs1
RULE 1: When FMVH.X.Q is executed on an RV64 implementation with the Q extension, it moves bits 127-64 of the source floating-point register rs1 into the destination integer register rd.
RULE 2: FMVH.X.Q transfers the upper 64 bits of a quad-precision floating-point value, enabling the complete 128-bit value to be moved to a pair of integer registers when used together with the FMV.X.D instruction that transfers the lower 64 bits.

INSTRUCTION: FMVP.D.X
FORMAT: fmvp.d.x fd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: When executed on an RV32 implementation that implements the D extension, FMVP.D.X moves a double-precision value formed from a pair of integer registers into a floating-point register.
RULE 2: FMVP.D.X takes bits 31-0 of integer register rs1 as the low-order 32 bits of the double-precision value.
RULE 3: FMVP.D.X takes bits 31-0 of integer register rs2 as the high-order 32 bits (bits 63-32) of the double-precision value.
RULE 4: FMVP.D.X writes the resulting double-precision value to floating-point register rd, overwriting its previous contents.

INSTRUCTION: FMVP.Q.X
FORMAT: fmvp.q.x fd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: FMVP.Q.X is defined only for RV64 implementations.
RULE 2: FMVP.Q.X is available only if the Q extension is implemented.
RULE 3: FMVP.Q.X takes bits 63:0 of the double-precision result from integer register rs1.
RULE 4: FMVP.Q.X takes bits 127:64 of the double-precision result from integer register rs2.
RULE 5: FMVP.Q.X assembles the double-precision value using bits 127:64 from rs2 as the most-significant bits and bits 63:0 from rs1 as the least-significant bits and writes the assembled value to floating-point register rd.

INSTRUCTION: FNMADD.S
FORMAT: fnmadd.s fd, fs1, fs2, fs3, rm
RULE 1: FNMADD.S computes the single-precision result rd as the negated product of rs1 and rs2 minus the value in rs3, i.e., rd <- -(rs1 × rs2) - rs3.
RULE 2: The rounding mode applied to the computation performed by FNMADD.S is taken from the rm field according to the encoding defined in Table 24.
RULE 3: FNMADD.S must set the invalid-operation exception flag when the multiplicands are Infinity and zero, even if the addend rs3 is a quiet NaN.

INSTRUCTION: FNMSUB.S
FORMAT: fnmsub.s fd, fs1, fs2, fs3, rm
RULE 1: FNMSUB.S must multiply the floating-point values in rs1 and rs2, negate the product, add the floating-point value in rs3, and write the final result to rd.
RULE 2: FNMSUB.S reads the floating-point registers rs1, rs2, and rs3 as sources and writes the computed result to the floating-point register rd.
RULE 3: FNMSUB.S must round the computed result according to the rounding mode specified in the rm field of the instruction.
RULE 4: FNMSUB.S must set the invalid operation exception flag when the multiplicands are one Infinity and one zero, even if the addend rs3 is a quiet NaN.

INSTRUCTION: FROUND.D
FORMAT: fround.d fd, fs1, rm
RULE 1: The FROUND.D instruction is defined only when the D extension is implemented.
RULE 2: When executed, FROUND.D rounds the double-precision floating-point value in floating-point register rs1 to an integer using the rounding mode specified in the instruction's rm field.
RULE 3: After rounding, FROUND.D writes the resulting integer, encoded as a double-precision floating-point number, to floating-point register rd.
RULE 4: If the source operand in rs1 is +0, −0, +∞, or −∞, FROUND.D copies that value to rd without modification.
RULE 5: If the source operand in rs1 is a signaling NaN, FROUND.D sets the invalid operation exception flag and does not set any other exception flags.
RULE 6: FROUND.D implements the roundToIntegral operations defined by IEEE-754-2019, excluding the roundToIntegralExact variant.

INSTRUCTION: FROUND.H
FORMAT: fround.h fd, fs1, rm
RULE 1: The FROUND.H instruction rounds the half-precision floating-point value in floating-point register rs1 to an integer using the rounding mode indicated by the instruction's rm field, and writes the resulting integer represented as a half-precision floating-point number into floating-point register rd.
RULE 2: When the source operand of a FROUND.H instruction is zero or an infinity, the value is copied to rd unchanged.
RULE 3: If the source operand of a FROUND.H instruction is a signaling NaN, the invalid operation exception flag is set and no other floating-point exception flags are set.

INSTRUCTION: FROUND.Q
FORMAT: fround.q fd, fs1, rm
RULE 1: FROUND.Q rounds the quad-precision floating-point number read from floating-point register rs1 to an integer according to the rounding mode specified in the instruction's rm field.
RULE 2: FROUND.Q writes the integer result, represented as a quad-precision floating-point number, to floating-point register rd.
RULE 3: When the input to FROUND.Q is a zero or an infinite value, FROUND.Q copies that value to rd without modification.
RULE 4: If the input to FROUND.Q is a signaling NaN, FROUND.Q sets only the invalid operation exception flag and does not set any other exception flags.
RULE 5: FROUND.Q implements the IEEE-754-2019 roundToIntegral operation and therefore does not perform the roundToIntegralExact variant.

INSTRUCTION: FROUND.S
FORMAT: fround.s fd, fs1, rm
RULE 1: FROUND.S rounds the single-precision floating-point value in rs1 to an integer according to the rounding mode specified in its rm field.
RULE 2: FROUND.S writes the rounded integer to rd encoded as a single-precision floating-point number.
RULE 3: FROUND.S copies zero or infinite inputs from rs1 to rd without modification.
RULE 4: When FROUND.S receives a signaling NaN in rs1, it sets the invalid operation exception flag and does not set any other exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FROUNDNX.D
FORMAT: froundnx.d fd, fs1, rm
RULE 1: The FROUNDNX.D instruction is defined only when the D extension is implemented, and it operates on double-precision floating-point numbers.
RULE 2: Execution of the FROUNDNX.D instruction rounds the double-precision value in floating-point register rs1 to an integer using the rounding mode specified in the instruction's rm field.
RULE 3: The integer result produced by the FROUNDNX.D instruction is written to floating-point register rd as a double-precision floating-point representation.
RULE 4: If the source operand rs1 of the FROUNDNX.D instruction is a zero or an infinite value, the same value is copied to destination register rd unchanged.
RULE 5: If the source operand rs1 of the FROUNDNX.D instruction is a signaling NaN, the invalid operation exception flag is set.
RULE 6: If the source operand rs1 of the FROUNDNX.D instruction is not NaN and its value differs from the rounded result, the inexact exception flag is set.
RULE 7: The rounding performed by the FROUNDNX.D instruction implements the IEEE 754-2019 roundToIntegralExact operation.

INSTRUCTION: FROUNDNX.Q
FORMAT: froundnx.q fd, fs1, rm
RULE 1: FROUNDNX.Q performs the IEEE 754-2019 roundToIntegralExact operation on its source operand.
RULE 2: FROUNDNX.Q sets the inexact exception flag when the input operand is not NaN and differs from the rounded result.
RULE 3: When the input operand to FROUNDNX.Q is zero or infinite, FROUNDNX.Q writes the same value to the destination register unchanged.
RULE 4: If the input operand to FROUNDNX.Q is a signaling NaN, FROUNDNX.Q sets the invalid operation exception flag and does not set any other exception flags.

INSTRUCTION: FROUNDNX.S
FORMAT: froundnx.s fd, fs1, rm
RULE 1: FROUNDNX.S rounds the single-precision floating-point value in rs1 to an integral value according to the rounding mode specified in its rm field and writes the integral value, represented as a single-precision floating-point number, to rd.
RULE 2: If the input value in rs1 for FROUNDNX.S is +0 or –0, FROUNDNX.S copies that zero value unchanged to rd.
RULE 3: If the input value in rs1 for FROUNDNX.S is +∞ or –∞, FROUNDNX.S copies that infinite value unchanged to rd.
RULE 4: When the input value in rs1 for FROUNDNX.S is a signaling NaN, FROUNDNX.S sets the invalid operation exception flag and does not set any other exception flags.
RULE 5: For a non-NaN input to FROUNDNX.S, the inexact exception flag is set if the rounded integral result differs from the original input value.
RULE 6: For a non-NaN input to FROUNDNX.S, the inexact exception flag is not set when the rounded integral result is identical to the original input value.
RULE 7: FROUNDNX.S implements the IEEE 754-2019 roundToIntegralExact operation.

INSTRUCTION: FSD
FORMAT: fsd fs2, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: FSD may give rise to multiple memory operations when it accesses more than XLEN bits.
RULE 2: FSD may be decomposed into a set of component memory operations of any granularity when it stores more than XLEN bits.
RULE 3: The component memory operations generated by FSD are not ordered with respect to each other in program order.
RULE 4: The component memory operations generated by FSD are ordered normally with respect to memory operations generated by preceding and subsequent instructions in program order.
RULE 5: FSD MUST store a double-precision value from a floating-point register to memory.
RULE 6: FSD MAY store a NaN-boxed single-precision value as the double-precision value.
RULE 7: FSD is only guaranteed to execute atomically when the effective address is naturally aligned and XLEN >= 64.
RULE 8: FSD does not modify any bits of the double-precision value it stores.
RULE 9: FSD preserves the payload of non-canonical NaNs when storing them.
RULE 10: The Zdinx variant of FSD must exhibit all architectural behavior defined for the D-extension's FSD, with the sole difference that any reference to a floating-point register fN is replaced by a reference to the integer register xN having the same number.
RULE 11: The FSD instruction provided by the Zdinx extension is only available when the Zfinx extension is also implemented.
RULE 12: The C.FSDSP instruction is defined only for processors that implement the RV32DC or RV64DC extensions.
RULE 13: The FSD operation performed by C.FSDSP stores a double-precision floating-point value from floating-point register rs2 to memory.
RULE 14: FSD computes its effective address by adding a zero-extended offset multiplied by 8 to the stack pointer x2.
RULE 15: The FSD instruction stores a double-precision floating-point value from floating-point register rs2 to memory.
RULE 16: The FSD instruction computes its effective address by adding a zero-extended offset scaled by 8 to the stack pointer x2.
RULE 17: The FSD instruction is available only in implementations that include the RV32DC or RV64DC extensions.
RULE 18: FSD stores the double-precision floating-point value from source floating-point register rs2′ to the memory address obtained by adding a zero-extended offset, scaled by 8, to the base address held in register rs1′.
RULE 19: The offset used by FSD is first zero-extended before being multiplied by 8 in the effective-address calculation.
RULE 20: The effective address for FSD is computed as rs1′ plus the product of the zero-extended offset and 8.
RULE 21: The compressed instruction C.FSD expands to the base ISA instruction FSD rs2′, offset(rs1′).
RULE 22: The architectural store operation performed by C.FSD is identical to that performed by the base ISA instruction FSD with the same operands.
RULE 23: The FSD instruction stores a quantity of data that is wider than the XLEN of the executing hart, such as the double-precision store in RV32D.
RULE 24: An implementation may decompose the store performed by FSD into multiple memory operations, and PMP checking is performed independently for each of those operations.
RULE 25: When FSD is decomposed, a portion of the store that passes PMP may become visible even if another portion fails PMP, allowing partial store visibility even for naturally aligned addresses.
RULE 26: If any memory operation generated by FSD fails PMP, an instruction, load, or store access-fault exception must be generated for the FSD instruction, while any operations that succeeded may already have produced visible side effects.
RULE 27: Each memory operation resulting from FSD is evaluated against PMP entries using the standard priority and matching logic, where the lowest-numbered entry that matches all bytes of the operation determines success or failure based on its L, R, W, and X bits.
RULE 28: For an FSD memory operation executed in M-mode, the operation must succeed when no PMP entry matches; for the same operation executed in S-mode or U-mode, the operation must fail when no PMP entry matches and at least one PMP entry is implemented.
RULE 29: When the A field of every PMP entry is set to OFF, any FSD memory operation performed in S-mode or U-mode must fail.
RULE 30: If the effective address of an FSD instruction lies within a page that lacks write permission, the implementation must raise a store page-fault exception.
RULE 31: When an FSD instruction is decomposed into multiple accesses, a store page-fault exception may be raised after one or more of those accesses have completed, and any portion that completed before the fault becomes architecturally visible.
RULE 32: For an FSD instruction that is wider than the XLEN (for example, FSD in RV32D), the implementation may decompose the store into multiple accesses and may raise a store page-fault exception after some accesses have succeeded, making the earlier portion visible even if the store address is naturally aligned.
RULE 33: If a standard, non-compressed store instruction that is an FSD traps, the transformed instruction written to the trap-instruction CSR has the format defined for transformed store instructions.
RULE 34: In the transformed FSD instruction, the rs2, funct3, and opcode fields are identical to those of the original trapping FSD instruction.
RULE 35: In a transformed FSD instruction, the Addr. Offset field occupying bits 19:15 holds the positive difference between the faulting virtual address (as recorded in mtval or stval) and the original virtual address, and this difference can be non-zero only when the memory access was misaligned.
RULE 36: All immediate offset fields of a transformed FSD instruction (bits 31:25, 24:20, and 11:7) are set to zero.
RULE 37: The two least-significant bits (bits 1:0) of a transformed FSD instruction are 11 because the trapping FSD instruction is not compressed.
RULE 38: If an FSD instruction traps because of a Store/AMO address-misaligned exception, the trap-instruction CSR may contain the transformed FSD encoding.
RULE 39: If an FSD instruction traps because of a Store/AMO access-fault exception, the trap-instruction CSR may contain the transformed FSD encoding.
RULE 40: If an FSD instruction traps because of a Store/AMO page-fault exception, the trap-instruction CSR may contain the transformed FSD encoding.
RULE 41: If an FSD instruction traps because of a Store/AMO guest-page-fault exception, the trap-instruction CSR may contain the transformed FSD encoding.
RULE 42: For any other exception that causes an FSD instruction to trap, the trap-instruction CSR must not contain a transformed FSD encoding.
RULE 43: FSD MUST be treated as an explicit memory-access instruction and therefore MUST be subject to the pointer-masking rules that apply to all explicit memory accesses.
RULE 44: The effective address used by an FSD instruction MUST be transformed by the current pointer-masking settings for the privilege mode in which the instruction executes.
RULE 45: Misaligned FSD accesses are supported, but they are subject to the same limitations that apply to misaligned accesses when pointer masking is not in effect.
RULE 46: The behavior of a misaligned FSD instruction MUST be identical to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each aligned sub-access that composes the misaligned memory operation.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJ.D
FORMAT: fsgnj.d fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: For FSGNJ.D, the register numbers of the x-register pair that hold a double-precision operand must be even, and using an odd-numbered register for such an operand is reserved.
RULE 2: For FSGNJ.D, irrespective of endianness, the lower-numbered register of the operand pair holds the low-order bits of the double-precision value and the higher-numbered register holds the high-order bits.
RULE 3: If the destination register pair for the double-precision result of FSGNJ.D includes x0, the write of the result must have no architectural effect.
RULE 4: If x0 is used as any part of a double-precision operand for FSGNJ.D, the operand is treated as zero and the paired register is not accessed.
RULE 5: A register move of a double-precision value can be accomplished with a single FSGNJ.D instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJ.H
FORMAT: fsgnj.h fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: The FSGNJ.H instruction must execute the half-precision floating-point sign-injection operation in the same manner as the single-precision sign-injection instruction defines for single-precision values.
RULE 2: The Zfhmin extension MUST NOT implement the FSGNJ.H instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJ.Q
FORMAT: fsgnj.q fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FSGNJ.Q must copy the sign bit of the second source floating-point operand to the destination floating-point register while preserving the magnitude of the first source operand.
RULE 2: The architectural effect of FSGNJ.Q is defined analogously to the double-precision sign-injection instruction, applied to quad-precision floating-point values.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJ.S
FORMAT: fsgnj.s fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FSGNJ.S MUST produce a result whose exponent and fraction bits are copied from rs1 and whose sign bit is copied from rs2, and the resulting floating-point value is written to rd.
RULE 2: FSGNJ.S MUST NOT set any floating-point exception flags.
RULE 3: FSGNJ.S MUST NOT canonicalize NaNs and MUST preserve NaN payload bits unchanged.
RULE 4: If rs1 and rs2 are the same register, FSGNJ.S must produce a result identical to that register, effectively moving the value to rd.
RULE 5: Executing the FSGNJ.S instruction with a source floating-point register containing a half-precision value and a destination floating-point register copies that half-precision value into the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJN.D
FORMAT: fsgnjn.d fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FSGNJN.D produces a double-precision floating-point result whose magnitude is taken from the first source operand and whose sign bit is the logical negation of the sign bit of the second source operand.
RULE 2: FSGNJN.D applies the same sign-injection semantics defined for the single-precision sign-injection instruction, but operates on double-precision (64-bit) floating-point operands.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJN.H
FORMAT: fsgnjn.h fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FSGNJN.H must compute a half-precision floating-point result whose magnitude equals the magnitude of the value in the first source register and whose sign bit equals the logical complement of the sign bit of the value in the second source register.
RULE 2: If either source operand of FSGNJN.H is a signaling NaN, the instruction must raise the invalid operation exception.
RULE 3: If either source operand of FSGNJN.H is any NaN (signaling or quiet), the result written by FSGNJN.H must be a canonical half-precision NaN.
RULE 4: FSGNJN.H must not raise any floating-point exception other than the optional invalid operation exception specified for signaling NaNs.
RULE 5: The rounding-mode field of the instruction encoding is ignored by FSGNJN.H and does not affect the produced result.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJN.S
FORMAT: fsgnjn.s fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FSGNJN.S copies all bits except the sign bit from the first source floating-point register (rs1) into the destination register.
RULE 2: FSGNJN.S sets the sign bit of the destination register to the logical complement of the sign bit of the second source floating-point register (rs2).
RULE 3: Execution of FSGNJN.S does not set any floating-point exception flags.
RULE 4: Execution of FSGNJN.S does not canonicalize NaNs, so the NaN payload bits from rs1 are preserved unchanged in the result.
RULE 5: When the two source registers of FSGNJN.S are the same register, the instruction produces a result equal to that operand with its sign bit inverted, effectively performing a floating-point negation.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJX.D
FORMAT: fsgnjx.d fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FSGNJX.D must produce a double-precision floating-point result whose sign bit equals the exclusive-OR of the sign bits of the two source registers.
RULE 2: FSGNJX.D must copy all remaining bits (the exponent and fraction fields) from the first source register into the result unchanged.
RULE 3: The handling of NaN inputs for FSGNJX.D must be identical to the handling defined for the analogous single-precision sign-injection instruction.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJX.H
FORMAT: fsgnjx.h fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FSGNJX.H MUST produce a half-precision result whose exponent and fraction fields are copied from the first source operand and whose sign bit is the exclusive-OR of the sign bits of the first and second source operands.
RULE 2: FSGNJX.H MUST write the half-precision result to the destination floating-point register.

INSTRUCTION: FSGNJX.S
FORMAT: fsgnjx.s fd, fs1, fs2
RULE 1: FSGNJX.S produces a result whose non-sign bits are copied verbatim from the source register rs1.
RULE 2: FSGNJX.S sets the sign bit of its result to the exclusive-OR of the sign bits of the source registers rs1 and rs2.
RULE 3: FSGNJX.S writes the resulting floating-point value into the destination register rd.
RULE 4: FSGNJX.S obtains its operand bits from source registers rs1 and rs2 as specified for the operation.
RULE 5: FSGNJX.S does not modify any floating-point exception flags.
RULE 6: FSGNJX.S does not canonicalize NaNs, leaving NaN payload bits unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: FSH
FORMAT: fsh fs2, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: FSH must guarantee atomic execution only when its effective address is naturally aligned; if the effective address is not naturally aligned, atomicity is not guaranteed.
RULE 2: FSH must transfer the half-word value without modifying any bits, and must preserve the payloads of non-canonical NaNs in the transferred data.
RULE 3: FSH must ignore all bits of rs2 except the lower 16 bits, using only those low 16 bits as the value to be stored.
RULE 4: If the Zfbfmin extension is not implemented, execution of the FSH instruction must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 5: An instruction whose opcode field identifies FSH and whose width[2:0] bits are 0 0 1 must be decoded as the scalar half-precision floating-point store instruction FSH.
RULE 6: The mew bit (inst[28]) in an FSH instruction is unspecified and does not affect the operation of FSH.
RULE 7: When FSH is executed, it stores a 16-bit half-precision floating-point element to memory.
RULE 8: The memory element size for FSH is 16 bits, so the store accesses exactly one 16-bit element in memory.
RULE 9: The source operand for FSH is a floating-point register of width FLEN, and the half-precision value stored is derived from that register.
RULE 10: FSH does not use an index register; its effective address is formed solely from the base register and immediate offset.
RULE 11: The width encoding 0 0 1 used by FSH is reserved for scalar floating-point operations and must not be used to encode vector floating-point loads or stores.
RULE 12: For a standard non-compressed store instruction that is an FSH, the transformed instruction must have the format defined for transformed store instructions, with the rs2, funct3, and opcode fields identical to those of the trapping FSH instruction.
RULE 13: In a transformed FSH instruction, the Addr. Offset field that replaces the original rs1 field (bits 19:15) must be the positive difference between the faulting virtual address (written to mtval or stval) and the original virtual address, and this difference may be nonzero only for a misaligned memory access.
RULE 14: The transformation of an FSH instruction must replace the instruction's original immediate offset fields with zero.
RULE 15: Bits 1:0 of a transformed FSH instruction must be binary 01 if the trapping FSH instruction is compressed and binary 11 if the trapping FSH instruction is not compressed.

INSTRUCTION: FSQ
FORMAT: fsq fs2, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: FSQ stores a 128-bit floating-point value to the memory location specified by its effective address.
RULE 2: FSQ is guaranteed to execute atomically only when the effective address is naturally aligned and XLEN equals 128; otherwise atomicity is not guaranteed.
RULE 3: FSQ must preserve all bits of the source floating-point value when storing, including the payloads of non-canonical NaNs.
RULE 4: FSQ uses the width field value binary 100 to select the 128-bit memory element width.
RULE 5: The Membits attribute of FSQ is 128, meaning each memory element accessed by FSQ is 128 bits wide.
RULE 6: The mew bit (inst[28]) of FSQ must be cleared (mew = 0) because a set mew bit encodes reserved expansions of 128 bits and above that are not usable.
RULE 7: The me w field of FSQ is don't-care and does not affect the operation of the instruction.
RULE 8: For a non-compressed standard store instruction whose opcode identifies FSQ, the transformed instruction MUST use the same rs2, funct3, and opcode fields as the original trapping FSQ instruction.
RULE 9: In the transformed FSQ instruction, the Addr. Offset field that replaces rs1 in bits 19-15 MUST contain the positive difference between the faulting virtual address written to mtval or stval and the original virtual address, and this difference MAY be nonzero only for a misaligned memory access.
RULE 10: In the transformed FSQ instruction, all immediate offset fields (bits 31-25, 24-20, and 11-7) MUST be set to zero.
RULE 11: FSQ MUST be subject to pointer masking because pointer masking applies to all explicit memory accesses.

INSTRUCTION: FSUB.Q
FORMAT: fsub.q fd, fs1, fs2, rm
RULE 1: FSUB.Q must subtract the quad-precision floating-point value in the source register identified by rs2 from the quad-precision floating-point value in the source register identified by rs1 and produce a quad-precision result.
RULE 2: FSUB.Q must round the subtraction result according to the rounding-mode specified by the rm field, following the IEEE-754 quad-precision rounding rules.
RULE 3: FSUB.Q must interpret a fmt field value of 11 as selecting the 128-bit quad-precision format for the operation.
RULE 4: FSUB.Q must read its first operand from the floating-point register specified by the rs1 field.
RULE 5: FSUB.Q must read its second operand from the floating-point register specified by the rs2 field.
RULE 6: FSUB.Q must write the resulting quad-precision value to the floating-point register specified by the rd field.

INSTRUCTION: FSUB.S
FORMAT: fsub.s fd, fs1, fs2, rm
RULE 1: FSUB.S performs a single-precision floating-point subtraction of the value in rs2 from the value in rs1 and writes the resulting single-precision value to rd.
RULE 2: The rounding mode specified by the rm field determines how the exact result of the FSUB.S subtraction is rounded to a representable single-precision value.

INSTRUCTION: FSW
FORMAT: fsw fs2, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: FSW stores the 32-bit single-precision value held in floating-point register rs2 to the memory location whose effective address is the sum of the contents of integer register rs1 and the 12-bit signed byte offset encoded in the instruction.
RULE 2: FSW uses the same base-plus-offset addressing mode as integer base ISAs, taking the base address from register rs1 and the offset from a 12-bit signed immediate.
RULE 3: FSW is only guaranteed to execute atomically if the effective address is naturally aligned.
RULE 4: FSW must not alter any bits of the value being transferred, and therefore the payload of a non-canonical NaN present in rs2 is preserved unchanged in memory.
RULE 5: If the effective address used by FSW is misaligned, the execution environment may either handle the misaligned store invisibly or raise a contained or fatal trap.
RULE 6: The FSW instruction stores a single-precision floating-point value held in floating-point register rs2 to memory.
RULE 7: The FSW instruction computes its effective address by adding the stack pointer register x2 to the zero-extended immediate offset multiplied by 4.
RULE 8: The immediate offset used by the FSW instruction is zero-extended before being scaled by a factor of 4.
RULE 9: The FSW instruction writes four bytes (a single-precision word) to the memory location whose effective address was computed.
RULE 10: The source register for the value stored by the FSW instruction is the floating-point register rs2.
RULE 11: When the compressed instruction C.FSWSP is decoded, it expands to an FSW instruction with the same rs2 and offset(x2) operands, and the resulting FSW instruction has identical architectural behavior to the uncompressed form.
