RULE 12: The C.FSW instruction is defined only on implementations that implement the RV32FC extension.
RULE 13: Execution of C.FSW stores the 32-bit single-precision floating-point value held in floating-point register rs2′ to memory.
RULE 14: The memory address used by C.FSW is computed by adding to the contents of integer register rs1′ the value obtained by zero-extending the offset field and multiplying it by 4.
RULE 15: The architectural effect of C.FSW is equivalent to the effect of the uncompressed fsw rs2′, offset(rs1′) instruction.
RULE 16: For a non-compressed FSW that traps, the transformed instruction written to mtinst or htinst must preserve the rs2, funct3, and opcode fields of the original FSW.
RULE 17: In the transformed FSW, the Addr. Offset field occupying bits 19:15 must contain the positive difference between the faulting virtual address recorded in mtval or stval and the original virtual address of the FSW, and this difference may be nonzero only when the FSW caused a misaligned address exception.
RULE 18: In the transformed FSW, all immediate offset fields (bits 31:25, 24:20, and 11:7) are set to zero.
RULE 19: When an FSW generates any of the synchronous exceptions store/AMO address misaligned, store/AMO access fault, or store/AMO page fault, the implementation must write a transformed standard FSW instruction to the trap-instruction register (mtinst or htinst).
RULE 20: Pointer masking MUST be applied to the effective address of every explicit memory access performed by the FSW instruction.
RULE 21: When a compressed instruction expands to the FSW operation, pointer masking MUST be applied to the effective address of that access.
RULE 22: The FSW instruction supports misaligned stores, subject to the same architectural limitations that apply to misaligned stores when pointer masking is not used.
RULE 23: For a misaligned FSW store, the architectural effect MUST be identical to the result of applying the pointer masking transformation to each constituent aligned byte-wise memory access that would occur without pointer masking.

INSTRUCTION: HFENCE.GVMA
FORMAT: hfence.gvma rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HFENCE.GVMA requires the same permissions and raises the same exceptions as HINVAL.GVMA.
RULE 2: Executing HFENCE.GVMA in U-mode always raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: Executing HFENCE.GVMA in S-mode or HS-mode when mstatus.TVM=1 raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 4: Executing HFENCE.GVMA in VS-mode or VU-mode raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 5: Attempting to execute HFENCE.GVMA when the V bit is 1 causes a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 6: Attempting to execute HFENCE.GVMA in U-mode causes an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 7: Attempting to execute HFENCE.GVMA in HS-mode when mstatus.TVM=1 causes an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 8: If TVM=1 and the current privilege mode is HS, an attempted execution of HFENCE.GVMA MUST cause an illegal-instruction trap.
RULE 9: If TVM=1 and the current privilege mode is VS, execution of HFENCE.GVMA does NOT cause a trap and its defined architectural effect occurs.
RULE 10: The TVM setting has no effect on the execution of HFENCE.GVMA in any privilege mode other than HS.
RULE 11: Executing HFENCE.GVMA with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0 must flush all G-stage address-translation cache entries that have cached PMP settings corresponding to the final translated supervisor physical address.
RULE 12: Executing HFENCE.GVMA with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0 must flush all VS-stage address-translation cache entries that have cached PMP settings corresponding to the final translated supervisor physical address.
RULE 13: Executing HFENCE.GVMA with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0 must synchronize with respect to the altered interpretation of G-stage PTEs' PBMT field when the PBMTE bit in menvcfg is changed, and must synchronize with respect to the altered interpretation of VS-stage PTEs' A/D bits when the ADUE bit in menvcfg is changed.
RULE 14: Changes to xSSE become architecturally visible immediately and are not delayed by the execution of HFENCE.GVMA.
RULE 15: HFENCE.GVMA need not be used to synchronize address-translation caches after modifications to xSSE.

INSTRUCTION: HFENCE.VVMA
FORMAT: hfence.vvma rs1, rs2
RULE 1: Executing the HFENCE.VVMA instruction in U-mode must raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 2: Executing the HFENCE.VVMA instruction in VS-mode must raise a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 3: Executing the HFENCE.VVMA instruction in VU-mode must raise a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 4: The HFENCE.VVMA instruction is not subject to any additional execution restrictions in S-mode, HS-mode, or M-mode.
RULE 5: HFENCE.VVMA MUST be executed only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode; executing it in any other mode causes an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: HFENCE.VVMA guarantees that any prior stores already visible to the current hart are ordered before all implicit reads performed for VS-stage address translation of later instructions, provided the hart's hgatp.VMID has the same value as when HFENCE.VVMA executed.
RULE 7: HFENCE.VVMA does not guarantee ordering of prior stores before implicit reads for VS-stage address translation when the hart's hgatp.VMID differs from the value at the time HFENCE.VVMA executed.
RULE 8: When the source register rs1 is not x0, HFENCE.VVMA treats the value in rs1 as a single guest virtual address to which the fence applies.
RULE 9: When the source register rs2 is not x0, HFENCE.VVMA treats the value in rs2 as a single guest address-space identifier (ASID) to which the fence applies.
RULE 10: HFENCE.VVMA applies its effect only to the virtual machine whose identifier equals the hgatp.VMID value at the moment HFENCE.VVMA is executed.
RULE 11: If rs2 is not x0, bits XLEN-1 through ASIDMAX of the rs2 value are reserved for future standard use and current implementations of HFENCE.VVMA must ignore them.
RULE 12: If ASIDLEN is less than ASIDMAX, the implementation of HFENCE.VVMA shall ignore bits ASIDMAX-1 through ASIDLEN of the rs2 value.
RULE 13: Implementations of HFENCE.VVMA are permitted to ignore the guest virtual address in rs1, the guest ASID in rs2, and the hgatp.VMID value, and instead perform a global fence for VS-level memory management of all virtual machines.
RULE 14: Implementations of HFENCE.VVMA are permitted to perform a global fence for all memory-management data structures, regardless of guest virtual address, ASID, or VMID.
RULE 15: The settings of mstatus.TVM and hstatus.VTVM do not cause HFENCE.VVMA to trap.
RULE 16: If the V bit is 1, executing HFENCE.VVMA causes a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 17: Executing HFENCE.VVMA in U-mode triggers an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 18: If the TVM bit in mstatus is set to 1, the HFENCE.VVMA instruction is not prohibited and may be executed in HS-mode.
RULE 19: If the TVM bit in mstatus is set to 1, execution of the HFENCE.VVMA instruction must not raise a trap.
RULE 20: If the PBMTE or ADUE bits in the henvcfg CSR are changed, executing HFENCE.VVMA with rs1=x0 and rs2=x0 suffices to synchronize the architectural view of VS-stage page-table entry PBMT and A/D bit fields for the currently active VMID.
RULE 21: When PMP settings are modified that affect the physical memory containing page tables or the physical memory to which page tables point, HFENCE.VVMA is not required because the required translation-cache flush can be achieved by executing HFENCE.GVMA with rs1=x0 and rs2=x0.

INSTRUCTION: HINVAL.GVMA
FORMAT: hinval.gvma rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HINVAL.GVMA requires the same permissions as HFENCE.GVMA and raises the same exceptions as HFENCE.GVMA.
RULE 2: Executing HINVAL.GVMA in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 3: Executing HINVAL.GVMA in S-mode or HS-mode while mstatus.TVM=1 raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 4: Executing HINVAL.GVMA in VS-mode raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 5: Executing HINVAL.GVMA in VU-mode raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 6: In HS-mode, if the TVM bit in mstatus is 1, execution of the HINVAL.GVMA instruction must be prevented.
RULE 7: In VS-mode, the value of the TVM bit in mstatus does not affect execution of the HINVAL.GVMA instruction.
RULE 8: HINVAL.GVMA is defined as a privileged instruction.

INSTRUCTION: HINVAL.VVMA
FORMAT: hinval.vvma rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HINVAL.VVMA has the same semantics as SINVAL.VMA, except that it combines with SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR to replace HFENCE.VVMA.
RULE 2: HINVAL.VVMA invalidates any address-translation cache entries that an HFENCE.VVMA instruction with the same rs1 and rs2 values would invalidate.
RULE 3: When a single hart executes SFENCE.W.INVAL, then HINVAL.VVMA, then SFENCE.INVAL.IR in that order, the combined effect is identical to executing an HFENCE.VVMA instruction with the same rs1 and rs2 values.
RULE 4: HINVAL.VVMA requires the same permissions as HFENCE.VVMA and raises the same exceptions as HFENCE.VVMA.
RULE 5: Executing HINVAL.VVMA in U-mode always raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 6: Executing HINVAL.VVMA in VS-mode or VU-mode raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 7: HINVAL.VVMA is not subject to the mstatus.TVM restriction.
RULE 8: When the TVM field in mstatus is 1, the TVM setting MUST NOT prevent execution of the HINVAL.VVMA instruction.
RULE 9: When the TVM field in mstatus is 1, the TVM setting MUST NOT cause any trap on execution of the HINVAL.VVMA instruction.
RULE 10: When the TVM field in mstatus is 1, execution of the HINVAL.VVMA instruction in VS-mode MUST NOT trap.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.B
FORMAT: hlv.b rd, rs1
RULE 1: When hstatus.SPVP = 0, execution of HLV.B performs its explicit memory access with an effective privilege mode of VU.
RULE 2: When hstatus.SPVP = 1, execution of HLV.B performs its explicit memory access with an effective privilege mode of VS.
RULE 3: Execution of HLV.B applies two-stage address translation to its memory access.
RULE 4: HS-level sstatus.SUM is ignored during the address translation performed by HLV.B.
RULE 5: If HS-level sstatus.MXR is set, HLV.B treats execute-only pages as readable for its explicit load during both the VS-stage and the G-stage of translation.
RULE 6: vsstatus.MXR influences only the first translation stage (VS-stage) of the address translation performed by HLV.B.
RULE 7: If the V bit in hstatus is 1, executing HLV.B raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 8: If HLV.B is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU = 0, an illegal-instruction exception is raised.
RULE 9: The rs2 field of an HLV.B encoding must be zero, and any non-zero rs2 value makes the encoding reserved.
RULE 10: The funct7 field of an HLV.B encoding must be 0110000, and any other funct7 value makes the encoding reserved.
RULE 11: The funct3 field of an HLV.B encoding must be 100, and any other funct3 value makes the encoding reserved.
RULE 12: The opcode field of an HLV.B encoding must be 1110011, and any other opcode value makes the encoding reserved.
RULE 13: In an HLV.B instruction the rs1 field specifies the base address register used for the memory operation.
RULE 14: In an HLV.B instruction the rd field specifies the destination integer register that receives the loaded byte.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.BU
FORMAT: hlv.bu rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.BU MUST be executed only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current mode is U-mode and the hstatus.HU field equals 1.
RULE 2: HLV.BU performs its explicit memory access with an effective privilege mode of VU when the hstatus.SPVP field equals 0.
RULE 3: HLV.BU performs its explicit memory access with an effective privilege mode of VS when the hstatus.SPVP field equals 1.
RULE 4: HLV.BU applies two-stage address translation (VS-stage followed by G-stage) as defined for VS-mode and VU-mode accesses.
RULE 5: HLV.BU ignores the HS-level sstatus.SUM field during address translation.
RULE 6: When the HS-level sstatus.MXR field is set, HLV.BU treats execute-only pages as readable in both the VS-stage and the G-stage of address translation.
RULE 7: When the vsstatus.MXR field is set, HLV.BU treats execute-only pages as readable only in the VS-stage of address translation.
RULE 8: HLV.BU reads an unsigned byte from the effective address, zero-extends it to XLEN, and returns the result, mirroring the architectural effect of the base-ISA LBU instruction.
RULE 9: If the V bit in hstatus is 1, execution of HLV.BU raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 10: If HLV.BU is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU equals 0, the instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.D
FORMAT: hlv.d rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.D MUST be executed only in M-mode or HS-mode, or in U-mode when hstatus.HU=1.
RULE 2: When hstatus.SPVP=0, HLV.D performs its explicit memory access with an effective privilege mode VU.
RULE 3: When hstatus.SPVP=1, HLV.D performs its explicit memory access with an effective privilege mode VS.
RULE 4: HLV.D MUST use two-stage address translation and MUST ignore HS-level sstatus.SUM.
RULE 5: If HS-level sstatus.MXR is 1, HLV.D MUST treat execute-only pages as readable for the explicit load in both VS-stage and G-stage translations.
RULE 6: The value of vsstatus.MXR MUST affect only the VS-stage of the two-stage address translation performed by HLV.D.
RULE 7: If the V bit is 1, executing HLV.D MUST cause a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 8: If execution is in U-mode and hstatus.HU=0, executing HLV.D MUST cause an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 9: HLV.D is defined only for the RV64 variant.
RULE 10: HLV.D uses the rs1 field to specify the source address register.
RULE 11: HLV.D uses the rd field to specify the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.H
FORMAT: hlv.h rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.H MUST be executed only in M-mode, HS-mode, or in U-mode when hstatus.HU=1.
RULE 2: If the V bit in hstatus is 1, execution of HLV.H causes a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 3: Execution of HLV.H from U-mode when hstatus.HU=0 causes an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 4: The explicit memory access performed by HLV.H uses an effective privilege mode of VU when hstatus.SPVP=0 and VS when hstatus.SPVP=1.
RULE 5: The memory address used by HLV.H undergoes two-stage address translation as defined for VS-mode and VU-mode.
RULE 6: The HS-level sstatus.SUM field is ignored during the address translation for HLV.H.
RULE 7: When HS-level sstatus.MXR=1, HLV.H may read from execute-only pages in both the VS-stage and G-stage of address translation, whereas vsstatus.MXR=1 affects only the VS-stage.
RULE 8: HLV.H loads a signed half-word from memory, sign-extends it to the hart's XLEN, and writes the result to the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.HU
FORMAT: hlv.hu rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.HU loads a 16-bit unsigned value from the effective memory address and writes the zero-extended result to the destination integer register.
RULE 2: HLV.HU MUST be executed only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current privilege mode is U-mode and hstatus.HU=1.
RULE 3: If HLV.HU is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU=0, an illegal-instruction exception MUST be raised.
RULE 4: If HLV.HU is executed when the V bit is 1, a virtual-instruction exception MUST be raised.
RULE 5: The effective privilege mode of the explicit memory access performed by HLV.HU is VU when hstatus.SPVP=0.
RULE 6: The effective privilege mode of the explicit memory access performed by HLV.HU is VS when hstatus.SPVP=1.
RULE 7: HLV.HU performs two-stage address translation and the HS-level sstatus.SUM bit is ignored during the translation.
RULE 8: The HS-level sstatus.MXR bit makes execute-only pages readable by HLV.HU for both the VS-stage and the G-stage of address translation.
RULE 9: The VS-level vsstatus.MXR bit affects only the first translation stage (VS-stage) for HLV.HU.
RULE 10: HLV.HU raises the same architectural exceptions as other load instructions.
RULE 11: HLV.HU loads a 16-bit halfword from the memory address computed by adding the sign-extended 12-bit immediate to the value in rs1 and writes the zero-extended result to rd.
RULE 12: HLV.HU treats the loaded halfword as unsigned and zero-extends it to the XLEN-wide destination register.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.W
FORMAT: hlv.w rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.W MUST be executed only in M-mode or HS-mode, or in U-mode when hstatus.HU=1.
RULE 2: HLV.W performs its explicit memory access with effective privilege mode VU when hstatus.SPVP=0, and with effective privilege mode VS when hstatus.SPVP=1.
RULE 3: HLV.W applies two-stage address translation and ignores HS-level sstatus.SUM.
RULE 4: When HS-level sstatus.MXR is set, HLV.W reads execute-only pages during both translation stages, whereas vsstatus.MXR affects only the VS-stage translation.
RULE 5: HLV.W raises the same exceptions as other load instructions.
RULE 6: If the virtual-machine status bit V=1, execution of HLV.W causes a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 7: If HLV.W is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU=0, it causes an illegal-instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: HLV.WU
FORMAT: hlv.wu rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLV.WU performs an explicit memory access whose effective privilege mode is VS if the hstatus.SPVP field is 1, otherwise the effective privilege mode is VU.
RULE 2: HLV.WU applies two-stage address translation and ignores the HS-level sstatus.SUM field during the translation process.
RULE 3: When the HS-level sstatus.MXR field is 1, HLV.WU treats execute-only pages as readable for both the VS-stage and the G-stage of address translation.
RULE 4: The VS-level vsstatus.MXR field influences only the VS-stage of address translation for HLV.WU.
RULE 5: HLV.WU is defined only for RV64 implementations and is not a valid instruction on RV32 implementations.
RULE 6: HLV.WU requires read permission during address translation and does not require execute permission, matching the permission requirements of the corresponding RV64I LWU instruction.
RULE 7: HLV.WU raises the same architectural exceptions as a regular load instruction, including access-fault exceptions on PMP violations.
RULE 8: If the V bit is 1, execution of HLV.WU causes a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 9: If the processor is in U-mode and the hstatus.HU field is 0, execution of HLV.WU causes an illegal-instruction exception.

INSTRUCTION: HLVX.HU
FORMAT: hlvx.hu rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLVX.HU instruction must be executed only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or U-mode with hstatus.HU=1; otherwise an illegal-instruction exception is raised.
RULE 2: When HLVX.HU executes, the effective privilege mode of its explicit memory access is VU if hstatus.SPVP=0 and VS if hstatus.SPVP=1.
RULE 3: HLVX.HU applies two-stage address translation and ignores the HS-level sstatus.SUM during translation.
RULE 4: During address translation for HLVX.HU, the HS-level sstatus.MXR makes execute-only pages readable for both the VS-stage and the G-stage.
RULE 5: During address translation for HLVX.HU, the vsstatus.MXR influences only the VS-stage of translation.
RULE 6: For HLVX.HU the memory being read must be executable in both stages of address translation, and read permission is not required for translation.
RULE 7: For HLVX.HU the supervisor physical memory attributes of the resulting supervisor physical address must grant both execute and read permissions.
RULE 8: HLVX.HU cannot override machine-level physical memory protection, and attempting to read memory that PMP marks as execute-only causes an access-fault exception.
RULE 9: HLVX.HU raises the same load-instruction exceptions as ordinary load instructions for faults, rather than fetch-type exceptions.
RULE 10: Memory accesses performed by HLVX.HU are not subject to pointer masking, even when MXR is set.
RULE 11: If the V bit is 1, executing HLVX.HU causes a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 12: An instruction whose opcode field is 1110011, funct3 field is 100, funct7 field is 0110010, and rs2 field is 00011 is decoded as HLVX.HU.
RULE 13: In an HLVX.HU instruction, the rs1 field specifies the source address register.
RULE 14: In an HLVX.HU instruction, the rd field specifies the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: HLVX.WU
FORMAT: hlvx.wu rd, rs1
RULE 1: HLVX.WU must be executed only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current mode is U-mode and hstatus.HU=1; otherwise an illegal-instruction exception is raised.
RULE 2: If the V bit is 1, executing HLVX.WU causes a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 3: HLVX.WU performs its explicit memory access with an effective privilege mode of VU when hstatus.SPVP=0, and with an effective privilege mode of VS when hstatus.SPVP=1.
RULE 4: HLVX.WU applies two-stage address translation (VS-stage followed by G-stage) to the effective address.
RULE 5: For HLVX.WU, the HS-level sstatus.SUM flag is ignored during address translation.
RULE 6: For HLVX.WU, if the HS-level sstatus.MXR flag is set, execute-only pages are treated as readable during both VS-stage and G-stage translation.
RULE 7: For HLVX.WU, the vsstatus.MXR flag influences only the VS-stage translation, making execute-only pages readable in that stage.
RULE 8: During address translation for HLVX.WU, the accessed memory must have execute permission in both translation stages; read permission is not required.
RULE 9: After address translation, the supervisor physical memory attributes for the resulting address must grant both execute and read permissions for HLVX.WU.
RULE 10: HLVX.WU cannot bypass machine-level PMP, and if PMP designates the target as execute-only, HLVX.WU generates an access-fault exception.
RULE 11: HLVX.WU raises the same load-type exceptions as ordinary load instructions, rather than fetch exceptions, even though it requires execute permission.
RULE 12: Memory accesses performed by HLVX.WU are not subject to pointer masking, and pointer masking does not apply even when MXR is set.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.B
FORMAT: hsv.b rs1, rs2
RULE 1: When hstatus.SPVP = 0, the explicit memory access performed by HSV.B uses an effective privilege mode of VU.
RULE 2: When hstatus.SPVP = 1, the explicit memory access performed by HSV.B uses an effective privilege mode of VS.
RULE 3: HSV.B applies two-stage address translation to its effective address.
RULE 4: The HS-level sstatus.SUM field is ignored for the address translation performed by HSV.B.
RULE 5: If HS-level sstatus.MXR is set, execute-only pages are treated as readable for explicit loads performed by HSV.B in both the VS-stage and G-stage of address translation.
RULE 6: If vsstatus.MXR is set, execute-only pages are treated as readable for explicit loads performed by HSV.B only in the VS-stage of address translation.
RULE 7: HSV.B produces the same architectural effect as the RV32I/RV64I SB store instruction.
RULE 8: If the V bit of the virtualization state is 1, executing HSV.B raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 9: If the current mode is U-mode and hstatus.HU = 0, executing HSV.B raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 10: HSV.B uses rs2 as the source register that provides the byte value to be stored.
RULE 11: HSV.B uses rs1 as the base address register to compute the effective memory address.
RULE 12: HSV.B stores the low-order byte of rs2 to the memory location whose address is the value held in rs1.
RULE 13: HSV.B ignores the rd field because store instructions do not produce a destination register result.
RULE 14: HSV.B has no specified permission checks, exception conditions, or side effects beyond performing the byte store.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.D
FORMAT: hsv.d rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HSV.D MUST be executed only in M-mode, HS-mode, or in U-mode when hstatus.HU = 1; execution in any other privilege mode is not valid.
RULE 2: HSV.D is not valid for RV32 and is defined only for RV64 implementations.
RULE 3: Each execution of HSV.D performs an explicit memory access.
RULE 4: The effective privilege mode of the explicit memory access performed by HSV.D is VU when hstatus.SPVP = 0 and VS when hstatus.SPVP = 1.
RULE 5: For VS-mode and VU-mode accesses performed by HSV.D, two-stage address translation is applied.
RULE 6: The HS-level sstatus.SUM field is ignored for the address translation performed by HSV.D.
RULE 7: The HS-level sstatus.MXR field makes execute-only pages readable by explicit loads for both translation stages of the memory access performed by HSV.D.
RULE 8: The vsstatus.MXR field affects only the first translation stage (VS-stage) of the memory access performed by HSV.D.
RULE 9: Executing HSV.D while the V bit is 1 causes a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 10: Executing HSV.D from U-mode when hstatus.HU = 0 causes an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 11: HSV.D must be decoded only when the 7-bit opcode field equals 1110011, the 7-bit funct7 field equals 0110111, the 3-bit funct3 field equals 100, and the rd field equals 00000.
RULE 12: HSV.D is defined only for RV64 implementations and is not defined for RV32 or any other base ISA variant.
RULE 13: In an HSV.D instruction the rs2 register provides the 64-bit value that is to be stored to memory.
RULE 14: In an HSV.D instruction the rs1 register supplies the base address used for the store operation.
RULE 15: The rd field of HSV.D is architecturally ignored and must be zero in the encoding.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.H
FORMAT: hsv.h rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HSV.H is valid only when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current mode is U-mode and the hstatus.HU field is 1.
RULE 2: When HSV.H executes, it performs an explicit memory access with an effective privilege mode of VU if hstatus.SPVP is 0, and with an effective privilege mode of VS if hstatus.SPVP is 1.
RULE 3: The explicit memory access performed by HSV.H is subject to two-stage address translation, and the HS-level sstatus.SUM flag is ignored.
RULE 4: If the V bit in hstatus is 1, executing HSV.H causes a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 5: If HSV.H is executed from U-mode while hstatus.HU is 0, an illegal-instruction exception is raised.

INSTRUCTION: HSV.W
FORMAT: hsv.w rs1, rs2
RULE 1: HSV.W is only valid when the current privilege mode is M-mode or HS-mode, or when the current privilege mode is U-mode and the hstatus.HU field is 1.
RULE 2: If HSV.W is executed while the V bit (V=1) is set, a virtual-instruction exception is raised.
RULE 3: If HSV.W is executed in U-mode while hstatus.HU is 0, an illegal-instruction exception is raised.
RULE 4: When hstatus.SPVP is 1, the explicit memory access performed by HSV.W uses an effective privilege mode of VS.
RULE 5: When hstatus.SPVP is 0, the explicit memory access performed by HSV.W uses an effective privilege mode of VU.
RULE 6: The explicit memory access performed by HSV.W is subject to two-stage address translation.
RULE 7: The HS-level sstatus.SUM field is ignored during the address translation for HSV.W.
RULE 8: The funct7 field of HSV.W MUST be the binary pattern 0110101, which identifies the Hypervisor Store class.
RULE 9: The rs2 field of HSV.W contains the value that is to be stored to memory.
RULE 10: The rs1 field of HSV.W holds the base address used for the store operation.
RULE 11: The funct3 field of HSV.W MUST be the binary pattern 100, which selects the word-size store variant.
RULE 12: The rd field of HSV.W is hard-wired to the value 00000 because store instructions do not write a destination register.
RULE 13: The opcode field of HSV.W MUST be the binary pattern 1110011, which selects the privileged-instruction space.

INSTRUCTION: JAL
FORMAT: jal rd, label
RULE 1: JAL forms its jump target address by adding a signed offset, represented as a multiple of 2 bytes in the sign-extended J-immediate field, to the address of the JAL instruction.
RULE 2: The jump target address produced by JAL is confined to a range of ±1 MiB relative to the address of the JAL instruction.
RULE 3: JAL stores the address of the following instruction (pc + 4) into the architectural register specified by its rd field.
RULE 4: When the rd field of JAL specifies register x0, the write of pc + 4 has no architectural effect because x0 is hardwired to zero.
RULE 5: JAL generates an instruction-address-misaligned exception if the computed target address is not aligned to a four-byte boundary.
RULE 6: The JAL instruction computes its target address by adding a sign-extended immediate offset to the address of the current instruction (PC-relative).
RULE 7: The JAL instruction supports an immediate offset range that is larger than the offset range supported by conditional branch instructions.
RULE 8: A JAL instruction with destination register x0 discards the link address and unconditionally transfers control to the target address.
RULE 9: A JAL instruction with destination register x1 writes the address of the instruction following the JAL (pc+2 when the JAL originates from a compressed 2-byte instruction) into register x1 before transferring control to the target address.
RULE 10: The target address of a JAL instruction is formed by sign-extending the immediate offset and adding the result to the current program counter.
RULE 11: JAL MUST transfer control to a target address that lies within ±1 MiB of the current PC.

INSTRUCTION: JALR
FORMAT: jalr rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: The target address produced by a JALR instruction is calculated by adding the sign-extended 12-bit immediate to the value in register rs1 and then setting the least-significant bit of the sum to zero.
RULE 2: A JALR instruction writes the address of the following instruction (pc + 4) to the architectural register specified by rd.
RULE 3: When rd is register x0 in a JALR instruction, the computed return address is discarded and no link is created.
RULE 4: A JALR instruction that uses rd = x0, rs1 = x1 and an immediate of zero implements the standard calling-convention return (RET) behavior.
RULE 5: The 12-bit immediate in a JALR instruction is added to rs1 without being multiplied by two; it is not treated as a multiple of two bytes.
RULE 6: The least-significant bit of the target address of a JALR instruction is always cleared, regardless of the values of rs1 and the immediate.
RULE 7: If the target address computed by a JALR instruction is not aligned to a four-byte boundary, the implementation must raise an instruction-address-misaligned exception.
RULE 8: On implementations where IALIGN = 16, a JALR instruction can never raise an instruction-address-misaligned exception because all instruction addresses are 16-byte aligned.
RULE 9: Encoding a plain unconditional indirect jump (JR) as a JALR with rd = x0 yields the same architectural effect as the JR pseudoinstruction.
RULE 10: In a constrained LR/SC loop, the dynamically executed instructions that occur between the LR instruction and the subsequent SC instruction must not include any JALR instruction.
RULE 11: The code that retries a failing LR/SC sequence must not include any JALR instruction, even though it may contain backward jumps or branches.
RULE 12: JALR with destination register x0 and immediate 0 must set the next PC to the value contained in source register rs1, performing an unconditional jump, provided rs1 ≠ x0.
RULE 13: JALR with destination register x1 and immediate 0 must set the next PC to the value contained in source register rs1 and must write the address of the following compressed instruction (pc + 2) into register x1, provided rs1 ≠ x0.
RULE 14: If rs1 = x0 for a JALR with destination register x1 and immediate 0, the encoding corresponds to the C.EBREAK instruction and the JALR semantics do not apply.
RULE 15: The link address stored by JALR when rd = x1 and immediate 0 is calculated as pc + 2 rather than pc + 4, reflecting the compressed instruction length.
RULE 16: JALR obtains its branch target address by adding the sign-extended 12-bit immediate encoded in the instruction to the value held in the rs1 register.
RULE 17: The term "call" refers to any JALR instruction that writes a non-zero value to the link register, i.e., a JALR with rd ≠ x0.
RULE 18: A C.JALR instruction expands to JALR x1, 0(rs1) and this expansion is classified as a call.
RULE 19: A C.JR instruction expands to JALR x0, 0(rs1) and, when the expanded rs1 is x1 or x5, the instruction is classified as a return.
RULE 20: After a JALR instruction has set the ELP state to LP_EXPECTED, if the next instruction in program order is not 4-byte aligned, the hart MUST raise a software-check exception with cause 18 and xtval indicating "landing pad fault (code = 2)".
RULE 21: After a JALR instruction has set the ELP state to LP_EXPECTED, if the next instruction is not an LPAD instruction, the hart MUST raise a software-check exception with cause 18 and xtval indicating "landing pad fault (code = 2)".
RULE 22: After a JALR instruction has set the ELP state to LP_EXPECTED, if the next instruction is an LPAD whose label field (bits 31:12) does not match the expected landing-pad label encoded in bits 31:12 of x7, the hart MUST raise a software-check exception with cause 18 and xtval indicating "landing pad fault (code = 2)".
RULE 23: Completion of a JALR (or C.JALR or C.JR) may cause a trap to be delivered to the same or a higher privilege mode before the instruction at the target address is decoded, if an asynchronous interrupt occurs or a synchronous exception with priority higher than a software-check exception and xtval set to landing-pad-fault (code = 2) is pending.
RULE 24: When a JALR instruction generates a software-check exception caused by the Zicfilp extension, that exception has higher priority than an illegal-instruction exception but lower priority than an instruction access-fault exception.
RULE 25: If a JALR instruction raises a software-check exception because the instruction is not an LPAD instruction while ELP is LP_EXPECTED, or because the LPAD instruction itself raises the exception, the resulting trap is delivered to the same or a higher privilege mode.
RULE 26: When a trap is taken as a consequence of executing a JALR instruction into privilege mode x, the x PELP bit is set to the current value of ELP and ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.

INSTRUCTION: LB
FORMAT: lb rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: LB must compute its effective address by adding the contents of register rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 2: LB must read an 8-bit byte from memory at the effective address.
RULE 3: LB must sign-extend the loaded 8-bit byte to the XLEN width of the processor and write the resulting value to destination register rd.
RULE 4: LB must raise any exceptions that occur during the memory access even when the destination register rd is x0, and the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 5: LB must not raise an address-misaligned exception when its effective address is naturally aligned for a byte access.
RULE 6: In a system where endianness is byte-address invariant, LB must return the exact byte value previously stored to the same address regardless of the current endianness.
RULE 7: LB accesses that are naturally aligned are guaranteed to execute atomically.
RULE 8: LB must load an 8-bit value from memory, sign-extend that value to the processor's XLEN, and write the sign-extended result into integer register rd.
RULE 9: LB is defined analogously to LW and LH, meaning that LB performs the same sign-extension operation on the loaded 8-bit value to XLEN that LW performs on a 32-bit value and that LH performs on a 16-bit value.
RULE 10: The LB instruction atomically loads a byte from the memory address contained in rs1.
RULE 11: The byte loaded by the LB instruction is sign-extended to XLEN bits before being written to rd.
RULE 12: In the transformed LB instruction, the funct3, rd, and opcode fields must be identical to those of the original trapping LB instruction.
RULE 13: When an LB instruction is transformed, all immediate offset fields of the original instruction are replaced with zero in the transformed encoding.
RULE 14: For a transformed LB instruction, bits 1:0 are 01 if the original trapping LB instruction was compressed and are 11 if the original trapping LB instruction was not compressed.
RULE 15: The effective address used by an LB instruction is transformed by pointer masking before the memory access is performed.
RULE 16: An LB instruction executed by any CPU instruction, including those from extensions such as an FPU, is subject to pointer masking.
RULE 17: An LB instruction must correctly handle misaligned address operands, subject to the same architectural limitations that apply when pointer masking is not performed.
RULE 18: When an LB instruction accesses a misaligned address, the byte fetched shall be the same as the byte that would be fetched if the pointer-masking transformation were applied separately to each aligned byte of the access without pointer masking.

INSTRUCTION: LBU
FORMAT: lbu rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: LBU computes its effective memory address by adding the contents of register rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset encoded in the I-type instruction.
RULE 2: LBU loads an unsigned 8-bit value from the effective address and zero-extends the value to the processor's XLEN width.
RULE 3: When the destination register rd of an LBU is x0, the instruction must still raise any load-related exceptions and must still perform any side effects even though the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 4: LBU must not raise an address-misaligned exception for any effective address because a byte access is naturally aligned for all addresses.
RULE 5: In a system whose endianness is byte-address invariant, an LBU performed at an address that previously stored a byte returns exactly the stored byte value, independent of the system's endianness.
RULE 6: LBU must fetch an 8-bit byte from the effective memory address, zero-extend that byte to 64 bits, and write the resulting 64-bit value into the destination register rd when executed on an RV64I implementation.
RULE 7: LBU must apply the same address-space and access-permission checks as all other load instructions, operating within the 64-bit address space defined by RV64I and respecting the execution environment's definition of legally accessible memory regions.
RULE 8: If the Zcb extension is implemented, the c.lbu instruction must be included in the ISA.
RULE 9: LBU has a 16-bit compressed form named c.lbu that is a version of the standard 32-bit LBU instruction.
RULE 10: LBU must be defined for RV32 implementations.
RULE 11: LBU must be defined for RV64 implementations.
RULE 12: LBU must compute the effective address by adding the sign-extended immediate offset to the base register value.
RULE 13: LBU must read one byte from memory at the effective address.
RULE 14: LBU must zero-extend the loaded byte to the XLEN of the executing ISA.
RULE 15: LBU must exhibit identical architectural behavior (address calculation, memory access, zero-extension) for both the compressed c.lbu form and the regular 32-bit LBU.
RULE 16: LBU (including its compressed c.lbu form) is only present when the Zcb extension is implemented, and the Zcb extension depends on the Zca extension.
RULE 17: LBU is a standard non-compressed load instruction that may cause a transformed instruction to be written to the trap-instruction CSR mtinst or htinst when a synchronous exception occurs.
RULE 18: When a synchronous exception of type load address misaligned, load access fault, load page fault, or load guest-page fault is raised by an LBU, the hardware MAY write a transformed LBU encoding to mtinst or htinst.
RULE 19: In the transformed encoding of an LBU, the funct3, rd, and opcode fields MUST be identical to those of the trapping LBU instruction.
RULE 20: In the transformed encoding of an LBU, the Addr. Offset field (bits 19:15) MUST contain the positive difference between the faulting virtual address recorded in mtval or stval and the original virtual address used by the trapping LBU.
RULE 21: When an LBU is transformed as a basic load, the immediate offset fields of the original instruction MUST be set to zero in the transformed encoding.
RULE 22: The transformed encoding of a non-compressed LBU MUST have bits 1:0 equal to 11.
RULE 23: Because LBU is an explicit memory access, the address used by an LBU must be transformed by pointer masking when pointer masking is enabled for the privilege mode that performs the access.
RULE 24: Pointer masking is excluded only for SFENCE.*, HFENCE.*, SINVAL.*, and HINVAL.*, and since LBU is not listed among these exclusions, pointer masking applies to LBU.
RULE 25: Misaligned LBU accesses are supported and the bytes loaded are identical to those that would be accessed if the pointer-masking transformation were applied individually to each aligned constituent byte of the access.
RULE 26: When an LBU executes while MPRV or SPVP is active, the pointer-masking settings of the effective privilege mode must be used for the address transformation of the LBU.
RULE 27: If MXR is in effect at the effective privilege mode where an LBU is performed, pointer masking must not be applied to the LBU address.
RULE 28: The presence or absence of page-based virtual memory does not affect whether pointer masking applies to an LBU, except that MXR determines the applicability of pointer masking.

INSTRUCTION: LD
FORMAT: ld rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: LD.{aq,aqrl} atomically loads a 2-width-byte value from the memory address contained in rs1 and writes the result into rd.
RULE 2: If the size of the value loaded by LD.{aq,aqrl} (2 width+3 bytes) is less than XLEN, the loaded value is sign-extended to fill the destination register rd.
RULE 3: LD.{aq,aqrl} is defined only for RV64 implementations and is not defined for RV32.
RULE 4: LD loads a 64-bit value from memory into the architectural register rd.
RULE 5: LD computes its effective address by adding the stack pointer register x2 to a zero-extended offset that is multiplied by eight.
RULE 6: LD uses the address form offset(x2) where the offset is the zero-extended immediate encoded in the C.LDSP instruction.
RULE 7: When LD is generated by expanding a C.LDSP instruction, the destination register rd must not be x0; encodings that specify rd = x0 are reserved.
RULE 8: The LD instruction must load a 64-bit value from memory into the destination register rd′.
RULE 9: The LD instruction must compute its effective address as the sum of the contents of the base register rs1′ and the zero-extended immediate offset multiplied by 8.
RULE 10: The immediate offset used by the LD instruction is zero-extended before being scaled by a factor of 8 for address calculation.
RULE 11: When executing the cm.pop operation, the implementation shall set bytes to 4 if XLEN equals 32, otherwise shall set bytes to 8.
RULE 12: When bytes equals 8 during the cm.pop operation, the implementation shall execute an LD instruction that loads from address addr with offset 0 into register x[i] for each register in xreg_list.
RULE 13: During the cm.pop operation, the address used by the LD instruction shall be computed as sp plus stack_adj minus bytes.
RULE 14: After each execution of the LD instruction in the cm.pop operation, the address value shall be decremented by bytes.
RULE 15: Each LD instruction executed by the cm.pop operation shall load a double-word (8 bytes) from the memory location given by addr into the destination register x[i]; the initial addr is sp plus stack_adj minus 8 and is decremented by 8 after each load.
RULE 16: In a 64-bit XLEN implementation, the LD instruction reads an 8-byte (doubleword) value from memory.
RULE 17: The LD instruction transfers the 8-byte value stored at the effective address to the integer register specified as the destination operand.
RULE 18: Within the cm.popret operation, the LD instruction is employed to load an 8-byte value from memory into a register.
RULE 19: For the LD instruction used in cm.popret, the effective address is calculated as sp plus stack_adj minus 8 bytes.
RULE 20: After the LD instruction completes, the address variable used for subsequent loads is decremented by 8 bytes.
RULE 21: The LD instruction writes the loaded doubleword into the architectural integer register identified by its destination field.
RULE 22: LD loads a 64-bit doubleword from memory into a pair of registers specified by the destination register field.
RULE 23: LD must not raise an address-misaligned exception when its effective address is a multiple of 8 bytes.
RULE 24: LD must perform each 32-bit word access atomically when its effective address is a multiple of 4 bytes.
RULE 25: When the effective address of LD is a multiple of 8 bytes, the word accesses are guaranteed to be atomic and no misaligned trap may be raised.
RULE 26: When the effective address of LD is a multiple of 4 bytes but not a multiple of 8 bytes, each word access is guaranteed to be atomic and a misaligned trap may be raised.
RULE 27: When the effective address of LD is not a multiple of 4 bytes, the word accesses are not guaranteed to be atomic and a misaligned trap may be raised.
RULE 28: If LD takes a trap, the architectural value of the base register (rs1) must remain unchanged.
RULE 29: When LD uses a stack-pointer-relative address, the base register that must retain its original value on a trap is x2; otherwise the base register is rs1.
RULE 30: The compressed LD instruction c.ldsp rd, offset(sp) MUST compute its effective address by adding the sign-extended immediate offset, left-shifted by three bits, to the current value of the stack pointer register sp.
RULE 31: The c.ldsp instruction MUST load a 64-bit doubleword from the effective address and store the low XLEN bits into the even-numbered destination register rd and the high XLEN bits into rd+1.
RULE 32: The c.ldsp instruction MUST raise a load-address-misaligned exception if the effective address is not aligned to an 8-byte boundary.
RULE 33: The c.ldsp instruction MUST raise a load-access-fault exception if the effective address translation fails or the memory access is not permitted.
RULE 34: The compressed LD instruction c.ld rd', offset(rs1') MUST compute its effective address by adding the sign-extended immediate offset, left-shifted by three bits, to the value in the source register rs1'.
RULE 35: The c.ld instruction MUST load a 64-bit doubleword from the effective address and store the low XLEN bits into the even-numbered destination register rd' and the high XLEN bits into rd'+1.
RULE 36: The c.ld instruction MUST raise a load-address-misaligned exception if the effective address is not aligned to an 8-byte boundary.
RULE 37: The c.ld instruction MUST raise a load-access-fault exception if the effective address translation fails or the memory access is not permitted.
RULE 38: LD instructions with destination x0 must perform the memory load as specified but must discard the loaded data and must not write any value to architectural register x0.
RULE 39: C.LD and C.SD instructions must use only registers x8 through x15 for any register operand.
RULE 40: If the effective address of an LD instruction is a multiple of 4, the instruction must generate two 4-byte memory operations.
RULE 41: An LD instruction is permitted to generate exactly one memory access regardless of the alignment of its effective address.
RULE 42: Each of the two 32-bit operations produced by a decomposed LD instruction with a 4-byte-aligned effective address is guaranteed to be atomic.
RULE 43: If an exception occurs on a later memory operation of an LD instruction, the architectural effects of any earlier memory operation of the same instruction become visible.
RULE 44: LD loads a 64-bit value from memory into register rd.
RULE 45: LD also stores the loaded 64-bit value into the register whose number is rd+1.
RULE 46: The immediate offset field of LD is zero-extended to XLEN before being multiplied by 8.
RULE 47: LD computes its effective address by adding the zero-extended offset multiplied by 8 to the stack pointer register x2.
RULE 48: LD performs a 64-bit load from the effective address into the destination registers rd and rd+1.
RULE 49: LD MUST load a 64-bit value from memory into the destination registers rd' and rd'+1.
RULE 50: LD MUST compute the effective address by adding the base address in register rs1' to the zero-extended offset multiplied by 8.
RULE 51: LD MUST zero-extend the offset before applying any scaling.
RULE 52: LD MUST scale the zero-extended offset by a factor of 8.
RULE 53: LD MUST be defined as a 64-bit load instruction in the RV64I base ISA.
RULE 54: The hypervisor virtual-machine load instruction HLV.D, which corresponds to LD, is not valid on RV32 implementations.
RULE 55: When a standard LD instruction traps, the transformed instruction written to the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) must have the same funct3, rd, and opcode fields as the original LD.
RULE 56: In the transformed LD stored in mtinst or htinst, the Addr.Offset field (bits 19:15) must equal the positive difference between the faulting virtual address (written to mtval or stval) and the original virtual address.
RULE 57: The Addr.Offset field of the transformed LD may be non-zero only when the faulting memory access was misaligned.
RULE 58: For a basic load such as LD, the transformation must replace all immediate offset fields in the encoded instruction with zeros.
RULE 59: The two low-order bits (bits 1:0) of the transformed LD in mtinst or htinst must be binary 11 because the original LD is not compressed.
RULE 60: LD is an explicit memory access and therefore must be subject to the pointer-masking transformation defined for explicit memory accesses.
RULE 61: When an LD instruction is executed on the CPU, including when generated by CPU extensions such as an FPU, pointer masking must be applied to the effective address.
RULE 62: If MPRV or SPVP is enabled, LD must apply pointer masking using the pointer-masking settings of the effective privilege mode.
RULE 63: When MXR is set in the effective privilege mode at which an LD is performed, pointer masking must not be applied to that LD.
RULE 64: Even when page-based virtual memory is not in effect, MXR must be consulted to determine whether pointer masking is applied to an LD.
RULE 65: LD must support misaligned accesses, subject to the same architectural limitations that apply when pointer masking is not present.
RULE 66: The bytes accessed by a misaligned LD must be identical to the bytes that would be accessed if the pointer-masking transformation were applied individually to each byte of the access.

INSTRUCTION: LH
FORMAT: lh rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: The effective address for LH is computed by adding the contents of register rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 2: LH loads a 16-bit value from memory, sign-extends it to 32 bits, and writes the result to register rd.
RULE 3: When the destination register rd of an LH instruction is x0, the load still raises any applicable exceptions and performs any other side effects even though the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 4: If the effective address of an LH instruction is naturally aligned (divisible by 2), the instruction shall not raise an address-misaligned exception regardless of the execution environment implementation.
RULE 5: The LH instruction on an RV64I implementation must read a 16-bit value from the memory address formed by adding its sign-extended immediate offset to the base register.
RULE 6: After reading the 16-bit value, the LH instruction must sign-extend the value to 64 bits before writing the result to the destination register rd.
RULE 7: LH.{aq,aqrl} atomically loads a 2-byte value from the address in rs1 and places the sign-extended result into rd.
RULE 8: LH sign-extends the loaded 2-byte value to XLEN when the loaded size is less than XLEN.
RULE 9: Encodings of LH without the aq bit set are RESERVED.
RULE 10: Encodings of LH with only the rl bit set (load-release) are not supported.
RULE 11: In the transformed LH instruction, the funct3, rd, and opcode fields must be identical to those of the trapping LH instruction.
RULE 12: The Addr. Offset field of a transformed LH instruction must contain the positive difference between the faulting virtual address written to mtval or stval and the original virtual address, and this difference may be nonzero only for a misaligned memory access.
RULE 13: For a transformed LH instruction, the immediate offset fields must be set to zero.
RULE 14: The two least-significant bits (bits 1:0) of a transformed LH instruction must be 11 because LH is not a compressed instruction.
RULE 15: The effective address used by LH must be processed by the pointer-masking transformation before the memory read is performed.
RULE 16: LH must support execution on addresses that are not halfword-aligned, providing defined architectural behavior for misaligned accesses.
RULE 17: For a misaligned LH, the implementation must treat the operation as the composition of aligned memory accesses each subjected to pointer masking, such that the overall effect is identical to applying the pointer-masking transformation to each byte of the original halfword access.
RULE 18: The set of bytes returned by a misaligned LH must be exactly the same as the bytes that would be obtained by individually applying pointer masking to each byte of the halfword and then assembling them.

INSTRUCTION: LHU
FORMAT: lhu rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: LHU loads a 16-bit value from memory and zero-extends it to a 32-bit value before writing the result to rd.
RULE 2: LHU copies the loaded value from memory into the destination register rd.
RULE 3: LHU computes its effective address by adding the contents of rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 4: LHU with destination register x0 must still raise any exceptions and perform all side effects even though the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 5: LHU whose effective address is naturally aligned (address divisible by 2) shall not raise an address-misaligned exception.
RULE 6: In a byte-address invariant system, each byte fetched by LHU reflects the value previously stored at its address independent of the system's endianness.
RULE 7: In a little-endian configuration, LHU places the byte from the lower memory address into the least-significant bits of rd and the byte from the higher address into the next higher bits.
RULE 8: In a big-endian configuration, LHU places the byte from the higher memory address into the least-significant bits of rd and the byte from the lower address into the next higher bits.
RULE 9: When LHU is naturally aligned, its execution is atomic with respect to other memory operations.
RULE 10: LHU loads a 16-bit halfword from memory and zero-extends the loaded value to the processor's XLEN before writing the result to register rd.
RULE 11: On an RV64I implementation, the XLEN is 64 bits, therefore LHU zero-extends the 16-bit memory value to a 64-bit result placed in rd.
RULE 12: The compressed instruction c.lhu performs a load halfword unsigned (LHU) operation as defined by the CLH compressed format.
RULE 13: The LHU instruction forms its 32-bit immediate offset uimm such that bits 31:2 are zero, bit 1 is taken from instruction bit 5, and bit 0 is zero.
RULE 14: The LHU instruction adds the XLEN-bit value of source register rs1′ to the zero-extended immediate uimm to compute the effective memory address.
RULE 15: The LHU instruction reads a halfword (bits 15 down to 0) from memory at the effective address.
RULE 16: The LHU instruction zero-extends the loaded halfword to XLEN bits and writes the result to destination register rd′.
RULE 17: The LHU instruction selects its destination register rd′ from the standard register set x8-x15.
RULE 18: The LHU instruction selects its source register rs1′ from the standard register set x8-x15.
RULE 19: The LHU instruction has no architectural prerequisites for execution.
RULE 20: When an LHU instruction traps, the implementation must write a transformed instruction into the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst).
RULE 21: The transformed instruction written for a trapped LHU must retain the original LHU's funct3, rd, and opcode fields.
RULE 22: The transformed instruction for a trapped LHU must conform to the transformed-instruction layout defined for basic loads.
RULE 23: The LHU instruction's explicit memory accesses are subject to pointer masking.
RULE 24: When the LHU instruction generates a misaligned memory access, the operation is performed as if pointer masking is applied to each aligned sub-access, and the same limitations that apply to misaligned accesses without pointer masking also apply.
RULE 25: Pointer masking applies to LHU memory accesses only when the access is generated by an instruction executing on the CPU, including accesses generated by CPU extensions such as an FPU.
RULE 26: If the MPRV or SPVP privilege-mode settings are active, the pointer-masking behavior of the LHU instruction follows the pointer-masking configuration of the effective privilege mode.
RULE 27: When MXR is active in the effective privilege mode that performs the LHU memory access, pointer masking does not apply to that LHU access.

INSTRUCTION: LPAD
FORMAT: lpad imm20
RULE 1: The LPAD instruction encodes a 20-bit immediate called the landing-pad-label (LPL) which is compared with bits 31:12 of the x7 register.
RULE 2: If the LPL field of an LPAD instruction is zero, the LPAD must not perform the label check and software may omit establishing an expected landing-pad label value in x7.
RULE 3: When the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED and the next instruction, which is expected to be an LPAD, is not 4-byte aligned, the processor must raise a software-check exception with cause 18 and xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 4: When the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED and the next instruction is not an LPAD, the processor must raise a software-check exception with cause 18 and xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 5: When the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED and the next instruction is an LPAD whose LPL is non-zero and does not equal bits 31:12 of x7, the processor must raise a software-check exception with cause 18 and xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 6: When the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED and the next instruction is a correctly aligned LPAD whose LPL is either zero or matches bits 31:12 of x7, the processor must update the ELP state to NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 7: The LPAD instruction must be placed at a 4-byte aligned address; misaligned placement can cause the software-check exception described for the LP_EXPECTED state.
RULE 8: When Zicfilp is not enabled, execution of the LPAD instruction MUST have no architectural effect (it is a no-op).
RULE 9: When Zicfilp is enabled and the LPAD instruction is executed while the PC is not 4-byte aligned and the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED, the LPAD instruction MUST raise a software-check exception with xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 10: When Zicfilp is enabled and the LPAD instruction is executed while the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED, the LPL field of the instruction is non-zero, and the LPL field does not equal bits 31:12 of the x7 register, the LPAD instruction MUST raise a software-check exception with xtval set to "landing pad fault (code=2)".
RULE 11: When Zicfilp is enabled and the LPAD instruction is executed while the ELP state is LP_EXPECTED, the PC is 4-byte aligned, and either the LPL field is zero or it matches bits 31:12 of the x7 register, the LPAD instruction MUST update the architectural ELP state to NO_LP_EXPECTED and MUST NOT raise a software-check exception.
RULE 12: When Zicfilp is enabled and the ELP state is not LP_EXPECTED, execution of the LPAD instruction MUST have no architectural effect (it is a no-op).
RULE 13: If the LPE field in the menvcfg CSR is 0, thereby disabling the Zicfilp extension in S-mode (or in U-mode when S-mode is not implemented), then execution of the LPAD instruction must produce no architectural effect (it operates as a no-op).
RULE 14: If the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode (the MLPE field in mseccfg is 0), execution of an LPAD instruction must have no architectural effect.
RULE 15: When the LPE field in the senvcfg CSR is 0, the LPAD instruction must operate as a no-op.
RULE 16: While the LPE field in the senvcfg CSR is 0, execution of the LPAD instruction must not update the ELP state, which remains NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 17: When the LPE field in the henvcfg CSR is 0, the LPAD instruction in VS-mode must perform no architectural effect (operate as a no-op).
RULE 18: A software-check exception that occurs because the fetched instruction is not an LPAD instruction while ELP is LP_EXPECTED, or a software-check exception generated by an LPAD instruction itself, MUST result in a trap being delivered to the same privilege mode or to a higher privilege mode.

INSTRUCTION: LR.D
FORMAT: lr.d rd, (rs1)
RULE 1: LR.D loads a doubleword from the address in rs1, writes the value to rd, and registers a reservation set that subsumes the bytes of the addressed doubleword.
RULE 2: LR.D is defined only for RV64 implementations.
RULE 3: The address in rs1 for LR.D must be naturally aligned to eight bytes; if the address is not naturally aligned, an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception will be generated.
RULE 4: Execution of LR.D MUST invalidate any previous reservation held by the hart.
RULE 5: A hart MUST hold at most one reservation at a time.
RULE 6: A store-conditional instruction can succeed only if it pairs with the most recent LR.D in program order executed by the same hart.
RULE 7: Setting the aq bit on LR.D gives the LR/SC sequence acquire semantics.
RULE 8: Setting the aq bit on LR.D does not act as a fence for ordinary loads and stores unless the paired SC also has the appropriate rl and aq bits set.
RULE 9: LR.D registers a reservation set that subsumes the addressed doubleword, analogous to the behavior of LR.W.

INSTRUCTION: LR.W
FORMAT: lr.w rd, (rs1)
RULE 1: LR.W MUST load a word from the address in rs1, place the sign-extended value into rd, and register a reservation set that includes at least the bytes of the addressed word.
RULE 2: On RV64 implementations, LR.W MUST sign-extend the loaded 32-bit value before writing it to rd.
RULE 3: LR.W MUST cause an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception if the address in rs1 is not naturally aligned to a four-byte boundary.
RULE 4: If a misaligned LR.W access is not emulated, the implementation MAY raise an access-fault exception instead of completing the load.
RULE 5: LR.W can be given acquire semantics by setting its aq bit.

INSTRUCTION: LUI
FORMAT: lui rd, uimm20
RULE 1: Executing a LUI instruction must write to the destination register a 32-bit value whose upper 20 bits are the instruction's 20-bit immediate and whose lower 12 bits are zero.
RULE 2: After a LUI instruction has loaded a register with its upper-immediate value, a subsequent JALR that uses that register as its base may add the signed 12-bit immediate to the register value to compute the jump target address.
RULE 3: LUI writes to integer register rd the 32-bit value formed by placing the 20-bit U-immediate into bits 31:12 and setting bits 11:0 to zero.
RULE 4: In RV64I, the 32-bit value produced by LUI is sign-extended to 64 bits before being written to rd.
RULE 5: The address offset that can be produced by pairing an LUI instruction with a subsequent load or store instruction in RV64I is limited to the inclusive range [−2^31−2^11, 2^31−2^11−1].
RULE 6: When a call-offset pseudoinstruction expands to a two-instruction sequence using LUI, the LUI writes the 20-bit immediate into bits 31:12 of the link register (x1 or x5) and sets bits 11:0 of that register to zero.
RULE 7: When a tail-offset pseudoinstruction expands to a two-instruction sequence using LUI, the LUI writes the 20-bit immediate into bits 31:12 of register x7 and sets bits 11:0 of x7 to zero.
RULE 8: An indirect call or jump site may use an LUI instruction to place a landing-pad label into bits 31:12 of register x7, leaving bits 11:0 cleared.
RULE 9: The LUI instruction places its 20-bit immediate value into bits 31:12 of the destination register and clears bits 11:0 of that register to zero.

INSTRUCTION: LW
FORMAT: lw rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: LW MUST compute its effective address by adding the value in register rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 2: LW MUST load a 32-bit value from the memory location given by its effective address into the destination register rd.
RULE 3: LW with destination register x0 MUST still raise any applicable exceptions and perform all side effects even though the loaded value is discarded.
RULE 4: In a little-endian configuration, LW MUST transfer the byte from the lowest memory address of the accessed word into the least-significant byte of rd and similarly for the remaining bytes.
RULE 5: In a big-endian configuration, LW MUST transfer the byte from the highest memory address of the accessed word into the least-significant byte of rd and similarly for the remaining bytes.
RULE 6: LW whose effective address is naturally aligned (i.e., divisible by 4) shall not raise an address-misaligned exception regardless of the execution-environment identifier (EEI).
RULE 7: LW with a naturally aligned effective address (address divisible by 4) is guaranteed to execute atomically.
RULE 8: LW MUST obey the permissions and address-space restrictions defined by the EEI, such as read-only regions or regions that only allow word accesses.
RULE 9: LW MUST load a 32-bit value from memory, sign-extend that value to 64 bits, and store the resulting 64-bit value into register rd on RV64I implementations.
RULE 10: LW MUST access memory using a 64-bit effective address, and the effective address must lie within the portions of the 64-bit address space that the execution environment defines as legal for load operations.
RULE 11: LW atomically reads two bytes from the memory address contained in rs1 and writes the sign-extended result into rd when the loaded size is less than XLEN.
RULE 12: LW encodings that do not have the aq bit set are RESERVED.
RULE 13: The LW instruction with encoding lw rd, offset(x2) is a 32-bit load instruction.
RULE 14: The LW instruction loads a 32-bit value from the memory location whose effective address is the sum of the offset and the value in register x2, and writes that value into register rd.
RULE 15: The C.LWSP instruction expands to the LW instruction form lw rd, offset(x2).
RULE 16: LW loads a 32-bit value from the memory location whose effective address is computed and places that value into register rd′.
RULE 17: LW computes its effective address by adding to the base address in rs1′ the zero-extended offset multiplied by 4.
RULE 18: The compressed instruction C.LW expands to the base LW instruction with the same destination register rd′, source register rs1′, and offset.
RULE 19: LW s4, 60(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 60 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s4.
RULE 20: LW s3, 56(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 56 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s3.
RULE 21: LW s2, 52(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 52 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s2.
RULE 22: LW s1, 48(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 48 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s1.
RULE 23: LW s0, 44(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 44 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register s0.
RULE 24: LW ra, 40(sp) loads a 32-bit word from the memory address formed by adding 40 to the current contents of register sp and writes the loaded value into register ra.
RULE 25: When XLEN = 32, the LW instruction must load a 32-bit word from the memory address calculated as sp + stack_adj – 4 into the specified integer register x[i].
RULE 26: The LW instruction must treat the value 4 as the number of bytes that constitute the word it loads when XLEN = 32.
RULE 27: After executing LW for a register in xreg_list, the address used for the next LW must be decremented by 4 bytes.
RULE 28: The LW instruction must be performed once for each register listed in xreg_list, using the current address before it is decremented.
RULE 29: The address used by each LW must be updated by subtracting 4 bytes after the load completes, so that subsequent LW operations use the new address.
RULE 30: When XLEN equals 32, an LW instruction loads a 32-bit word from the memory location whose effective address is given by the address operand and writes the value into the specified integer register.
RULE 31: For a 32-bit load performed by LW, the effective address is calculated as sp + stack_adj − 4, and after each LW execution the address operand for the next iteration is decremented by 4.
RULE 32: An LW instruction may raise an exception or interrupt if the memory access at its effective address causes a fault.
RULE 33: If an LW instruction raises an exception or interrupt, the load loop is terminated and subsequent adjustments of sp and the return operation are not performed.
RULE 34: The load loop containing LW instructions repeats until every LW in the sequence completes without raising an exception or interrupt.
RULE 35: LW is valid only when the processor is executing in M-mode, HS-mode, or U-mode with the hstatus.HU bit equal to 1.
RULE 36: LW raises the same architectural exceptions as any other load instruction.
RULE 37: When bits 1:0 of the trap-instruction register are binary 11 and the register's value encodes a standard LW instruction, the trapping instruction must be interpreted as LW and the register's value is defined as the transformation of that trapping LW instruction.
RULE 38: When a synchronous exception of type load address misaligned, load access fault, or load page fault is taken for an LW instruction, the trap-instruction register may contain the transformed LW encoding.
RULE 39: When a load guest-page fault is taken for an LW instruction, the trap-instruction register must contain the special pseudoinstruction encoding of lw x0,0(x0) instead of a transformed LW.
RULE 40: In a transformed LW instruction that is not compressed, the funct3, rd, and opcode fields are identical to those of the trapping LW instruction.
RULE 41: In a transformed LW instruction, the Addr. Offset field that replaces the rs1 field (bits 19:15) equals the positive difference between the faulting virtual address (from mtval or stval) and the original virtual address, and this field is non-zero only if the faulting access was misaligned.
RULE 42: In a transformed LW instruction, all immediate offset fields are set to zero.
RULE 43: In a transformed LW instruction, bits 1:0 are 01 when the original trapping LW was a compressed instruction and 11 when it was an uncompressed instruction.
RULE 44: The encoding 0x00002003 on RV32 and 0x00003003 on RV64 represents the pseudoinstruction lw x0,0(x0) and is used as the trap-instruction register value for load guest-page faults involving an LW instruction.
RULE 45: The LW instruction, being an explicit memory access, MUST be subject to pointer masking.
RULE 46: Before the LW instruction accesses memory, the effective address used for the load is transformed by the pointer-masking operation.
RULE 47: The LW instruction MUST support misaligned accesses, and the same limitations that apply to misaligned accesses without pointer masking also apply when pointer masking is used.
RULE 48: When the LW instruction performs a misaligned access, the bytes loaded MUST be identical to the bytes that would be loaded if the pointer-masking transformation were applied separately to each aligned sub-access that composes the misaligned access.

INSTRUCTION: LWU
FORMAT: lwu rd, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: An LWU instruction reads a 32-bit word from the effective memory address and writes the zero-extended 64-bit value into the destination register.
RULE 2: An LWU instruction on RV64I forms a 64-bit effective address, and the execution environment defines which portions of that address space are legal for the load to access.
RULE 3: A LWU instruction executed on an RV32 implementation is not a defined instruction and therefore is not valid.
RULE 4: The transformed encoding of a trapping LWU instruction must have the same funct3, rd, and opcode fields as the original LWU instruction.
RULE 5: In the transformed LWU encoding the rs1 field is replaced by an Addr. Offset field that contains the positive difference between the faulting virtual address (written to mtval or stval) and the original virtual address, and this difference may be non-zero only for a misaligned memory access.
RULE 6: All immediate offset fields (bits 31:25, 24:20, and 11:7) of the original LWU instruction are replaced with zero in the transformed LWU encoding.
RULE 7: Because LWU is not a compressed instruction, bits 1:0 of the transformed LWU encoding are binary 11.
RULE 8: When a load address misaligned exception is taken, the hardware may write the transformed LWU encoding to the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst).
RULE 9: When a load access fault exception is taken, the hardware may write the transformed LWU encoding to the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst).
RULE 10: When a load page fault exception is taken, the hardware may write the transformed LWU encoding to the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst).
RULE 11: When a load guest-page fault exception is taken, the hardware may write the transformed LWU encoding to the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst).
RULE 12: LWU does not perform any CSR reads, CSR writes, DMA controller accesses, or hardware-generated CSR writes.

INSTRUCTION: MAX
FORMAT: max rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: AMOMAX.W and AMOMAXU.W must operate on a 32-bit word located at the address in rs1, and AMOMAX.D and AMOMAXU.D must operate on a 64-bit doubleword located at the address in rs1.
RULE 2: The address in rs1 used by any AMOMAX or AMOMAXU instruction must be naturally aligned to the operand size (four-byte aligned for word operations and eight-byte aligned for doubleword operations) unless a misaligned-atomicity-granule PMA applies and all accessed bytes lie within the same granule.
RULE 3: If the address in rs1 used by an AMOMAX or AMOMAXU instruction is not naturally aligned and no applicable PMA relaxes the requirement, the implementation must raise an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception.
RULE 4: For each execution of an AMOMAX or AMOMAXU instruction, the processor must atomically load the memory value at the address in rs1, place the loaded value into rd, compute the signed maximum for AMOMAX or the unsigned maximum for AMOMAXU of the loaded value and the value in rs2, and store the computed maximum back to the same address in rs1.
RULE 5: For RV64 implementations, a 32-bit AMOMAX.W or AMOMAXU.W must sign-extend the value placed in rd and must ignore the upper 32 bits of rs2 when performing the maximum computation.
RULE 6: If rd is x0 in an AMOMAX or AMOMAXU instruction, the loaded value is discarded while the computed maximum is still stored to memory.
RULE 7: If the aq bit of an AMOMAX or AMOMAXU instruction is set, no later memory operation in the same hart may be observed to occur before that AMO.
RULE 8: If the rl bit of an AMOMAX or AMOMAXU instruction is set, no other hart may observe that AMO before memory accesses that precede the AMO in the issuing hart.
RULE 9: If both aq and rl bits are set for an AMOMAX or AMOMAXU instruction, the operation must be sequentially consistent.
RULE 10: The result written to rd by an AMOMAX[U].B or AMOMAX[U].H instruction is always sign-extended, and the original bits of rs2 are ignored.
RULE 11: The address contained in rs1 for an AMOMAX[U].B or AMOMAX[U].H instruction must be naturally aligned to the operand size, and a misaligned address triggers the same exception as defined for the A extension.
RULE 12: An AMOMAX[U].B or AMOMAX[U].H instruction may set the aq and rl bits to request acquire or release semantics for optional release-consistency behavior.
RULE 13: MAX must write to destination register rd the larger of the two single-precision floating-point operands in rs1 and rs2 according to IEEE-754 ordering.
RULE 14: MAX must treat the value -0.0 as less than +0.0, so when the operands are -0.0 and +0.0 the result is +0.0.
RULE 15: MAX must produce the canonical NaN as the result when both rs1 and rs2 contain NaN values.
RULE 16: MAX must produce the non-NaN operand as the result when exactly one of rs1 or rs2 contains a NaN value.
RULE 17: MAX must set the invalid operation exception flag whenever any operand is a signaling NaN, even if the resulting value is not NaN.
RULE 18: The MAX instruction MUST be a quad-precision floating-point computational instruction defined in the FMIN-MAX class with the fmt field set to Q.
RULE 19: The MAX instruction MUST use the OP-FP opcode.
RULE 20: The MAX instruction MUST contain a funct5 field value that identifies the FMIN-MAX operation.
RULE 21: The MAX instruction MUST use the rs1 register to provide the first quad-precision operand.
RULE 22: The MAX instruction MUST use the rs2 register to provide the second quad-precision operand.
RULE 23: The MAX instruction MUST have the MIN/MAX field set to select the MAX operation.
RULE 24: The MAX instruction MUST write its quad-precision result to the destination register rd.
RULE 25: The MAX instruction MUST operate on quad-precision (128-bit) floating-point operands.
RULE 26: The MAX instruction MUST produce a quad-precision (128-bit) floating-point result.
RULE 27: For the FMAXM.S instruction, when both source operands are not NaN, the destination register must contain the greater of the two operands according to the IEEE 754-2019 maximum operation.
RULE 28: For the FMAXM.S instruction, if either source operand is a NaN, the destination register must be written with the canonical NaN.
RULE 29: For the FMAXM.D instruction, when both source operands are not NaN, the destination register must contain the greater of the two operands according to the IEEE 754-2019 maximum operation.
RULE 30: For the FMAXM.D instruction, if either source operand is a NaN, the destination register must be written with the canonical NaN.
RULE 31: For the FMAXM.H instruction, when both source operands are not NaN, the destination register must contain the greater of the two operands according to the IEEE 754-2019 maximum operation.
RULE 32: For the FMAXM.H instruction, if either source operand is a NaN, the destination register must be written with the canonical NaN.
RULE 33: For the FMAXM.Q instruction, when both source operands are not NaN, the destination register must contain the greater of the two operands according to the IEEE 754-2019 maximum operation.
RULE 34: For the FMAXM.Q instruction, if either source operand is a NaN, the destination register must be written with the canonical NaN.
RULE 35: All FMAXM.* instructions must implement the IEEE 754-2019 maximum operation, including the defined handling of signed zeros, infinities, and other special cases.
RULE 36: The MAX instruction is only available when the Zbb extension is implemented.
RULE 37: Executing the MAX instruction writes to rd the greater of the integer values held in rs1 and rs2.
RULE 38: The MAX instruction does not generate any architecturally defined exceptions or permission checks.
RULE 39: The MAX instruction returns the larger of the two source register values.
