RULE 40: The MAX operation performed by the vfredmax.vs instruction is a vector single-width floating-point reduction.
RULE 41: The MAX operation computes the maximum value among the elements of the two source vectors specified as vs2 and vs1.
RULE 42: The MAX operation writes the resulting maximum value to the destination vector register specified as vd.
RULE 43: The MAX operation is applied only to element positions for which the vector mask vm is true.
RULE 44: The MAX operation processes the elements of the source vectors as single-precision floating-point values.
RULE 45: The scalar MIN/MAX instruction must not be affected by the Zvkt extension and must perform its defined maximum operation semantics unchanged.
RULE 46: The vector vmax[u].v[vx] instruction must not be affected by the Zvkt extension and must perform its defined maximum operation semantics unchanged.

INSTRUCTION: MAXU
FORMAT: maxu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: MAXU computes the unsigned maximum of the values in source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes the selected value to destination register rd.
RULE 2: An implementation that includes the Zbb (Basic bit-manipulation) extension MUST provide the MAXU instruction for both RV32 and RV64 base ISAs.
RULE 3: The MAXU instruction MUST read the unsigned integer values from registers rs1 and rs2, compare them, and write the larger of the two values to register rd.
RULE 4: If the unsigned values in rs1 and rs2 are equal, the MAXU instruction MUST write that equal value to rd.
RULE 5: The MAXU instruction MUST be implemented in both RV32 and RV64 configurations and produces a result with the native XLEN width of the executing hart.
RULE 6: Execution of the MAXU instruction MUST not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 7: MAXU reads the architectural value of source register rs1 as an unsigned integer rs1_val.
RULE 8: MAXU reads the architectural value of source register rs2 as an unsigned integer rs2_val.
RULE 9: MAXU compares rs1_val and rs2_val using an unsigned less-than comparison.
RULE 10: If the unsigned comparison determines that rs1_val is less than rs2_val, MAXU writes rs2_val into destination register rd.
RULE 11: If the unsigned comparison determines that rs1_val is not less than rs2_val, MAXU writes rs1_val into destination register rd.
RULE 12: MAXU does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 13: For the MAXU reduction instruction, the source vector register vs1, the source vector register vs2, and the destination vector register vd must all have the same selected element width (SEW).
RULE 14: The MAXU reduction instruction computes the unsigned maximum of the element vs1[0] and every element of vs2, and writes that unsigned maximum into the lowest element vd[0] of the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: MIN
FORMAT: min rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: A MIN instruction atomically loads the data value from the address in rs1, writes the loaded value to rd, applies the signed or unsigned integer minimum binary operator to the loaded value and the original rs2 value, and stores the resulting value back to the address in rs1.
RULE 2: For the signed-integer variant of MIN (AMOMIN), the binary operator used by the instruction is the signed integer minimum of the loaded value and the original rs2 value.
RULE 3: For the unsigned-integer variant of MIN (AMOMINU), the binary operator used by the instruction is the unsigned integer minimum of the loaded value and the original rs2 value.
RULE 4: When a 32-bit MIN instruction executes on RV64, the value placed in rd is sign-extended to 64 bits and the upper 32 bits of the original rs2 value are ignored.
RULE 5: The address held in rs1 for a MIN instruction must be naturally aligned to the operand size (four-byte aligned for word operations and eight-byte aligned for doubleword operations) unless the accessed bytes are all contained within a single misaligned atomicity granule.
RULE 6: If the address in rs1 for a MIN instruction is not naturally aligned and the accessed bytes cross a misaligned atomicity granule boundary, the implementation must raise either an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception.
RULE 7: If the address in rs1 for a MIN instruction is not naturally aligned but all accessed bytes lie within the same misaligned atomicity granule, the implementation must not raise an alignment exception and must execute the instruction atomically as a single memory operation.
RULE 8: If the aq bit of a MIN instruction is set, then no later memory operations in the same hart may be observed to take place before that MIN instruction.
RULE 9: If the rl bit of a MIN instruction is set, then other harts must not observe the MIN instruction before memory accesses that precede it in the issuing hart.
RULE 10: If both aq and rl bits of a MIN instruction are set, the operation must be sequentially consistent.
RULE 11: A MIN instruction may raise the same memory-access exceptions (such as page-fault or access-fault exceptions) as other atomic memory operations because it performs a load-modify-store sequence.
RULE 12: The byte-and halfword signed MIN AMO must sign-extend the value placed in the destination register rd to the XLEN width.
RULE 13: The signed MIN AMO must ignore the bits of the source register rs2 and use only the memory value for the comparison.
RULE 14: If the address supplied to the signed MIN AMO is not naturally aligned, the implementation must invoke the same exception handling defined for misaligned A-extension AMOs.
RULE 15: FMIN.S must write to rd the value that is smaller of the two source registers rs1 and rs2 according to the comparison semantics defined for this instruction.
RULE 16: For the purposes of FMIN.S the value –0.0 is considered less than the value +0.0.
RULE 17: If both rs1 and rs2 are NaNs, FMIN.S must produce the canonical NaN as the result written to rd.
RULE 18: If exactly one of rs1 or rs2 is a NaN, FMIN.S must produce the non-NaN operand as the result written to rd.
RULE 19: If any operand of FMIN.S is a signaling NaN, the invalid operation exception flag must be set even when the result written to rd is not a NaN.
RULE 20: FMIN.S implements the IEEE 754-201x minimumNumber operation semantics rather than the IEEE 754-2008 minNum semantics, including the specified handling of signaling NaNs.
RULE 21: MIN.S must return the IEEE 754-2019 minimum of the two single-precision source operands when neither operand is NaN.
RULE 22: MIN.S must return the canonical NaN when either single-precision source operand is NaN.
RULE 23: When the D extension is implemented, MIN.D must return the IEEE 754-2019 minimum of the two double-precision source operands when neither operand is NaN.
RULE 24: When the D extension is implemented, MIN.D must return the canonical NaN when either double-precision source operand is NaN.
RULE 25: When the Zfh extension is implemented, MIN.H must return the IEEE 754-2019 minimum of the two half-precision source operands when neither operand is NaN.
RULE 26: When the Zfh extension is implemented, MIN.H must return the canonical NaN when either half-precision source operand is NaN.
RULE 27: When the Q extension is implemented, MIN.Q must return the IEEE 754-2019 minimum of the two quad-precision source operands when neither operand is NaN.
RULE 28: When the Q extension is implemented, MIN.Q must return the canonical NaN when either quad-precision source operand is NaN.
RULE 29: An implementation that includes the Zbb (Basic bit-manipulation) extension MUST implement the MIN instruction.
RULE 30: In RV32 implementations the MIN instruction is defined and must execute when encoded.
RULE 31: In RV64 implementations the MIN instruction is defined and must execute when encoded.
RULE 32: The MIN instruction computes the signed integer minimum of the values in source registers rs1 and rs2 and writes that result to destination register rd.
RULE 33: MIN rd, rs1, rs2 must compute the signed smaller of the two XLEN-bit operand values and write that result to rd.
RULE 34: The MIN instruction MUST write to rd the smaller of the signed integer values read from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 35: The MIN instruction performs the comparison between rs1 and rs2 using signed less-than semantics.
RULE 36: When the signed value in rs1 is less than the signed value in rs2, the MIN instruction MUST write the rs1 value to rd.
RULE 37: When the signed value in rs1 is not less than the signed value in rs2, including the case of equality, the MIN instruction MUST write the rs2 value to rd.
RULE 38: The signed integer MIN instruction must be provided in a vector-vector form with the mnemonic vmin.vv, taking source vector registers vs2 and vs1 and writing the result to destination vector register vd.
RULE 39: The signed integer MIN instruction must be provided in a vector-scalar form with the mnemonic vmin.vx, taking source vector register vs2, scalar register rs1, and writing the result to destination vector register vd.
RULE 40: The unsigned integer MIN instruction must be provided in a vector-vector form with the mnemonic vminu.vv, taking source vector registers vs2 and vs1 and writing the result to destination vector register vd.
RULE 41: The unsigned integer MIN instruction must be provided in a vector-scalar form with the mnemonic vminu.vx, taking source vector register vs2, scalar register rs1, and writing the result to destination vector register vd.
RULE 42: The MIN instruction computes the minimumNumber of its two floating-point source operands and writes the selected operand value to the destination register.
RULE 43: The MIN instruction must exhibit the same architectural behavior as defined for the scalar floating-point MIN instruction in version 2.2 of the RISC-V F/D/Q extension.
RULE 44: The MIN instruction must use the same SEW width for every source operand and for its result.
RULE 45: The MIN reduction must compute the signed-integer minimum of the scalar element vs1[0] and the set of active elements of vector register vs2.
RULE 46: The MIN reduction must write the computed signed-integer minimum into the destination element vd[0].
RULE 47: The MIN instruction reduces the scalar argument in vs1[0] together with all active elements in vs2 by applying the minimumNumber operation.
RULE 48: The MIN reduction must produce the same final value and raise the same exception flags as a floating-point MAX reduction would, regardless of the order in which elements are processed.
RULE 49: When the MIN reduction has no active elements, it must copy the scalar in vs1[0] to the destination register unchanged, must not canonicalize NaN values, and must not set any exception flags.
RULE 50: The MIN instruction must be naturally aligned and may be executed only on memory locations whose width is supported for reads and writes by the underlying memory region.

INSTRUCTION: MINU
FORMAT: minu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: MINU must be defined in the Zbb basic bit-manipulation extension and must be available in implementations that support either RV32 or RV64 XLEN.
RULE 2: MINU must interpret the values in registers rs1 and rs2 as unsigned integers and must select the smaller of the two values.
RULE 3: MINU must write the selected unsigned minimum value to the destination register rd.
RULE 4: MINU must perform a full-XLEN operation on its source operands and must produce a full-XLEN result written to rd.
RULE 5: When a MINU instruction is executed, the hardware must interpret the contents of rs1 and rs2 as unsigned integers.
RULE 6: A MINU instruction must write to rd the unsigned integer that is less than or equal to both unsigned values read from rs1 and rs2.
RULE 7: If the unsigned values in rs1 and rs2 are equal, a MINU instruction must write that equal value to rd.
RULE 8: MINU must read the unsigned integer value from architectural register rs1 and assign it to an internal variable rs1_val.
RULE 9: MINU must read the unsigned integer value from architectural register rs2 and assign it to an internal variable rs2_val.
RULE 10: MINU must compare rs1_val and rs2_val using unsigned less-than semantics.
RULE 11: MINU must set the internal result to rs1_val if rs1_val is less than rs2_val under unsigned comparison, otherwise it must set the result to rs2_val.
RULE 12: MINU must write the internal result to architectural register rd.
RULE 13: MINU must not alter any architectural state other than the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: MNRET
FORMAT: mnret
RULE 1: When the MNRET instruction is executed and the privilege mode that becomes active after the instruction is not Machine mode, the MDT bit is set to 0.
RULE 2: When the MNRET instruction is executed and the privilege mode that becomes active after the instruction is User mode, Virtual Supervisor mode, or Virtual User mode, the SDT bit in sstatus is set to 0.
RULE 3: When the MNRET instruction is executed and the privilege mode that becomes active after the instruction is Virtual User mode, the SDT bit in vsstatus is set to 0.
RULE 4: MNRET MUST be implemented as an instruction by any core that implements the Smrnmi extension.
RULE 5: Execution of MNRET MUST cause the processor to resume execution from the RNMI handler.
RULE 6: When IALIGN is 32, the implicit read of the mnepc CSR performed by the MNRET instruction returns a value in which bit mnepc[1] is masked to 0.
RULE 7: When IALIGN is 32, a write to bit mnepc[1] updates the stored mnepc value, but the MNRET instruction will still read that bit as 0 because the read is masked.
RULE 8: MNRET MUST be executed only when the current privilege mode is M, and executing MNRET in any other privilege mode is treated as an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 9: MNRET sets the program counter to the value in the mnepc CSR and restores the privilege mode and virtualization mode of the interrupted context from the appropriate fields in the mnstatus CSR.
RULE 10: MNRET sets the NMIE bit in the mnstatus CSR.
RULE 11: If MNRET changes the privilege mode to a mode that is less privileged than M, MNRET clears the MPRV bit in the mstatus CSR.
RULE 12: If the Zicfilp extension is implemented and MNRET selects privilege mode y as the new mode, MNRET writes to the ELP CSR the logical AND of the y LPE CSR and the MNPELP field of mnstatus.
RULE 13: When the MNRET instruction is executed, it must set the program counter to the value stored in the mnepc CSR.
RULE 14: When the MNRET instruction is executed, it must restore the current privilege mode to the value encoded in the mnstatus CSR.
RULE 15: When the MNRET instruction is executed, it must set the NMIE bit in the mnstatus CSR, thereby re-enabling interrupts.

INSTRUCTION: MRET
FORMAT: mret
RULE 1: MRET returns from a trap that was taken into machine mode (M-mode).
RULE 2: When MRET is executed, MIE is set to the value of MPIE.
RULE 3: When MRET is executed, the current privilege mode is changed to the privilege mode stored in MPP.
RULE 4: When MRET is executed, MPIE is set to 1.
RULE 5: When MRET is executed, MPP is set to the least-privileged supported mode, which is U-mode if U-mode is implemented otherwise M-mode.
RULE 6: When MRET is executed and the privilege mode stored in MPP is not M, MRET also sets MPRV to 0.
RULE 7: When MRET is executed in M-mode, MRET MUST set the MDT bit to 0.
RULE 8: If the privilege mode entered after MRET is U, VS, or VU, MRET MUST set sstatus.SDT to 0.
RULE 9: If the privilege mode entered after MRET is VU, MRET MUST set vsstatus.SDT to 0.
RULE 10: When an MRET instruction changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than machine mode, the implementation must set MPRV to 0.
RULE 11: When a machine-mode handler executes the MRET instruction, the trap is redirected back to the privilege level that was active before the trap.
RULE 12: When IALIGN=32, the implementation MUST mask bit mepc[1] on all reads so that the read value of mepc[1] appears as 0, and this masking MUST also apply to the implicit read of mepc performed by the MRET instruction.
RULE 13: When IALIGN=32, the implementation MUST permit writes to bit mepc[1] even though reads of that bit are masked, and the writability of mepc[1] is unaffected by execution of the MRET instruction.
RULE 14: MRET MUST be provided in every RISC-V implementation.
RULE 15: MRET may be executed when the current privilege mode is machine.
RULE 16: If MRET is executed in a privilege mode that is less privileged than machine, an illegal-instruction exception MUST be raised.
RULE 17: When MRET is executed, after performing the privilege-stack manipulation defined in Section 3.1.6.1, the program counter is set to the value stored in the mepc register.
RULE 18: MRET execution must not be influenced by the current value of the mnstatus.NMIE bit.
RULE 19: MRET execution must leave the mnstatus.NMIE bit unchanged.
RULE 20: When a double trap originates in VS-mode, M-mode SHOULD execute an MRET to resume execution at the address contained in the stvec CSR.
RULE 21: When an MRET instruction is executed and the MPP field does not equal 3, the virtualization mode V MUST be set to the value of the MPV bit.
RULE 22: When an MRET instruction is executed and the MPP field equals 3, the virtualization mode V MUST be set to 0.
RULE 23: MRET MUST determine the new privilege mode by evaluating the MPP and MPV fields in mstatus or mstatush according to their defined encoding.
RULE 24: MRET MUST set MPV to 0 in mstatus or mstatush.
RULE 25: MRET MUST set MPP to 0 in mstatus or mstatush.
RULE 26: MRET MUST set MIE to the previous value of MPIE in mstatus or mstatush.
RULE 27: MRET MUST set MPIE to 1 in mstatus or mstatush.
RULE 28: MRET MUST set the privilege mode to the mode previously determined from MPP and MPV.
RULE 29: MRET MUST set the program counter to the value stored in mepc.
RULE 30: When MRET is executed and the new privilege mode after the return has its LPE bit equal to 1, the effective privilege level (ELP) MUST be set to the value of the machine-mode PELP bit.
RULE 31: When MRET is executed and the new privilege mode after the return has its LPE bit equal to 0, the effective privilege level (ELP) MUST be set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.
RULE 32: After MRET sets ELP, the machine-mode PELP bit MUST be set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.

INSTRUCTION: MUL
FORMAT: mul rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: An implementation that declares support for the Zmmul extension MUST provide the MUL instruction.
RULE 2: The binary encoding of the MUL instruction in the Zmmul extension MUST be identical to the binary encoding of the MUL instruction defined in the M extension.
RULE 3: Any implementation that supports the M extension MUST also support the MUL instruction via the Zmmul extension.
RULE 4: The MUL operation provided by the compressed instruction c.mul is only implemented when the processor implements the M extension or the Zmmul extension.
RULE 5: The MUL operation performed by c.mul is defined for both RV32 and RV64 base ISAs.
RULE 6: The MUL operation executed by c.mul computes the lower XLEN bits of the product of the values in the source registers and writes that result to the destination register, identical to the architectural effect of the standard MUL instruction.
RULE 7: If neither the M extension nor the Zmmul extension is implemented, the c.mul encoding for the MUL operation is reserved and its behavior is not defined by the specification.
RULE 8: The MUL instruction is defined only when the implementation provides either the M extension or the Zmmul extension and those extensions are enabled.
RULE 9: The MUL instruction treats the XLEN-bit values in rs1' and rs2' as signed two's complement integers and multiplies them to produce a 2 × XLEN-bit product.
RULE 10: The MUL instruction writes the low XLEN bits of the 2 × XLEN-bit product to the destination register rd'.
RULE 11: The high XLEN bits of the product computed by the MUL instruction are discarded and have no architectural effect.
RULE 12: The result of the MUL instruction is written to the destination register atomically as a single architectural update.

INSTRUCTION: MULH
FORMAT: mulh rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: An implementation that implements the Zmmul extension MUST provide the MULH instruction.
RULE 2: The binary encoding of the MULH instruction in a Zmmul implementation MUST be identical to the binary encoding defined for MULH in the M extension.
RULE 3: If an implementation includes the M extension, then it MUST also include the Zmmul extension, and therefore the MULH instruction MUST be available.
RULE 4: The MULH instruction supplied by the Zmmul extension MUST exhibit the same architectural behavior as the MULH instruction defined in the M extension.
RULE 5: Implementations of the RV32 and RV64 base integer ISAs MUST support the MULH instruction.

INSTRUCTION: MULHSU
FORMAT: mulhsu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: MULHSU MUST interpret the value in rs1 as a signed XLEN-bit integer and the value in rs2 as an unsigned XLEN-bit integer, multiply them to form a full 2 × XLEN-bit product.
RULE 2: MULHSU MUST write the upper XLEN bits of the full 2 × XLEN-bit product to the destination register, discarding the lower XLEN bits.
RULE 3: When performing multi-word signed multiplication, using MULHSU with the signed most-significant word of the multiplicand in rs1 and an unsigned less-significant word of the multiplier in rs2 yields the upper XLEN bits of the product.

INSTRUCTION: MULHU
FORMAT: mulhu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: MULHU MUST return the upper XLEN bits of the full 2 × XLEN-bit product generated by unsigned multiplication of the values in rs1 and rs2.
RULE 2: In a MULHU instruction the destination register must not be the same as either source register, and the source register specifiers must appear in the same order as in the corresponding MUL instruction.
RULE 3: An implementation that includes the Zmmul extension MUST provide the MULHU instruction.
RULE 4: MULHU performs an unsigned multiplication of the values in rs1 and rs2 and does not perform division or remainder operations.

INSTRUCTION: MULW
FORMAT: mulw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: In RV64, MULW MUST multiply the low-order 32 bits of the first source register with the low-order 32 bits of the second source register.
RULE 2: MULW MUST ignore all bits above bit 31 of both source registers when performing the multiplication.
RULE 3: MULW MUST produce a 32-bit product from the multiplication of the low-order 32 bits of the source registers.
RULE 4: MULW MUST sign-extend the low-order 32 bits of the product to 64 bits and write that value to the destination register.
RULE 5: MULW MUST not modify any architectural state other than the destination register.

INSTRUCTION: NTL.P1
FORMAT: ntl.p1
RULE 1: Execution of NTL.P1 MUST NOT modify any architectural state except that it advances the program counter to the next instruction and updates any applicable performance counters.
RULE 2: After NTL.P1 is executed, the program counter is incremented to point to the following instruction.
RULE 3: NTL.P1 is subject to the same permission checks and exception conditions that apply to all HINT instructions.
RULE 4: No exceptions or side-effects beyond those defined for generic HINT instructions are specified for NTL.P1.

INSTRUCTION: OR
FORMAT: or rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The OR instruction is defined in the RV32I base integer ISA as a register-register (R-type) operation that works on integer registers.
RULE 2: When an OR instruction executes, the architectural registers identified by rs1 and rs2 are read as source operands and the computed result is written to the architectural register identified by rd.
RULE 3: The OR operation is selected for execution when the funct7 and funct3 fields of the instruction encoding specify the OR function.
RULE 4: The OR instruction performs a bitwise logical OR of the two source register values, producing a result in which each bit is 1 if the corresponding bit of either source operand is 1, and 0 otherwise.
RULE 5: The OR instruction computes rd = rs1 | rs2, where each bit of the result is 1 if the corresponding bit of rs1 or rs2 is 1, and writes the result to rd.
RULE 6: When the pseudo-instruction czero.nez or is executed, the OR instruction is conditionally performed such that if rc equals zero then rd receives rs1 | rs2, otherwise rd retains its previous architectural value.
RULE 7: When the pseudo-instruction czero.eqz or is executed, the OR instruction is conditionally performed such that if rc is not equal to zero then rd receives rs1 | rs2, otherwise rd retains its previous architectural value.
RULE 8: The pseudo-instruction czero.nez or expands into a two-instruction sequence that executes an OR rd, rs2, rc followed by an OR rd, rs1, rd.
RULE 9: The pseudo-instruction czero.eqz or expands into a two-instruction sequence that executes an OR rd, rs2, rc followed by an OR rd, rs1, rd.
RULE 10: The AMOOR instruction atomically loads a value from the address in rs1, writes the loaded value to rd, computes the bitwise OR of the loaded value and the original value in rs2, and stores the OR result back to the same address in rs1.
RULE 11: The address in rs1 used by the AMOOR instruction must be naturally aligned to the operand size; if it is not, an address-misaligned exception OR an access-fault exception is generated.
RULE 12: For RV64 implementations, a 32-bit AMOOR operation sign-extends the value placed in rd and ignores the upper 32 bits of rs2.
RULE 13: If the aq bit of an AMOOR instruction is set, no later memory operations in the same hart may be observed to occur before the AMOOR.
RULE 14: If the rl bit of an AMOOR instruction is set, other harts may not observe the AMOOR before memory accesses that precede the AMOOR in the same hart.
RULE 15: If both aq and rl bits of an AMOOR instruction are set, the operation is sequentially consistent and cannot be reordered with earlier or later memory operations from the same hart.
RULE 16: The AMOOR.B/H instruction performs a bitwise OR on the accessed memory operand.
RULE 17: For AMOOR.B/H the OR result placed in rd is sign-extended from the accessed byte or half-word and the original bits of rs2 are ignored.
RULE 18: The AMOOR.B/H instruction OR requires the address in rs1 to be naturally aligned to the operand size, and a misaligned address triggers the same exception behavior defined for the base A-extension atomic instructions.
RULE 19: When the aq or rl bits are set, the AMOOR.B/H instruction OR may provide release-consistency semantics as defined for the A-extension.
RULE 20: If the Zbb extension is implemented, the ORN rd, rs1, rs2 instruction must be available in both RV32 and RV64 configurations.
RULE 21: The ORN instruction computes the destination register as the bitwise OR of rs1 with the bitwise NOT of rs2.
RULE 22: If the Zbb extension is implemented, the ORC.B rd, rs instruction must be available in both RV32 and RV64 configurations.
RULE 23: The ORC.B instruction performs a byte-granular bitwise OR-combine operation on the source register and writes the result to the destination register.
RULE 24: The ORC.B instruction MUST set each byte of the destination register rd to all zeros if the corresponding byte of the source register rs contains no bits set to one, and MUST set that byte to all ones if any bit of the corresponding source byte is one.
RULE 25: The ORC.B instruction reads the full XLEN-bit source register rs and writes the XLEN-bit result to rd, applying the bytewise zero-or-one rule independently to every byte of the register.
RULE 26: The vor.vv instruction performs an element-wise bitwise OR of each pair of corresponding elements from source vector registers vs2 and vs1 and writes the resulting elements to the destination vector register vd.
RULE 27: The vor.vx instruction performs an element-wise bitwise OR of each element of source vector register vs2 with the XLEN-wide value held in integer register rs1 and writes the resulting elements to the destination vector register vd.
RULE 28: The vor.vi instruction performs an element-wise bitwise OR of each element of source vector register vs2 with the sign-extended immediate imm and writes the resulting elements to the destination vector register vd.
RULE 29: For any vor.* instruction, if the vector mask vm disables a particular element, the bitwise OR result for that element is not written and the corresponding element in the destination vector register vd retains its prior value.
RULE 30: All vor.* instructions treat the operation as a pure bitwise logical OR and do not raise architectural exceptions other than those defined for illegal encodings.
RULE 31: The element 0 of the destination vector register vd MUST be written with the bitwise OR of the element 0 of source register vs1 and the reduction OR of all active elements of source register vs2.
RULE 32: The reduction OR of source register vs2 is computed by applying a bitwise OR across every element of vs2 that is active according to the vector mask vm.
RULE 33: The SEW for the OR operation and for all source and destination registers MUST be identical.
RULE 34: For each element index i, the vmor.mm instruction writes the destination mask bit vd.mask[i] to the logical OR of the corresponding source mask bits vs2.mask[i] and vs1.mask[i].
RULE 35: The vmor.mm instruction reads its two source operands as mask registers, and the logical OR is performed on the mask bits of those registers.
RULE 36: Each element participating in the vmor.mm logical OR operation is a single-bit mask element, and this behavior is independent of the vlmul setting in vtype.
RULE 37: Execution of the vmor.mm logical OR instruction must not modify the vlmul field.
RULE 38: The destination register of the vmor.mm logical OR may be the same register as either source register without affecting the correctness of the operation.
RULE 39: Elements with indices less than the current vstart are not modified by the vmor.mm logical OR, and the vstart register is reset to zero after the instruction completes.
RULE 40: The vmor.mm logical OR instruction is always unmasked, meaning all elements are active for the OR operation and encodings with vm=0 are reserved.
RULE 41: Mask elements whose indices are greater than or equal to the vector length (tail elements) are updated by the vmor.mm logical OR according to the tail-agnostic policy.
RULE 42: The truth table for vmor.mm confirms that the logical OR of the two input mask bits yields an output of 1 whenever either input bit is 1, and 0 only when both input bits are 0.
RULE 43: The architectural effect of c.or MUST be identical to the base-integer OR instruction defined in RVI, performing a bitwise OR of the source registers and writing the result to the destination register.
RULE 44: The VREDOR.VS instruction MUST NOT be affected by the Zvkt extension.
RULE 45: An implementation that signals support for the AMOLogical class MUST implement the atomic memory operation OR (amoor).

INSTRUCTION: ORC.B
FORMAT: orc.b rd, rs1
RULE 1: ORC.B MUST read the architectural value of the source register rs and consider only the least-significant 8 bits of that value, ignoring all more-significant bits.
RULE 2: ORC.B MUST perform the defined bitwise OR-Combine operation on the least-significant 8 bits of the source register to produce an 8-bit intermediate result.
RULE 3: ORC.B MUST write an XLEN-wide value to the destination register rd that is either sign-extended or zero-extended from the 8-bit intermediate result, as specified for the instruction.
RULE 4: ORC.B MUST not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 5: ORC.B MUST set each byte of the destination register rd to all zeros when the corresponding byte of the source register rs contains no bits that are set to one.
RULE 6: ORC.B MUST set each byte of the destination register rd to all ones when the corresponding byte of the source register rs contains at least one bit that is set to one.
RULE 7: ORC.B reads the current XLEN-bit value of source register rs.
RULE 8: ORC.B computes an XLEN-bit output where for each byte the output byte is 0x00 if the corresponding byte of the value read from rs is zero, otherwise the output byte is 0xFF.
RULE 9: ORC.B writes the computed XLEN-bit output to destination register rd.
RULE 10: ORC.B does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: ORI
FORMAT: ori rd, rs1, imm12
RULE 1: The ORI instruction MUST sign-extend the 12-bit immediate to the processor's XLEN before performing the operation.
RULE 2: The ORI instruction MUST compute the bitwise OR of the sign-extended immediate and the value in source register rs1.
RULE 3: The ORI instruction MUST write the result of that bitwise OR into destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: ORN
FORMAT: orn rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The ORN instruction MUST compute the bitwise OR of the value in source register rs1 with the bitwise complement of the value in source register rs2 and write the resulting value to destination register rd.
RULE 2: The ORN instruction is defined for RV32 implementations and produces a 32-bit result written to rd.
RULE 3: The ORN instruction is defined for RV64 implementations and produces a 64-bit result written to rd.
RULE 4: Both RV32 and RV64 implementations must support the ORN instruction.
RULE 5: When an ORN instruction executes, the architectural result written to rd equals the bitwise OR of the value in rs1 and the bitwise NOT of the value in rs2.
RULE 6: The ORN instruction is available in RV32 implementations that support the Zbkb extension.
RULE 7: The ORN instruction is available in RV64 implementations that support the Zbkb extension.
RULE 8: Executing ORN with source registers rs1 and rs2 produces a destination register value equal to the bitwise OR of rs1 and the bitwise complement of rs2.
RULE 9: ORN rd, rs1, rs2 must compute the bitwise logical OR of the XLEN-bit value in rs1 and the bitwise inversion of the XLEN-bit value in rs2, and must write the XLEN-bit result to rd.
RULE 10: ORN must read the XLEN-bit values from registers rs1 and rs2 and must write the resulting XLEN-bit value to register rd without modifying any other architectural state.
RULE 11: The ORN instruction must compute the bitwise logical OR of the value in source register rs1 and the bitwise inversion of the value in source register rs2, and must write the resulting word to destination register rd.
RULE 12: An implementation that includes the RVB (Bitmanip) extension MUST provide the ORN instruction for both RV32 and RV64 base ISAs.
RULE 13: The ORN instruction MUST compute the bitwise OR of the first source register and the bitwise NOT of the second source register and write the XLEN-wide result to the destination register.
RULE 14: Implementations that support the Bit-manipulation extension must define the ORN instruction for both RV32 and RV64 base ISA variants.
RULE 15: The ORN instruction must read the value from source register rs1, compute the bitwise NOT of the value in source register rs2, perform a bitwise OR of these two values, and write the resulting value to destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: PACK
FORMAT: pack rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The PACK instruction MUST be defined only for RV64-based implementations.
RULE 2: The PACK instruction packs the low 16 bits of rs1 and the low 16 bits of rs2 into the 32-least-significant bits of rd and sign-extends the 32-bit result into the upper bits of rd.
RULE 3: When the PACK instruction executes, it extracts lo_half = X(rs1)[15..0] and hi_half = X(rs2)[15..0] and writes X(rd) = EXTS(hi_half @ lo_half).
RULE 4: On RV64, executing the PACK instruction with rs2 set to x0 yields the same architectural result as the ZEXT.H instruction.
RULE 5: Any RV64 implementation that provides the PACK instruction also provides the ZEXT.H instruction.
RULE 6: The PACK instruction reads the lower XLEN/2 bits of the source register rs1 and treats them as the low half of the result.
RULE 7: The PACK instruction reads the lower XLEN/2 bits of the source register rs2 and treats them as the high half of the result.
RULE 8: The PACK instruction concatenates the high half taken from rs2 with the low half taken from rs1, forming a bit-vector of length XLEN.
RULE 9: The PACK instruction writes to rd the zero-extended value of the concatenated XLEN-bit vector, leaving all other architectural state unchanged.
RULE 10: PACK concatenates the low half of the first source register with the low half of the second source register to form the destination register value.

INSTRUCTION: PACKH
FORMAT: packh rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: An implementation that includes the Zbkb extension MUST implement the PACKH instruction for RV32.
RULE 2: An implementation that includes the Zbkb extension MUST implement the PACKH instruction for RV64.
RULE 3: The PACKH instruction packs the low-order bytes of its source registers into the destination register.
RULE 4: PACKH MUST read the least-significant byte (bits 7:0) of the source register rs1.
RULE 5: PACKH MUST read the least-significant byte (bits 7:0) of the source register rs2.
RULE 6: PACKH MUST write to bits 15:8 of the destination register rd the byte taken from bits 7:0 of rs2 and write to bits 7:0 of rd the byte taken from bits 7:0 of rs1.
RULE 7: PACKH MUST set all bits of rd above bit 15 (bits XLEN-1:16) to zero, thereby zero-extending the packed 16-bit result.
RULE 8: PACKH is defined in the Zbkb extension, which is ratified and requires a minimum ISA version of v1.0.0-rc4.
RULE 9: PACKH extracts the least-significant byte of the source register rs1 and assigns it to the low-order 8 bits of the destination register rd.
RULE 10: PACKH extracts the least-significant byte of the source register rs2 and assigns it to bits 15-8 of the destination register rd.
RULE 11: PACKH concatenates the extracted byte from rs2 as the high half and the extracted byte from rs1 as the low half to form a 16-bit value that becomes the least-significant 16 bits of rd.
RULE 12: PACKH zero-extends the 16-bit result so that all bits of rd above bit 15 are cleared to zero.
RULE 13: The architectural result of PACKH is equivalent to computing EXTZ(hi_half @ lo_half), where hi_half is X(rs2)[7:0] and lo_half is X(rs1)[7:0].
RULE 14: Implementations that support the Zbkb, Zbkc, or Zbkx extension must provide the PACKH instruction for both RV32 and RV64 execution environments.
RULE 15: Executing the PACKH instruction packs the low-order byte of each source register into the destination register according to the architecture-defined packing order.
RULE 16: PACKH is defined only for the RV64 ISA variant and its encoding is reserved for implementations that do not support RV64.

INSTRUCTION: PACKW
FORMAT: packw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: PACKW ignores the upper 32 bits of its source registers rs1 and rs2.
RULE 2: PACKW operates on the least-significant 32 bits of rs1 and rs2 as signed integers.
RULE 3: PACKW produces a 32-bit signed result that is sign-extended to the machine XLEN.
RULE 4: PACKW packs the low 16 bits of rs1 and the low 16 bits of rs2 into a 32-bit result.
RULE 5: PACKW extracts bits 15-0 of the first source register and writes them into bits 15-0 of the destination register.
RULE 6: PACKW extracts bits 15-0 of the second source register and writes them into bits 31-16 of the destination register.
RULE 7: PACKW sets bits 63-32 of the destination register to zero on RV64 implementations.
RULE 8: PACKW on RV64 forms a 32-bit intermediate value by concatenating the low 16 bits of rs2 as bits 31–16 and the low 16 bits of rs1 as bits 15–0.
RULE 9: PACKW on RV64 sign-extends the 32-bit intermediate produced from rs2 and rs1 to the full XLEN width and writes the result to rd.
RULE 10: When rs2 is x0, PACKW on RV64 produces the same architectural result as the ZEXT.H instruction.
RULE 11: PACKW on an RV64 implementation writes to rd a 64-bit value whose lower 32 bits are formed by concatenating the low 16 bits of rs2 as the high half and the low 16 bits of rs1 as the low half, and sign-extends bit 31 of that 32-bit result into the upper 32 bits of rd.
RULE 12: PACKW is defined only for RV64-based systems; executing PACKW on an RV32 implementation results in reserved behavior.
RULE 13: PACKW reads the low 16 bits of rs1 and the low 16 bits of rs2 and does not modify the contents of rs1 or rs2.
RULE 14: PACKW is available only when the Zbkb extension is implemented; if the Zbkb extension is not present, the PACKW encoding is reserved.
RULE 15: PACKW MUST be implemented only for RV64 implementations; its encoding is reserved on RV32 implementations.
RULE 16: When executed on an RV64 implementation, PACKW packs the low 16-bit portions of the source registers into the destination register.
RULE 17: The PACKW instruction is defined for the RV64 base ISA.
RULE 18: The PACKW instruction is not defined for the RV32 base ISA.

INSTRUCTION: PREFETCH.I
FORMAT: prefetch.i imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: The effective address used by PREFETCH.I is calculated by adding the sign-extended 12-bit immediate to the value held in rs1.
RULE 2: The effective address produced by PREFETCH.I is translated to a physical address using the standard address-translation mechanisms before any prefetch action is taken.
RULE 3: Execution of PREFETCH.I must not alter any architecturally visible integer registers, floating-point registers, or memory state; any state change other than a possible instruction-cache prefetch is not architecturally defined.
RULE 4: Because imm[4:0] is required to be zero, the effective address for PREFETCH.I is always aligned on a 32-byte boundary.

INSTRUCTION: REM
FORMAT: rem rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The REM instruction must produce as its result the remainder of the signed integer division of the value in rs1 by the value in rs2.
RULE 2: If the remainder produced by the REM instruction is nonzero, the sign of that remainder must equal the sign of the dividend (the value in rs1).
RULE 3: For signed division, when the division does not overflow, the relationship dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder must hold, where the remainder is the value returned by the REM instruction.
RULE 4: For unsigned division, when the division does not overflow, the relationship dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder must hold, where the remainder is the value returned by the REMU instruction.
RULE 5: If rs2 is zero, the REM instruction must return a remainder equal to the dividend (the value in rs1).
RULE 6: Signed division overflow occurs only when the dividend is the most-negative integer and the divisor is –1; in that overflow case the REM instruction must return a remainder of zero.
RULE 7: When a signed division overflow occurs, the remainder produced by the REM instruction must be zero.

INSTRUCTION: REMU
FORMAT: remu rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: REMU computes the remainder of the unsigned integer division of the value in rs1 (the dividend) by the value in rs2 (the divisor).
RULE 2: For the unsigned division underlying REMU, the dividend equals the divisor multiplied by the unsigned quotient plus the remainder produced by REMU.
RULE 3: If the divisor supplied to REMU (the value in rs2) is zero, REMU produces a remainder equal to the dividend (the value in rs1).
RULE 4: Because unsigned division overflow cannot occur, REMU never encounters an overflow condition and therefore does not raise an overflow exception.
RULE 5: The architectural result of REMU is defined solely by its remainder semantics and is unaffected by any microarchitectural fusion with a preceding DIVU instruction.

INSTRUCTION: REMUW
FORMAT: remuw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: REMUW uses the lower 32 bits of rs1 as the unsigned dividend and the lower 32 bits of rs2 as the unsigned divisor.
RULE 2: REMUW produces a 32-bit remainder that is sign-extended to a full 64-bit register value, regardless of the divisor value.
RULE 3: For all executions of REMUW where unsigned division does not overflow, the remainder value satisfies the identity dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder.
RULE 4: When the unsigned divisor supplied to REMUW is zero, REMUW returns the unsigned dividend as the remainder.
RULE 5: REMUW must not raise an exception or trap for a divide-by-zero condition; it returns the defined remainder value instead.
RULE 6: REMUW has no defined overflow case because unsigned division overflow cannot occur.

INSTRUCTION: REMW
FORMAT: remw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: REMW is an RV64 instruction that computes the signed remainder of the lower 32-bit value of rs1 divided by the lower 32-bit value of rs2, treating both 32-bit values as signed integers.
RULE 2: REMW always sign-extends the 32-bit remainder result to a full 64-bit value before writing it to the destination register.
RULE 3: When the lower 32-bit divisor (rs2) of a REMW execution is zero, REMW produces a remainder equal to the sign-extended lower 32-bit dividend (rs1).
RULE 4: When the lower 32-bit dividend of a REMW execution equals –2³¹ and the lower 32-bit divisor equals –1, REMW produces a zero remainder that is sign-extended to 64 bits.
RULE 5: For all other cases of REMW execution, the produced remainder satisfies dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder, where dividend and divisor are the signed lower 32-bit values of rs1 and rs2 respectively.

INSTRUCTION: REV8
FORMAT: rev8 rd, rs1
RULE 1: An RV32 or RV64 implementation must support the REV8 instruction.
RULE 2: Executing REV8 rd, rs on an RV32 hart must write to rd the 32-bit value whose four bytes are the bytes of rs in reverse order.
RULE 3: Executing REV8 rd, rs on an RV64 hart must write to rd the 64-bit value whose eight bytes are the bytes of rs in reverse order.
RULE 4: REV8 produces a result that is the byte-reversed representation of the architectural value contained in source register rs.
RULE 5: REV8 writes the byte-reversed result into destination register rd.
RULE 6: REV8 leaves the bit pattern within each individual byte unchanged while reversing the order of the bytes in the register.
RULE 7: The REV8 instruction MUST read the XLEN-bit value of the source register rs, reverse the order of its bytes across the full XLEN, and write the resulting XLEN-bit value to the destination register rd.
RULE 8: The REV8 instruction MUST operate on the full XLEN of the source register; no defined architectural behavior exists for reversing only a subset of the register width.
RULE 9: The REV8 instruction MUST NOT modify any architectural state other than writing the reversed byte result to rd.
RULE 10: Software may obtain a 32-bit byte-reversed result by executing REV8 followed by an arithmetic right shift (SRAI) of the destination register by XLEN-32, and may obtain a 16-bit byte-reversed result by executing REV8 followed by SRAI with shift amount XLEN-16.
RULE 11: REV8 MUST produce a destination register value whose XLEN-bit content consists of the bytes of the source register taken in reverse order, so that the most-significant byte of the source becomes the least-significant byte of the destination and vice-versa.
RULE 12: REV8 MUST operate on the full XLEN bits of the source register, reversing exactly XLEN/8 bytes regardless of whether the processor is RV32 or RV64.
RULE 13: REV8 MUST leave all architectural state unchanged except for writing the computed byte-reversed value to the destination register.
RULE 14: An implementation that ratifies the Zbb or Zbkb extension MUST include the REV8 instruction as defined by these extensions.
RULE 15: The REV8 instruction reads a source integer register, reverses the order of its bytes, and writes the resulting value to a destination integer register.
RULE 16: In an RV32 implementation, REV8 reverses the order of the four bytes of the 32-bit source register so that byte 0 becomes byte 3, byte 1 becomes byte 2, byte 2 becomes byte 1, and byte 3 becomes byte 0 in the destination register.
RULE 17: In an RV64 implementation, REV8 reverses the order of the eight bytes of the 64-bit source register so that byte 0 becomes byte 7, byte 1 becomes byte 6, byte 2 becomes byte 5, byte 3 becomes byte 4, byte 4 becomes byte 3, byte 5 becomes byte 2, byte 6 becomes byte 1, and byte 7 becomes byte 0 in the destination register.
RULE 18: The REV8 instruction reverses the order of the eight-bit bytes within the XLEN-bit value in rs1 and writes the resulting XLEN-bit value to rd.
RULE 19: The REV8 instruction reverses the order of bytes within each element of the source vector and writes the resulting elements to the destination vector.
RULE 20: When the vs1 field of a vector instruction encoding has the value 01001, the instruction must be decoded as REV8.

INSTRUCTION: ROL
FORMAT: rol rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: ROL rd, rs1, rs2 rotates left the bit pattern of the value in register rs1 by the amount specified in register rs2 and writes the resulting value to register rd.
RULE 2: ROL MUST be defined and available in both RV32 and RV64 implementations.
RULE 3: ROL MUST compute the rotate distance as the value in rs2 masked to the low log2(XLEN) bits (i.e., rs2 modulo XLEN).
RULE 4: ROL MUST produce a destination value that is the bits of rs1 rotated left by the rotate distance, where each bit is taken from the position (i − rotate_distance) modulo XLEN of the original rs1.
RULE 5: ROL MUST not shift in zeros; bits that are shifted out of the most-significant side of the value must reappear at the least-significant side, implementing a left circular shift.
RULE 6: An RV32 implementation that includes the Zbkb extension MUST implement the ROL instruction.
RULE 7: An RV64 implementation that includes the Zbkb extension MUST implement the ROL instruction.
RULE 8: The ROL instruction rotates the bits of the source register left, with bits shifted out of the most-significant position reappearing in the least-significant position, and writes the rotated value to the destination register.
RULE 9: ROL rd, rs1, rs2 MUST rotate the XLEN-bit value in register rs1 left by a shift amount equal to the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of register rs2.
RULE 10: For a ROL instruction the shift amount shamt MUST be taken as bits [4:0] of rs2 when XLEN = 32 and as bits [5:0] of rs2 when XLEN = 64.
RULE 11: ROL MUST compute the result as (X(rs1) << shamt) OR (X(rs1) >> (XLEN − shamt)) using XLEN-bit unsigned arithmetic.
RULE 12: ROL MUST write the computed result into register rd, overwriting rd's previous architectural value.
RULE 13: The ROL instruction must compute the shift amount shamt from the least-significant log₂(XLEN) bits of rs2, using bits [4:0] when XLEN = 32 and bits [5:0] when XLEN = 64.
RULE 14: The ROL instruction must produce a result equal to (X(rs1) << shamt) | (X(rs1) >> (XLEN - shamt)).
RULE 15: The ROL instruction must write the computed result into the destination register rd.
RULE 16: An RV32 implementation MUST provide the ROL instruction.
RULE 17: An RV64 implementation MUST provide the ROL instruction.
RULE 18: The ROL instruction performs a left rotation of the bits of a register value.
RULE 19: ROL applied to a 32-bit operand produces a 32-bit result whose bits are rotated left by the shift amount reduced modulo 32.
RULE 20: When the shift amount supplied to ROL is a multiple of 32, the result of ROL equals the original 32-bit operand.
RULE 21: For any non-zero shift amount less than 32, ROL moves each bit of the 32-bit operand to a more significant position by that amount, and bits shifted out of the most-significant position reappear at the least-significant positions.
RULE 22: ROL does not modify any architectural state other than delivering its result and does not raise an exception.
RULE 23: The shift operand of ROL is interpreted as an unsigned integer and only its lower 5 bits determine the effective rotation amount.
RULE 24: When the operand of ROL is a 32-bit value, the result is defined to be a 32-bit value regardless of the implementation's native register width.
RULE 25: ROL is defined for any integer shift amount; shift amounts outside the range 0-31 are handled by the modulo 32 reduction rather than being illegal or reserved.
RULE 26: The 32-bit variant ROL (named ROL32) takes a 32-bit operand and an integer shift amount and produces the value of the operand rotated left by that amount.
RULE 27: In the SM4 round function, the ROL32 operation is applied to the 32-bit value S with shift amounts 2, 10, 18, and 24 to contribute to the round computation.

INSTRUCTION: ROLW
FORMAT: rolw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: Implementations that support the Zbb extension MUST provide the ROLW instruction.
RULE 2: Implementations that support the Zbk b extension MUST provide the ROLW instruction.
RULE 3: The ROLW instruction is defined for both RV32 and RV64 base ISAs.
RULE 4: The ROLW instruction MUST ignore the upper 32 bits of rs1 and rs2, using only the least-significant 32 bits of each operand.
RULE 5: The ROLW instruction MUST interpret the least-significant 32 bits of rs1 as a signed 32-bit value for the rotation operation.
RULE 6: The ROLW instruction MUST use the least-significant 32 bits of rs2 as the rotate amount.
RULE 7: The ROLW instruction MUST rotate left the 32-bit signed value from rs1 by the amount specified in the lower 32 bits of rs2.
RULE 8: The ROLW instruction MUST produce a 32-bit signed result that is sign-extended to the processor's XLEN.
RULE 9: The ROLW instruction does NOT invoke any additional privilege checks or exceptions beyond those defined for w-suffixed instructions.
RULE 10: The ROLW instruction rotates the bits of the 32-bit word in rs1 to the left by the amount specified in rs2, inserting into the low-order positions the bits that were shifted out of the high-order positions instead of zeros.
RULE 11: The architectural result of executing ROLW rd, rs1, rs2 is equivalent to the result produced by the four-instruction sequence "neg …; sll …; srl …; or …" that would otherwise be used to achieve the same rotation.
RULE 12: The ROLW instruction is defined only for RV64 implementations and its encoding is reserved on RV32 implementations, providing no defined architectural effect on RV32.
RULE 13: The ROLW instruction rotates left the low 32 bits of the rs1 register by a rotate amount equal to the low 5 bits of the rs2 register.
RULE 14: The rotate amount used by the ROLW instruction is computed as the value of rs2 modulo 32 (i.e., the lower 5 bits of rs2).
RULE 15: During the ROLW operation, bits shifted out of the low 32-bit field are re-introduced on the right side of that field, effecting a circular left shift.
RULE 16: After completing the 32-bit rotation, the ROLW instruction writes the 32-bit result to the destination register rd and sign-extends the result to 64 bits.
RULE 17: If the rotate amount for the ROLW instruction is zero, rd receives the sign-extended low 32 bits of rs1 unchanged.
RULE 18: ROLW rotates left the least-significant 32-bit word of rs1 by a shift amount equal to the value of the least-significant five bits of rs2.
RULE 19: ROLW obtains the shift amount by extracting bits 4 down to 0 of rs2 and ignoring all higher bits.
RULE 20: ROLW computes the rotated value as (rs1_word << shamt) | (rs1_word >> (32-shamt)), where rs1_word is the zero-extended low 32 bits of rs1 and shamt is the extracted shift amount.
RULE 21: When the extracted shift amount for ROLW is zero, the low-order word of rs1 is left unchanged before sign extension.
RULE 22: ROLW sign-extends the 32-bit rotation result by copying bit 31 into all more-significant bits of the destination register.
RULE 23: ROLW writes the sign-extended 32-bit result into rd, replacing the entire XLEN-bit destination register.
RULE 24: ROLW does not modify any architectural state other than the destination register rd.
RULE 25: The ROLW instruction rotates left the least-significant 32-bit word of the source register rs1 by a rotate amount equal to the value of the least-significant 5 bits of the source register rs2.
RULE 26: For the ROLW instruction the rotate amount shamt is obtained by extracting bits 4 down to 0 of X(rs2).
RULE 27: For the ROLW instruction the 32-bit value rs1 used in the rotation is obtained by zero-extending bits 31 down to 0 of X(rs1).
RULE 28: For the ROLW instruction the rotation result is computed as (rs1 << shamt) | (rs1 >> (32 − shamt)) where the shifts are performed on the 32-bit zero-extended rs1 value.
RULE 29: The ROLW instruction writes to rd the 32-bit rotation result sign-extended to XLEN bits by replicating bit 31 of the result into all more-significant bits of X(rd).
RULE 30: In RV64 the ROLW instruction is defined, whereas in RV32 the ROLW encoding is not defined and its behavior is reserved.
RULE 31: The ROLW instruction uses the value read from source register rs1 as the data to be rotated.
RULE 32: The ROLW instruction uses the value read from source register rs2 as the rotation amount.
RULE 33: The ROLW instruction writes the result of rotating the rs1 value left by the rs2 amount into destination register rd.

INSTRUCTION: ROR
FORMAT: ror rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The ROR instruction rotates right the bits of the value held in register rs1 by the amount specified in the low XLEN bits of register rs2, with the rotation count taken modulo XLEN, and writes the resulting value to register rd.
RULE 2: The ROR instruction MUST be implemented in RV32 implementations.
RULE 3: The ROR instruction MUST be implemented in RV64 implementations.
RULE 4: The ROR instruction is defined for both RV32 and RV64 implementations.
RULE 5: ROR rd, rs1, rs2 rotates the bits of the value in rs1 to the right by the amount specified in rs2 and writes the resulting value to rd.
RULE 6: ROR inserts into the vacated high-order bits the bits that were shifted out of the low-order side during the rotation.
RULE 7: ROR performs a circular shift, so bits that exit one end of the operand re-enter at the opposite end.
RULE 8: ROR provides the same architectural effect as a four-instruction sequence that uses neg, shift-left logical, shift-right logical, and bitwise OR to compute the rotated value.
RULE 9: The ROR instruction rotates the bits of a source register value to the right and writes the rotated result to the destination register.
RULE 10: ROR computes a rotate-right of the XLEN-bit value in rs1 by a shift amount taken from the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of rs2.
RULE 11: When XLEN equals 32, ROR extracts bits 4 down to 0 of rs2 to form the shift amount shamt.
RULE 12: When XLEN is greater than 32, ROR extracts bits 5 down to 0 of rs2 to form the shift amount shamt.
RULE 13: ROR calculates the rotation result as (X(rs1) logical right-shifted by shamt) OR (X(rs1) logical left-shifted by (XLEN minus shamt)).
RULE 14: ROR writes the computed rotation result into rd, replacing the previous architectural value of rd.
RULE 15: If the extracted shamt for ROR is zero, the value written to rd is identical to the original value of rs1.
RULE 16: ROR treats the source registers rs1 and rs2 as XLEN-bit unsigned integers for all shift and logical operations.
RULE 17: ROR MUST extract the least-significant 32-bit word of rs1, zero-extend it, and use that value as the operand to be rotated.
RULE 18: ROR MUST extract bits 4 down to 0 of rs2 and use that 5-bit value as the rotation amount (shamt).
RULE 19: ROR MUST compute the rotation result as (rs1 >> shamt) OR (rs1 << (32 - shamt)), where the shifts are logical and rs1 is the zero-extended 32-bit word.
RULE 20: ROR MUST sign-extend the 32-bit rotation result by copying bit 31 of the result into all more-significant bits of the destination register rd.
RULE 21: ROR MUST produce a result equal to the original 32-bit word of rs1 when the extracted rotation amount shamt is zero.
RULE 22: The ROR instruction MUST compute the rotate amount shamt from the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of rs2, using bits 4..0 when XLEN = 32 and bits 5..0 when XLEN = 64.
RULE 23: The ROR instruction MUST produce a result equal to (X(rs1) >> shamt) OR (X(rs1) << (XLEN – shamt)), where the shifts are performed on XLEN-bit values.
RULE 24: The ROR instruction MUST write the computed result into destination register rd.
RULE 25: The ROR instruction MUST rotate right the least-significant 32-bit word of the value in source register rs1 by a rotate amount equal to the unsigned value of the least-significant five bits of source register rs2.
RULE 26: The ROR instruction MUST obtain the rotate amount shamt by extracting bits 4 down to 0 of rs2 and ignoring all higher bits.
RULE 27: The ROR instruction MUST treat the low 32 bits of rs1 as an unsigned operand by zero-extending bits 31 down to 0 of rs1 before performing the rotation.
RULE 28: The ROR instruction MUST compute the 32-bit rotation result as (operand >> shamt) OR (operand << (32 - shamt)), where operand is the zero-extended low 32 bits of rs1 and shamt is the low-5-bit value of rs2.
RULE 29: The ROR instruction MUST write to destination register rd the sign-extended XLEN-bit value obtained by copying bit 31 of the rotation result into all more-significant bits of the destination.
RULE 30: The ROR instruction MUST be architecturally defined only when the Zbb or Zbkb extensions are implemented; otherwise the encoding is reserved.
RULE 31: ROR rotates a 64-bit value taken from a source register right by the immediate rotation amount imm, with bits shifted out on the right re-entering on the left, and produces a 64-bit result.
RULE 32: The sha512sig1 instruction computes X(rd) as the bitwise exclusive-or of ROR of X(rs1) by 19 bits, ROR of X(rs1) by 61 bits, and the logical right shift of X(rs1) by 6 bits.
RULE 33: The sha512sig1 instruction, which includes two ROR operations, must retire with the status RETIRE_SUCCESS.
RULE 34: The ROR instruction rotates the 64-bit value in the source register right by the immediate amount encoded in the instruction and writes the 64-bit rotated result to the destination register.
RULE 35: When the immediate encoded in an ROR instruction is 28, the instruction produces a result equal to the source value rotated right by 28 bit positions.
RULE 36: When the immediate encoded in an ROR instruction is 34, the instruction produces a result equal to the source value rotated right by 34 bit positions.
RULE 37: When the immediate encoded in an ROR instruction is 39, the instruction produces a result equal to the source value rotated right by 39 bit positions.
RULE 38: The sha512sum0 operation computes its destination register as the bitwise exclusive-or of the three 64-bit results obtained by applying ROR with immediates 28, 34, and 39 to the same source register.
RULE 39: Executing ROR rd, rs1, rs2 rotates the bits of the value in rs1 right by the distance given by the low XLEN bits of rs2, with bits shifted out on the right re-entering on the left.
RULE 40: The result of the ROR operation is written to the destination register rd.
RULE 41: The ROR instruction must be defined for RV32 and RV64 implementations and uses the syntax "ror rd, rs1, rs2".
RULE 42: In RV64 implementations only, the 32-bit-word variant RORW must be defined and uses the syntax "rorw rd, rs1, rs2".
RULE 43: The ROR instruction performs a rotate-right operation on vector data.
RULE 44: The ROR instruction rotates each active element of the source vector register vs2 to the right by a rotate amount, producing the result in the destination vector register vd.
RULE 45: In the vector-vector form of the ROR instruction, the rotate amount for element i is taken from the corresponding element i of vector register vs1.
RULE 46: In the vector-scalar form of the ROR instruction, the rotate amount for all elements is taken from the low bits of integer register rs1.
RULE 47: In the vector-immediate form of the ROR instruction, the rotate amount for all elements is taken from the low bits of the 6-bit immediate field uimm[5:0].
RULE 48: For any form of the ROR instruction, only the low log2(SEW) bits of the rotate-amount value are used and all higher bits are ignored.
RULE 49: For any form of the ROR instruction, the rotation is performed on each element using an element width EEW equal to SEW.
RULE 50: For any form of the ROR instruction, the operation is applied to element indices i ranging from vstart up to vl-1 inclusive.
RULE 51: For any form of the ROR instruction, the rotated result of each element is written to the corresponding element i of the destination register vd.
RULE 52: For any form of the ROR instruction, the instruction completes without raising an exception and retires successfully.

INSTRUCTION: RORI
FORMAT: rori rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: RORI is an instruction that is defined only when either the Zbb extension or the Zbk extension is implemented.
RULE 2: RORI operates on the full XLEN-wide values of its registers because it is not a w-suffixed or .uw-suffixed instruction.
RULE 3: RORI rotates the bits of the XLEN-wide value in rs1 to the right by the amount specified in the immediate field shamt and writes the XLEN-wide result to rd.
RULE 4: RORI does not cause any architectural side effects other than writing the rotation result to rd and does not raise any defined exceptions.
RULE 5: The RORI instruction rotates the XLEN-bit value in register rs1 to the right by the amount given in shamt, inserting into the vacated most-significant bits the bits that were shifted out of the least-significant side, thereby performing a circular right shift.
RULE 6: The RORI instruction writes the result of the rotation into the destination register rd.
RULE 7: RORI rotates the bits of the source register right by the immediate amount and writes the rotated value to the destination register.
RULE 8: RORI performs a rotate-right of the value in rs1 by the amount given by the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt field.
RULE 9: For RV32, any encoding of RORI in which shamt[5] equals 1 is reserved.
RULE 10: When XLEN is 32, RORI uses shamt[4:0] as the effective rotate amount.
RULE 11: When XLEN is greater than 32, RORI uses shamt[5:0] as the effective rotate amount.
RULE 12: RORI computes the result as (X(rs1) >> shamt) OR (X(rs1) << (XLEN – shamt)).
RULE 13: RORI writes the computed result into register rd, replacing the previous contents of rd.
RULE 14: RORI determines the effective shift amount by extracting the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt immediate field.
RULE 15: RORI uses shamt[4:0] as the effective shift amount when XLEN = 32.
RULE 16: RORI uses shamt[5:0] as the effective shift amount when XLEN ≠ 32.
RULE 17: RORI computes the result as (X(rs1) >> shamt) | (X(rs1) << (XLEN - shamt)).
RULE 18: RORI writes the computed result into integer register rd.
RULE 19: RORI rotates the bits of the source register right by the immediate amount and writes the rotated result to the destination register.
RULE 20: RORI operates on the native XLEN width of the hart, performing a 32-bit rotation on RV32 implementations and a 64-bit rotation on RV64 implementations.
RULE 21: RORI instruction MUST be defined for RV32 implementations.
RULE 22: RORI instruction MUST be defined for RV64 implementations.

INSTRUCTION: RORIW
FORMAT: roriw rd, rs1, shamt
RULE 1: RORIW must ignore the upper 32 bits of its source register operand when executing.
RULE 2: RORIW must treat the least-significant 32 bits of its source operand as a signed 32-bit integer during the rotation operation.
RULE 3: RORIW must produce a 32-bit signed result and then sign-extend that result to the processor's XLEN.
RULE 4: An implementation that supports the Zbkb extension on RV64 must provide the RORIW instruction, and the RORIW instruction is not defined for RV32.
RULE 5: The architectural effect of the RORIW instruction is to rotate right a 32-bit word operand by an immediate amount.
RULE 6: RORIW must take the rotate amount from the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the shamt operand.
RULE 7: RORIW must operate only on the least-significant 32-bit word of the source register rs1, ignoring all more-significant bits.
RULE 8: RORIW must zero-extend the selected 32-bit word of rs1 to XLEN bits before performing the rotation.
RULE 9: RORIW must compute the rotated 32-bit word as (rs1_data >> shamt) | (rs1_data << (32-shamt)), where rs1_data is the zero-extended 32-bit value and shamt is the effective rotate amount.
RULE 10: RORIW must sign-extend the 32-bit rotation result by copying bit 31 into all more-significant bits and write the resulting XLEN-bit value to rd.
RULE 11: RORIW must treat a rotate amount of zero as producing the original 32-bit word (after sign-extension) in rd.
RULE 12: When RORIW rd, rs1, shamt is executed, the processor extracts bits 31-0 of register rs1, zero-extends that word to XLEN bits, and uses the resulting value as rs1_data.
RULE 13: For RORIW the rotate amount shamt is taken from the least-significant log2(XLEN) bits of the immediate; any higher bits of shamt are ignored.
RULE 14: RORIW computes result = (rs1_data >> shamt) | (rs1_data << (32 - shamt)), where the shifts are performed on the XLEN-bit rs1_data and the shamt value is defined by RULE 2.
RULE 15: RORIW writes the lower 32 bits of result into rd and sign-extends bit 31 of that word to all more-significant bits, so that X(rd) = sign-extend(result[31:0]).
RULE 16: RORIW is only defined for RV64 implementations and has no defined behavior for RV32.
RULE 17: RORIW rotates a 32-bit word taken from the source register right by an immediate shift amount.
RULE 18: The immediate shift amount used by RORIW is a 5-bit value and the rotation is performed modulo 32.
RULE 19: On RV64, execution of the RORIW instruction rotates the bits of the source register rs1 right by the immediate amount imm and writes the rotated value to the destination register rd.
RULE 20: An RV64 implementation must provide the RORIW instruction as defined.

INSTRUCTION: RORW
FORMAT: rorw rd, rs1, rs2
RULE 1: The RORW instruction ignores the upper 32 bits of its source registers and uses only the low 32 bits as input.
RULE 2: The RORW instruction interprets the low 32 bits of each operand as a signed 32-bit integer.
RULE 3: The RORW instruction produces a 32-bit signed result that is sign-extended to the processor's XLEN before being written to the destination register.
RULE 4: The RORW instruction does not cause any architectural side effects, permission checks, or defined exceptions beyond writing its result.
RULE 5: In an RV64 implementation the RORW instruction is defined, while in an RV32 implementation the RORW encoding is not defined.
RULE 6: Execution of the RORW rd, rs1, rs2 instruction rotates the low-word (bits 0-31) of the value in register rs1 right by the amount given by the value in register rs2, with bits shifted out on the right re-entering on the left, and writes the resulting 32-bit value to register rd.
RULE 7: The RORW instruction performs a bitwise rotation rather than a logical shift, so no zero bits are introduced during the operation.
RULE 8: When executed, RORW rotates right the low 32 bits of the first source register by the amount specified in the low 5 bits of the second source register and writes the 32-bit rotated value to the destination register.
RULE 9: An RV64 implementation must support RORW, and an RV32 implementation must treat the RORW encoding as reserved, yielding undefined architectural behavior.
RULE 10: RORW MUST take the lower 32 bits of the value in rs1, zero-extend them to the XLEN width, and use that as the operand for the rotation.
RULE 11: RORW MUST extract bits 4 down to 0 of the value in rs2 to form a 5-bit rotation amount that determines how many positions the operand is rotated right.
RULE 12: RORW MUST compute the rotated value as (operand >> shamt) OR (operand << (32 - shamt)), where operand is the zero-extended 32-bit value and shamt is the extracted rotation amount.
RULE 13: RORW MUST write to rd the sign-extended 32-bit rotated result, replicating bit 31 of the rotated value into all higher bits of the XLEN-wide destination register.
RULE 14: RORW MUST ignore all bits of rs1 above bit 31 when performing the rotation, operating solely on the least-significant word.
RULE 15: RORW MUST treat the rotation amount as modulo 32 because only the low 5 bits of rs2 are used.
RULE 16: RORW MUST, when the rotation amount is zero, produce a result equal to the sign-extended original least-significant word of rs1.
RULE 17: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 rotates right the least-significant 32-bit word of register rs1 by a shift amount equal to the least-significant 5 bits of register rs2.
RULE 18: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 determines the shift amount shamt by extracting bits 4-0 of rs2 and ignoring all higher bits.
RULE 19: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 treats the least-significant 32 bits of rs1 as a zero-extended 32-bit value before performing the rotation.
RULE 20: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 computes the 32-bit rotation result as (rs1 >> shamt) | (rs1 << (32 - shamt)), where both shifts are logical and the width is exactly 32 bits.
RULE 21: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 sign-extends the 32-bit rotation result by copying bit 31 of the result into all more-significant bits of the destination register.
RULE 22: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 writes the sign-extended 32-bit result into register rd, replacing the previous contents of rd.
RULE 23: RORW rd, rs1, rs2 with a shift amount of zero leaves the least-significant word of rs1 unchanged before sign-extension.
RULE 24: RORW rotates right a 32-bit word taken from a source register and writes the rotated result to the destination register.
RULE 25: RORW is only implemented on RV64 implementations and is not available on RV32 implementations.
RULE 26: On an RV32 implementation, the encoding for RORW is reserved and its architectural behavior is undefined.

INSTRUCTION: SB
FORMAT: sb rs2, imm12(rs1)
RULE 1: SB computes its effective memory address by adding the contents of register rs1 to the sign-extended 12-bit immediate offset.
RULE 2: SB stores the low-order 8 bits of register rs2 into the memory byte at the effective address.
RULE 3: SB shall never raise an address-misaligned exception because an 8-bit store is naturally aligned on any address.
RULE 4: SB is guaranteed to execute atomically.
RULE 5: In a system where endianness is byte-address invariant, a byte stored by SB at a particular address is returned unchanged by any byte-sized load from the same address regardless of the endianness in effect.
RULE 6: SB must store the low-order 8-bit value from register rs2 into the memory byte at the effective address.
RULE 7: SB computes its effective address within the 64-bit RV64I address space, and the execution environment defines which portions of that address space are legal to access.
RULE 8: SB stores two-width bytes of memory from the address computed using rs1 and the immediate, and the store is performed atomically.
RULE 9: The SB (c.sb) instruction must be present in implementations that support the RV32 base ISA.
RULE 10: The SB (c.sb) instruction must be present in implementations that support the RV64 base ISA.
RULE 11: The SB (c.sb) instruction stores the least-significant byte of the source register rs2′ to the memory location whose address is the sum of the value in base register rs1′ and the zero-extended unsigned immediate uimm.
RULE 12: The SB (c.sb) instruction does not modify any architectural integer register.
RULE 13: SB instruction must form a 2-bit immediate uimm where uimm[1] is bit 5 of the instruction encoding, uimm[0] is bit 6 of the instruction encoding, and all higher bits uimm[31:2] are zero.
RULE 14: SB instruction must zero-extend the 2-bit immediate uimm to an XLEN-wide value before using it in address calculation.
RULE 15: SB instruction must compute the effective address by adding the zero-extended immediate to the value in the base register rs1', where rs1' is required to be one of the registers x8-x15.
RULE 16: SB instruction must store the least significant byte of the source register rs2', where rs2' is required to be one of the registers x8-x15, into the memory byte addressed by the effective address.
RULE 17: SB instruction must replace bits [7:0] of the addressed memory location with the stored byte while leaving all other bits of that byte-aligned location unchanged.
RULE 18: SB instruction has no architectural prerequisites and may be executed in any privilege mode.
RULE 19: When a standard store instruction that is not compressed and is SB, the transformed instruction format uses the same rs2, funct3, and opcode fields as the trapping SB instruction.
RULE 20: In a transformed SB instruction, the Addr. Offset field that occupies bits 19:15 contains the positive difference between the faulting virtual address (written to mtval or stval) and the original virtual address, and this difference is non-zero only for a misaligned memory access.
RULE 21: In a transformed SB instruction, all immediate offset fields (bits 31:25, 24:20, and 11:7) are replaced with zero.
RULE 22: In a transformed SB instruction, bits 1:0 are binary 11 because SB is non-compressed.
RULE 23: When an SB instruction causes a Store/AMO address misaligned exception, the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) shall be written with the transformed SB encoding rather than zero.
RULE 24: When an SB instruction causes a Store/AMO access fault exception, the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) shall be written with the transformed SB encoding rather than zero.
RULE 25: When an SB instruction causes a Store/AMO page fault exception, the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) shall be written with the transformed SB encoding rather than zero.
RULE 26: When an SB instruction causes a Store/AMO guest-page fault exception, the trap-instruction CSR (mtinst or htinst) shall be written with the transformed SB encoding rather than zero.
RULE 27: Because SB has a defined transformed encoding, a synchronous trap caused by SB for any of the listed exception causes shall result in the trap-instruction register containing the transformed SB rather than zero or a pseudoinstruction.
RULE 28: SB MUST be subject to pointer masking because pointer masking applies to all explicit memory accesses.
RULE 29: Pointer masking MUST be applied to an SB access only when the access is generated by an instruction executing on the CPU, including CPU extensions such as an FPU.
RULE 30: When MPRV is in effect, pointer masking for SB MUST use the pointer-masking configuration of the effective privilege mode.
RULE 31: When SPVP is in effect, pointer masking for SB MUST use the pointer-masking configuration of the effective privilege mode.
RULE 32: If MXR is in effect at the effective privilege mode where an SB access occurs, pointer masking MUST NOT be applied to that SB access.
RULE 33: Misaligned SB accesses MUST be supported.
RULE 34: A misaligned SB access MUST behave as if the pointer-masking transformation were applied to the aligned byte address that constitutes the access, yielding the same stored byte as would result from applying pointer masking to each constituent aligned byte.
RULE 35: Misaligned SB accesses MUST be subject to the same limitations that apply to misaligned accesses when pointer masking is not present.

INSTRUCTION: SC.D
FORMAT: sc.d rd, rs2, (rs1)
RULE 1: SC.D is defined only for RV64 implementations and operates on doubleword-sized operands analogously to SC.W.
RULE 2: The address held in rs1 for an SC.D must be eight-byte naturally aligned, and if it is not naturally aligned the implementation must raise either an address-misaligned exception or an access-fault exception.
RULE 3: An SC.D may succeed only when the address in rs1 is within the reservation set of the most recent LR instruction in program order.
RULE 4: An SC.D must fail when the address in rs1 is not within the reservation set of the most recent LR instruction in program order.
